
\chapter{Introduction}\label{chap:intro}

New avenues in the drug discovery pipeline have opened up thanks to advances in large dataset collection, analysis, and computation. Therapeutic targets are identified, potential drug candidates are screened and optimized, and the most promising of these go on to pre-clinical and clinical trials \citep{Hughes2011}. However, attrition is high, and roughly 90\% of drugs that enter clinical trials are never approved, often due to poor efficacy or toxicity \citep{Sun2022}. Computational methods can remove poor candidates early on in the drug discovery pipeline, long before clinical trials. This is done through virtual screening, which allows for a large-scale analysis of libraries of compounds \citep{Neves2018}. One standard screening method is the prediction of bioactivities and pharmacological properties of such candidates. There is a long history of using Quantitative Structure-Activity Relationship (QSAR) models to make these predictions, to act as a filter for compounds with undesirable properties, and thus reduce the time and cost associated with experimental screening \citep{Sabando2021}. QSAR models aim to predict the relationship between a molecule's structural and chemical features as encoded by a particular molecular representation and a desired experimental molecular property \citep{Sabando2021} of interest. A QSAR model requires a molecular representation and some form of machine learning (ML) model. An ideal molecular representation will allow a model to learn only the relevant information about each molecule with respect to the target property. Representations are either `static' hand-crafted descriptors or circular fingerprints that encode structural and physicochemical features \citep{rogers2010, David2020, sns, Gedeck2006}; or learned representations, where the encoding is derived from a predictive model, often taken from the activations of the final layer of a neural network, or a latent space embedding \citep{Sabando2021, kishimoto2023}. 

% TODO: check (TH lens): many numbers, no take-home. Is landrum2024's 'mean disagreement' the same statistic as the standard deviation? Table 2.1 gives 0.44 for Kramer2012, here 0.54.
QSAR models are of course, sensitive to the quality of the input data on which they are trained. Experimental noise can arise from measurement errors, variability under experimental conditions, varying provenance of the data, intrinsic biological, chemical variability, and human error. Even the very measures of model performance may be flawed if test and validation sets are laden with noise. \citet{Kolmar2021} showed that error in the test labels can make a model look worse than it is: a model trained on noisy data can predict the noise-free values more accurately than an evaluation on the noisy test set indicates. There is also a limit to how much noise the model can tolerate; \citet{jorner2021} argued that once their model's error approached the experimental error, more training data would not be expected to improve accuracy much. In the context of activity prediction, the inherent variability within experimental setups and assays, batch effects, measurement errors, and biological variability introduce additional layers of complexity \citep{Kolmar2021, Kramer2012, Kalliokoski2013}. All of this increases the likelihood of encountering noise in the data. Even the molecular representations in the training data themselves may be incorrect or misleading \citep{Fourches2010, Walters2023}, and chemical datasets contain too few repeat measurements of each compound to characterize the distribution the label is drawn from. Thus, models treat a single label as the population mean \citep{Kolmar2021}. Here pIC$_{50}$ and p$K_i$ are the negative base-10 logarithms of the IC$_{50}$ and $K_i$ in molar units, so a difference of one log unit is a tenfold difference in concentration. Repeat measurements of the same compound-target pair have a standard deviation of about $0.68$ log units for pIC$_{50}$ and $0.54$ log units for p$K_i$ \citep{Kalliokoski2013, Kramer2012}, which is about a 4.8-fold and a 3.5-fold difference in concentration. Restricting to measurements from a single assay lowers the mean disagreement from $0.50$ to $0.27$ log units \citep{landrum2024}, or from about a 3.2-fold to a 1.9-fold difference.

% [TH edit 2026-10-10: 'implicitly' added]
This thesis addresses the challenge of conducting molecular property prediction when the underlying experimental data, or ``labels'' are noisy. Most work on noise in QSAR modeling implicitly assumes Gaussian label noise and evaluates either representations or model architectures in isolation. \citet{Cortes2015} examined 12 algorithms across multiple noise levels but used fixed representations, and studies that compare representations typically use clean data. No work has systematically compared the relative importance of representation versus architecture choice for label noise robustness, asked whether probabilistic methods justify their computational overhead under noise, or tested whether findings generalize across different types of injected noise. This thesis fills that gap. We will also discuss noise mitigation, using the results of our noise robustness study. We explore why many noise mitigation techniques underperform in cheminformatics data and methods to address this problem in the form of novel molecular representations.

The first half of this thesis therefore asks four questions.\label{sec:intro:questions} First, how does the choice of molecular representation and model architecture explain the variance in predictive performance, and does that division change when you instead consider how much performance a QSAR model is able to retain under increasing label noise? Second, what does the choice of model provide under label noise? Within this question we compare the effects of deterministic and probabilistic modeling. Third, does the type of label noise matter, or just the quantity of it? We explore different techniques of generating and adding label noise to both clean data, as well as data with inherent experimental noise. Fourth, what is the impact of artificial label noise on a model's uncertainty estimation? Does the estimated uncertainty rise with increased label noise and can these uncertainty values point to which labels are corrupted? Together, these questions aim to identify what, if anything, makes a QSAR model robust to noise. To answer them we introduce NoiseInject, an open-source Python package for benchmarking noise robustness and uncertainty quantification, described in Section~\ref{sec:noiseinject_pkg}.

% TODO: check (TH lens): say in one sentence why distance matters for noisy labels: these methods judge a label against its nearest neighbors' labels, so they are only as good as the distance that picks them.
We ran a sweep of published methods for noisy labels across clean labels with injected Gaussian noise. Some of the worst-performing methods within this sweep relied on molecular distance measures. We posited that this distance measure was at least partially to blame for the poor performance. On a given dataset, most features of a general-purpose representation either barely vary or do not relate to the property. Yet each one that varies adds a term to the distance, so with enough irrelevant features, the distance becomes irrelevant as well, as described in Chapter~\ref{chap:flaws}. 

We propose an alternative: adaptive feature selection, where a fixed number of features are selected from several input feature sets, or molecular representations in the case of small molecules. We refer to the resulting fixed feature set as the hybrid representation. These hybrid representations work as ordinary inputs to prediction models as well. Chapter~\ref{chap:method} explains how this search takes place and Chapter~\ref{chap:similarity} tests whether the hybrids do in fact improve distance measures. We return back to noisy labels in Chapter~\ref{chap:noise}, where for a given dataset, we compare the representation with the highest predictive performance against the top-performing hybrid across a wider range of methods designed for noisy labels. We extend these methodologies to antibody and chemical reaction prediction tasks. 

% [alignment 2026-10-10: new paragraph: the second-half questions that Ch5 and Ch8 answer, worded as Ch8 answers them]
The second half of this thesis asks four further questions. First, can a search find the best set of features for a given dataset and model, and does it carry over from small molecules to antibodies and reactions? Second, does distance in a hybrid follow the label more closely than distance in a single representation, and do methods built on that distance gain? Third, can noisy labels be found and corrected, and does a hybrid help more than the best single representation? Fourth, does feature importance help, both in building the hybrid and when reused during training?

\section{Outline of the thesis and contributions}

Chapter~\ref{chap:background} introduces molecular representations, model families, molecular similarity and activity cliffs, and the sources of noise in molecular data. It defines the seven noise conditions used in Chapter~\ref{chap:robustness} and the three groups of methods for noisy labels (noise detection, noise mitigation and noise-robust training), and closes with artificial noise injection, and evaluation metrics.

% TODO: ALIGN: the Ch3 uncertainty takeaway is missing here: outside censoring, per-sample uncertainty did not identify corrupted labels; under censoring, error divided by uncertainty picked out the clipped labels. Ch1's fourth question asks exactly this.
Chapter~\ref{chap:robustness} looks into what makes a QSAR model robust to label noise. The primary prediction target is the HOMO--LUMO gap in QM9, whose computed labels carry negligible measurement noise. The same experiments were also run on three assay datasets: LogD, Caco-2 and hERG $K_i$. We compare seven different types of label noise, referred to as noise conditions. We matched the quantity of noise to the training set label spread for all but one of the noise conditions. We measure noise robustness as the proportion of predictive performance retained as artificial label noise increases. We found that model family was the main factor in noise robustness, and choice of molecular representation matters more for predictive performance than noise robustness. Strong predictive performance on clean labels does not imply robustness. LightGBM and XGBoost predicted well on clean labels but lost the most with increasing label noise, while random forests were the most consistent model across datasets, representations, and noise conditions. We replicated systematic noise by giving every scaffold family its own offset and by clipping labels at a made-up assay limit. Models struggled the most when training on this systematic noise. Label noise raised mean predicted uncertainty for the probabilistic models which estimate it; however, only the BNNs with a variance head, the heteroscedastic GP and the heteroscedastic VBLL networks correctly attributed the rise in uncertainty to the data rather than to the model itself, though not on every dataset and representation. To run these experiments, we built NoiseInject, an open-source Python package for benchmarking noise robustness and uncertainty in QSAR models.

% TODO: check (TH lens): 'another topic' suggests similarity is unrelated to noise, but many methods for noisy labels rely on it. Say the link directly.
Chapter~\ref{chap:flaws} takes a turn to look at another topic: molecular similarity. The natural next step to Chapter~\ref{chap:robustness} is noise mitigation. However, a small sweep of twelve published noise-handling methods, four that remove molecules and retrain and eight that change the training, showed disappointing results. None of the methods that incorporated the concept of molecular similarity showed consistently promising results. On a given dataset, standard representations carry many features that do not vary, and some map different molecules to the same vector. Similarity computed over these representations inherits both of these problems, and methods that rely on similarity suffer as a result. The chapter ends with what a representation would need to mitigate these problems, which Chapter~\ref{chap:method} builds.

% TODO: check (TH lens): 'budget' and 'settings' are not defined yet. Say the budget is the number of features kept, and give an example of the settings.
Chapter~\ref{chap:method} presents the adaptive hybrid representation. The adaptive hybrid search customizes the settings of a feature selection process to a given dataset, model, and budget. It takes in several single representations and selects the most important features for that task by a fixed budget. This produces a tailor-made feature set, referred to as the hybrid representation. The actual output of the hybrid search is not the set of features, but rather the settings that define the feature selection method used to generate that hybrid representation. A newly published embedding can be added to the search as one more representation. For antibodies, we also built representations that read only the complementarity-determining regions (CDRs), each region, or the mutations from the parent antibody. Finally, we test the search on data held at separate sites.

% [alignment 2026-10-10: 'select downstream models' replaced (Ch5: both models); antibody ensemble result added as in Ch5 conclusions]
We compare the hybrid representations in a variety of scenarios, like their performance compared against principal components analysis, recursive feature elimination, and ensembles trained on different single representations. We pay attention to the selected features and test methods across a variety of datasets and representations. The hybrid demonstrated higher predictive performance across all small-molecule datasets when compared to the single representations, with both of each dataset's downstream models. On antibodies the change in performance depended on the data, however, the larger datasets tended to benefit the most. On most antibody datasets, an ensemble of one model per representation did better. The reactions gained nothing from the hybrids. Ranking features by importance helped to build the hybrid, but reusing the scores to weight features during training added little. 

% TODO: check (TH lens): candidate what? The hybrid is chosen and judged by the neighbors' predictions: say the judging is on held-out molecules. And 'more closely' than what, the single representations?
% TODO: ALIGN: gives only the small-molecule result. Ch6 and Ch8 add that on antibodies and Buchwald--Hartwig distance tracks labels more closely but the methods built on it do not always gain.
Chapter~\ref{chap:similarity} sits in contrast to Chapter~\ref{chap:flaws}, asking if the flaws identified with using insufficient representations in similarity and distance measures are improved by the use of hybrid representations. We build hybrids whose features are specifically optimized for distance, ranking each candidate by how well each molecule's five nearest neighbors predict its label. On every small-molecule dataset, distance in the hybrid tracked label differences more closely, and the nearest neighbors' predictions improved. 

% [alignment 2026-10-10: QM9 caveat from Ch7 and Ch8 conclusions added; simple-methods example matches Ch8]
Chapter~\ref{chap:noise} returns to label noise, expanding on the noise detection, noise mitigation, and noise-robust training explored in  Chapter~\ref{chap:flaws}. We adapt published methods to QSAR data, such as censored expectation maximization for labels clipped at an assay limit, a random intercept per scaffold family, and the buddy check from meteorology as a detector of noisy labels. We also introduce three methods we found no earlier use of. These are the cliff-aware gap, a detector that allows for activity cliffs; cleaning the labels before the hybrid search; and returning activity-cliff molecules after a cleanup. Every method is tested on single representations and on hybrids, against clean QM9 labels, random deletion and shuffled detector scores. These correction techniques work best when a known flag, such as an assay limit or a scaffold family, indicates which labels are wrong. For practical applications, this is possible if enough is known about the source of the data. On small molecules, hybrids stayed ahead of the best single representation in almost every condition, before and after treatment. QM9, whose labels are computed, shows that many gains on measured labels come from better training rather than from removing noise. Chapter~\ref{chap:discussion} answers the research questions. It argues that the noise in a dataset should be modeled before a method is chosen. No single method fits every dataset, and thus simple methods, such as random forests or raising every clipped label by one shared amount, are often as good as, if not better than, complex ones. The chapter ends with the limitations and future work.

\pagebreak
\chapter{QSAR modelling and label noise}\label{chap:background}\label{sec2}

\section{Molecular representations in QSAR}

% TODO: check (TH lens): this opening does not say why representations matter for a thesis on noise. One sentence: the representation is the model's input and decides which molecules are neighbors.
The success of machine learning hinges on the quality of input data. Although the quality can refer to noise, which the majority of our work is centered around, in this case we are talking about the type of input. Molecular representations provide different features with which QSAR models and noise detection techniques can be learned. However, a potential bottleneck on larger datasets should be noted; the more complex the data structure, the greater the computational cost. For smaller datasets, the number of parameters is limited by the size of the training set, to prevent overfitting.

% TODO: check (TH lens): one molecule has many valid SMILES; worth saying, since Chapter 4 uses canonical and randomized SMILES.
One of the most common and intuitive molecular representations is the Simplified Molecular-Input Line-Entry System (SMILES) string, which consists of a sequence of ASCII characters generated by a fixed set of rules to specify a given chemical identity \citep{Weininger1988}. A common way to use SMILES strings as inputs to QSAR models is to tokenize and one-hot-encode them rather than to use the strings themselves \citep{TeachOpenCADD2023}. The strings are tokenized into atomic branches and symbols and mapped to indices of a vocabulary, and finally converted into binary vectors using one-hot encodings \citep{TeachOpenCADD2023}. This representation, referred to as one-hot-encoded SMILES, allows models to process molecular structures as bit-vector-based fingerprints or as sequences of vectors. Sequence-based models such as recurrent neural networks and transformers have proven to be particularly successful in capturing text semantics within a sequence. When applied to raw or one-hot-encoded SMILES strings, these models can effectively capture the structure of the molecule in its textual representation \citep{TeachOpenCADD2023}. 

% TODO: check (TH lens): more precisely, each MACCS bit records whether one predefined substructure pattern (SMARTS) is present.
Another common representation is the molecular fingerprint, a vector containing information about the properties of the molecule in question. In a typical binary fingerprint, each bit accounts for the presence or absence of a given chemical feature \citep{Fassio2022}, and in the case of Morgan fingerprints, a given substructure. One common fingerprint is the Molecular ACCess System (MACCS) key, a substructure key-based fingerprint that encodes predefined atom symbols and properties, as well as bond types \citep{Xie2020}. Another is the Extended Connectivity Fingerprint (ECFP), or Morgan fingerprint, which encodes local neighborhoods around each atom and bond connectivity in molecules \citep{Xie2020}. These existing molecular representations are well established and have been quite successful when coupled with QSAR models \citep{CortsCiriano2020, Sabando2021}. They are relatively simple, and as the bits can be mapped back to one or more substructures, they are human-interpretable, and furthermore easy-to-compute. However, hashed fingerprints are subject to information loss due to bit collisions, which happens when two different substructures are encoded at the same bit position \citep{Fassio2022}. They are also based on a fixed procedure and are not trainable or adaptable to a particular dataset. Alternative representations that capture different structural, chemical, or physical features are able to overcome these limitations. 

Recent advances have led to the development of new molecular fingerprints. One such method is the Sort \& Slice (SNS) technique, an alternative to hash-based folding for pooling ECFP substructures \citep{sns}. Sort \& Slice begins by sorting all circular substructures in the training set according to their frequency of occurrence. It then ``slices'' away all but the $N$ most frequent substructures, which are subsequently used to generate an $N$-dimensional, bit-collision-free binary fingerprint. This approach solves the ambiguity problem of hashed fingerprints, where multiple substructures might be encoded at the same bit position, making it very interpretable \citep{sns}. Sort \& Slice fingerprints outperform ECFP of diameter four (ECFP4) with standard hash-based folding methods across various molecular property prediction tasks. These experiments consisted of different data splitting techniques, machine learning models, fingerprint diameters, fingerprint dimensions, and atomic invariants \citep{sns}. Furthermore, Sort \& Slice outperformed advanced substructure selection schemes such as filtered fingerprints and mutual information maximization \citep{sns}. 

Neural fingerprints are a category of learned  representations that overcome the fixed, hard-coded nature of traditional fingerprints. Unlike ECFP or MACCS keys, which rely on predefined hashing schemes or substructure catalogs, neural fingerprints train on molecular data to capture chemical information \citep{Jaeger2018, Chithrananda2020}. These representations are the result of deep learning architectures that have learned dense, continuous embeddings which can be used for downstream tasks. 

Mol2vec, which adapts the Word2vec algorithm from NLP to molecular substructures \citep{Jaeger2018}, is a common neural fingerprint. Mol2vec treats molecules like ``sentences'' and circular substructures derived from Morgan fingerprints like ``words''. The model learns vector embeddings for each substructure through unsupervised training, such that chemically similar substructures are mapped to nearby points in the embedding space \citep{Jaeger2018}. The embeddings of all present substructures are summed to obtain a full representation. This approach produces 300-dimensional dense vectors that avoid the sparsity and bit collisions inherent in hashed fingerprints \citep{Jaeger2018}. Mol2vec has performed strongly on various QSAR benchmarks, matching or exceeding Morgan fingerprints on solubility and bioactivity prediction tasks \citep{Jaeger2018}.

Transformer-based architectures have also been applied to molecular representation learning, similarly stemming from NLP ideas. MolBERT applies a bidirectional transformer to learn molecular representations from SMILES strings through self-supervised pre-training \citep{Fabian2020}. The model has domain-relevant auxiliary adaptations, such as the prediction of calculated physicochemical properties alongside the masked token prediction, to improve the chemical fidelity of the learned embeddings \citep{Fabian2020}. MolBERT outperformed count-based ECFP4 (ECFC4), RDKit descriptors and the CDDD neural representation on virtual screening and QSAR benchmarks \citep{Fabian2020}. ChemBERTa is another transformer-based approach which pre-trains masked language models on SMILES data from PubChem \citep{Chithrananda2020}.  ChemBERTa uses a byte-pair-encoding tokenizer by default and compares it with a SMILES-specific tokenizer \citep{Chithrananda2020}. Attention visualization shows neurons selective for chemically relevant functional groups and aromatic rings, and others that track bracket closures in the SMILES string \citep{Chithrananda2020}. ChemBERTa-2, pre-trained on up to 77 million PubChem compounds, is competitive with existing architectures on MoleculeNet \citep{Ahmad2022}.

% TODO: check (TH lens): say whether the learned representations in this thesis are fine-tuned or used as fixed pre-trained embeddings. Fixed, task-agnostic embeddings are not expected to give a smooth property landscape.
Neural fingerprints provide several advantages over traditional fingerprints. They produce dense continuous vectors that avoid sparseness and information loss from bit collisions \citep{Jaeger2018}. They can also be pre-trained once on large unlabeled datasets and then fine-tuned for specific downstream tasks, opening the door to transfer learning \citep{Chithrananda2020, Fabian2020}. However, pre-training them is computationally expensive, and they are less directly interpretable than fingerprints, although attention visualization offers some insight \citep{Chithrananda2020}.

Beyond fingerprints, physicochemical-descriptor vectors (PDVs) are a class of continuous-value molecular descriptors that encode structural and chemical properties of molecules \citep{Todeschini2009}. Unlike binary fingerprints or graph-based representations, PDVs aggregate atom and bond-level quantities into fixed-length feature vectors that capture a wide variety of information about a molecule and its structure \citep{Mauri2006}. PDVs typically consist of hundreds of properties including constitutional descriptors such as molecular weight, atom and bond counts, and heteroatom ratios; topological indices encoding molecular connectivity patterns; physicochemical properties such as logP (lipophilicity), molar refractivity, and topological polar surface area; and electronic descriptors derived from partial charges and polarizabilities \citep{Todeschini2009, Cherkasov2014}. The \texttt{RDKit} cheminformatics toolkit provides a comprehensive suite of more than 200 molecular descriptors that cover these categories \citep{rdkit}. Many PDV descriptors, such as logP, molecular weight and partial charges, have a direct physicochemical meaning, although many of the thousands available have no clear interpretation \citep{Cherkasov2014}. They are also less computationally expensive than some 3D methods or neural fingerprints, with many descriptors simply requiring a 2D molecular structure without conformer generation or extensive pre-training. This efficiency makes PDVs particularly appropriate for high-throughput applications. Descriptors based on 3D structure can also be used \citep{Mauri2006}.

However, PDVs have limitations. The dimensionality of PDV feature spaces can lead to problems with multicollinearity \citep{Todeschini2009}. This can be mitigated by feature selection or dimensionality reduction techniques \citep{Guyon2003, Jolliffe2016}. Additionally, PDVs encode molecular properties as aggregated global features, which may not capture fine-grained structural details such as specific substructure arrangements or local chemical environments, information important for particular endpoints.

% TODO: check (TH lens): 2D graphs do record stereochemistry, through chirality tags and stereo bonds; the next sentence says so. They miss conformation, not stereochemistry.
One of the more intuitive representations is the molecular graph. These graphs can be categorized into 2D and 3D graphs. 2D graphs rely on adjacency matrices that encode topological atom- and bond-level features but leave out geometric properties such as bond angles or torsion, meaning they may fail to capture stereochemistry. 3D graphs incorporate these spatial features, but are often limited by remaining in a single conformation \citep{Zhichun2023}. A standard molecular graph is constructed such that each molecule is represented as $G = (V,E)$ with node features $\mathbf{x}_i$ and edge features $\mathbf{e}_{ij}$. In one common featurization, nodes encode atom type, chirality, hybridization and aromaticity, and edges encode bond type, ring membership, direction and stereochemistry \citep{Zhichun2023}. 

There are also alternative molecular graph representations such as graphs with quantum 3D embeddings, weight alignment graphs, association graphs containing pairs of molecules, and geometric interaction graph neural networks that incorporate 3D structures and physical interactions \citep{Hnig2022, yan2023, Yang2023GIGN}. \citet{Du2022} developed hybrid 2D and 3D graphs, combining 3D data, guaranteeing precision and completeness by providing unabridged information, and 2D data, providing chemical intuitions for guidance, in a zipper-like arrangement. Voxel-based 3D representations, in which each atom occupies one grid cell, are too sparse for convolutional networks to perform well; \citet{Kuzminykh2018} instead spread each atom over nearby voxels with a wave transform.
      
These graph representations do not highlight specific substructures or specific arrangements of atoms and bonds within each molecule. The substructures contain functional groups that are related to a range of chemical properties and are potentially involved in downstream tasks related to the function of molecules. \citet{Bouritsas2023} developed graph substructure networks, a topologically aware message passing scheme based on substructure encoding. The method involves encoding structural roles based on the appearance of specific substructures and using them in message passing updates. Since message-passing graph neural networks (GNNs) are at most as powerful as the Weisfeiler--Lehman test, they cannot isolate sufficient features from the graph structure \citep{Bouritsas2023}. Graph substructure networks, on the other hand, capture these substructure-related features. Encoders pretrained on unlabeled molecules, whether graph-based \citep{kishimoto2023} or transformers on SMILES \citep{ross2022}, can also produce embeddings that capture structural features. 

\section{Models for molecular property prediction}

There are a wide variety of model architectures that can be used for QSAR modeling. Starting off with simpler and more explainable models, we have tree-based methods. Decision trees are non-parametric supervised learning algorithms that perform recursive binary partitioning of the feature space \citep{Breiman1984}. A decision tree builds a predictive model by learning a series of if-then rules that recursively split the data based on given feature values, creating a tree  where internal nodes represent decision rules, branches represent the results of those decisions, and leaf nodes contain predicted values or class labels \citep{Breiman1984}. Each recursive split is chosen to maximize  homogeneity within the resulting partitions. For classification, Gini impurity is commonly used and for regression, mean squared error reduction \citep{Breiman1984}.

There are several properties that make decision trees a good foundation for QSAR modeling. First, they handle non-linear relationships between molecular descriptors and target properties without requiring explicit feature engineering or transformation \citep{Breiman1984}. They also do not make assumptions about the underlying distribution of the data, making them robust to violations of parametric assumptions that can limit linear regression or other classical methods \citep{Breiman1984}. 

% TODO: check (TH lens): in QSAR a 'consensus model' combines several models' predictions. Where do these two terms come from? 'Local' and 'global' may be clearer, or cite the source.
Decision trees handle data in a ``contributory'' rather than ``consensus'' manner. In consensus methods such as linear regression or neural networks, all training points fit one shared set of parameters, so a training point can affect predictions far from it in the feature space. Tree-based methods make predictions based on the subset of training samples that fall into the same leaf node as the test point \citep{Breiman1984}. This mechanism essentially quarantines outliers in their respective leaf nodes, limiting their impact on predictions for samples in other regions or chemical domains. 

Individual decision trees tend to have high variance and often overfit, particularly at large depths \citep{Breiman1984}. Small modifications made to the training data can result in large differences in the structure of the tree, resulting in unstable predictions \citep{Breiman1996}. Ensemble methods are used to counteract this instability, aggregating predictions from many trees. Random forests (RFs) aggregate predictions from trees trained on bootstrap samples of data with random subsets of features at each split \citep{Breiman2001}. Bootstrapping ensures that each tree is trained on a slightly different subset of data, which effectively reduces the influence of any individual sample \citep{Breiman2001}. This is especially beneficial with noisy samples. The selection of random features at every node decorrelates the trees, which lowers the generalization error of the ensemble \citep{Breiman2001}. The aggregation of trees further prevents overfitting to noise by smoothing out prediction errors from individual noisy labels. 

Neural networks (NNs), a very popular family of models, can also be used for QSAR. An NN is a system of interconnected nodes which learn by passing information through its layers. There are weights and biases attached to the nodes and various other parameters which the model optimizes to minimize the difference between the predicted output and the actual target value. During training, the network parameters are updated using backpropagation, in which gradients of the loss function with respect to each weight are propagated backward through the network \citep{Rumelhart1986}. The structure of these networks and the hyperparameters used depend on the type of NN. In QSAR, one of the most commonly used architectures is the multilayer perceptron (MLP), an NN containing fully-connected layers with non-linear activation functions which transforms molecular feature vectors (i.e., fingerprints, descriptors) into a property or activity prediction \citep{Ma2015}.

A typical MLP for molecular property prediction has a few fully connected hidden layers with rectified linear unit (ReLU) activations and dropout regularization to prevent overfitting. Training is performed using backpropagation with gradient descent optimizers such as Adam, to minimize a loss function (mean squared error for regression, cross-entropy for classification) on batches of training data. 

However, NN performance on QSAR-related tasks has been very mixed. NNs have a long history in QSAR \citep{Baskin2008}, and tuned deep networks have outperformed shallow methods such as random forests and support vector machines across diverse activity classes \citep{Koutsoukas2017}. Their complexity and use of gradients make them prone to label memorization \citep{Zhang2017}, which in the context of noise is problematic, as it means that models may memorize the noise themselves. Although models that are memorizing labels may initially appear to have strong predictive performance, they cannot generalize to out-of-distribution samples properly and often provide inaccurate uncertainty estimates. Various approaches have been proposed to improve neural network robustness to label noise, including noise-robust loss functions \citep{Song2022}, confident learning to identify mislabeled examples \citep{Northcutt2021}, and regularization techniques such as mixup \citep{Zhang2018mixup} or label smoothing \citep{Szegedy2015}. 

All the models up to this point have assumed a vector-based input. Molecular graphs, on the other hand, require different types of predictive modeling. Two standard ML methods on molecular graphs include Graph Convolutional Networks (GCNs) and Graph Isomorphism Networks (GINs). GCNs apply the concept of convolution to graph-structured data. Instead of operating on grid-based data, such as images, GCNs aggregate information from neighbors of a node to update its representation \citep{Kipf2017}. In contrast, GINs address the limitations in GCNs’ ability to differentiate between non-isomorphic graphs. Unlike GCNs, which normalize and weight the sum of neighboring node features, GINs use a simple sum followed by a trainable MLP, giving the model flexibility to learn complex transformations of node features. The GIN model’s design is informed by the Weisfeiler--Lehman test for graph isomorphism, enabling it to distinguish between molecular graphs that are structurally similar but differ in subtle ways \citep{TeachOpenCADD2023, Xu2019}. GIN features have been competitive with or better than fingerprints and descriptors at classifying pairs of molecules as activity cliffs (ACs), described in Section~\ref{sec:mol_similarity}. In ACs, small variations in molecular structure can lead to significant changes in chemical or biological activity \citep{Dablander2023}. Descriptor-based models still predicted the potency of cliff molecules better than deep learning methods \citep{vanTilborg2022}. 

\subsection{Tree ensembles}

Gradient boosting methods such as eXtreme Gradient Boosting (XGBoost) sequentially fit trees to the residuals of previous iterations, progressively reducing the prediction error \citep{Chen2016, Mustapha2016, Tian2022}. We used a handful of gradient-boosted tree ensembles in this thesis including Light Gradient-Boosting Machine (LightGBM) \citep{ke2017lightgbm}, and Natural Gradient Boosting (NGBoost) \citep{Duan2020}.

\subsection{Support vector machines}

Support vector regression fits a function that is as flat as possible while keeping training errors small \citep{Vapnik1995, Smola2004}. It uses the $\epsilon$-insensitive loss. Under this loss, errors smaller than $\epsilon$ cost nothing and larger errors cost their size minus $\epsilon$. A constant $C$ trades flatness against tolerance of large errors \citep{Cortes1995, Smola2004}. The features enter the model only through the kernel. With the radial basis function (RBF) kernel, every feature therefore enters the Euclidean distance between two molecules. In Chapter~\ref{chap:robustness} the continuous representations are standardized and the binary fingerprints are used as built. A wrong label has limited effect. An error inside the $\epsilon$ tube changes nothing, and outside it the loss grows only linearly. The weight of each molecule is also capped at $C$ \citep{Smola2004}. Under simulated experimental error, the RBF-kernel SVM degraded smoothly and linearly \citep{Cortes2015}. Its main limitation is computational cost. Training operates on a kernel matrix that grows with the square of the number of training molecules \citep{Chang2011}.

\subsection{Gaussian processes}

We employed Gaussian process (GP) models using the \texttt{Gauche} framework \citep{gauche}, which is optimized for cheminformatics tasks. Standard kernels for GPs are not optimized for the chemical landscape. \texttt{Gauche} defines such kernels for strings, fingerprints, and graphs that operate on a range of widely used molecular representations \citep{gauche}. We used the Tanimoto kernel \citep{Ralaivola2005, moss2020}, which is defined for binary vectors $\mathbf{x}, \mathbf{x}' \in \{0, 1\}^d$ for $d \geq 1$ as:

\begin{equation}
k_{\text{Tanimoto}}(\mathbf{x}, \mathbf{x}') := \sigma_f^2 \cdot \frac{\langle \mathbf{x}, \mathbf{x}' \rangle}{\|\mathbf{x}\|^2 + \|\mathbf{x}'\|^2 - \langle \mathbf{x}, \mathbf{x}' \rangle},
\label{eq:tanimoto_kernel}
\end{equation}

where $\|\cdot\|$ represents the Euclidean norm \citep{gauche}. 

The RBF kernel is defined for
$\mathbf{x}, \mathbf{x}' \in \mathbb{R}^d$ as:

\begin{equation}
k_{\text{RBF}}(\mathbf{x}, \mathbf{x}') := \sigma_f^2 \cdot \exp\left(-\frac{\|\mathbf{x} - \mathbf{x}'\|^2}{2\ell^2}\right),
\end{equation}

where $\ell$ is the lengthscale and $\sigma_f^2$ the signal variance, which scales both kernels.

\subsection{Bayesian neural networks}

Standard feed-forward networks are deterministic; however, Figure~\ref{fig:bayesian_transformation} shows how they can be transformed into probabilistic models.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=0.85\textwidth,height=0.72\textheight,keepaspectratio]{bayesian_transformation.png}
    \caption[Nodes of a deterministic and a Bayesian neural network.]{One node of a deterministic and of a Bayesian neural network. The deterministic node (bottom left) applies a fixed weight $W$ and returns one prediction $\hat{y}$. The Bayesian node (bottom right) draws its weight from $\mathcal{N}(\mu, \sigma^2)$, so repeated predictions $\hat{y}_1, \dots, \hat{y}_N$ form a histogram.}
    \label{fig:bayesian_transformation}
\end{figure}

Unlike standard deterministic ML methods, Bayesian methods model distributions over predictions using Bayes' rule, allowing for the quantification of uncertainty within parameters and labels. Bayesian methods treat the model parameters as random variables with distributions. Uncertainty can also be estimated from other non-deterministic methods, including deep ensembles \citep{lakshminarayanan2017} and bootstrap modeling \citep{palmer2022}.

\section{Molecular similarity and activity cliffs}\label{sec:mol_similarity}

% [TH lens edit 2026-10-10: AI paragraph: says how neighbor-based noise detection uses similarity; split the long sentence; ends with take-home that representation and coefficient decide which labels are flagged. All citations kept.]
% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
Much of cheminformatics rests on the similar property principle: structurally similar molecules tend to have similar properties, including biological activity \citep{Johnson1990, vanTilborg2022}. Nearest-neighbor prediction and GP kernels rely on this principle. So do many noise detection methods, which flag a label that differs from the labels of the molecule's nearest neighbors. However, similarity is only defined relative to a representation and a coefficient, the formula that compares two feature vectors \citep{Maggiora2014}. Two molecules that are near-identical under a substructure fingerprint may be far apart under a PDV. A computed similarity value is therefore easily over-interpreted \citep{Maggiora2014}. The representation and the coefficient decide which molecules count as neighbors. They therefore also decide which labels such a noise detector flags.

% TODO: check (TH lens): Chapter 4 uses Manhattan, not Euclidean, for continuous representations; say so here. Also Tanimoto is a ratio of sums, not a sum.
The most common measure for binary fingerprints is the Tanimoto coefficient \citep{Bajusz2015}. For two molecules with fingerprints $\mathbf{x}, \mathbf{x}' \in \{0,1\}^d$,
\begin{equation}
T(\mathbf{x}, \mathbf{x}') = \frac{\sum_{j=1}^{d} x_j x'_j}{\sum_{j=1}^{d} x_j + \sum_{j=1}^{d} x'_j - \sum_{j=1}^{d} x_j x'_j},
\label{eq:tanimoto}
\end{equation}
which is the number of bits set in both molecules divided by the number of bits set in either. Continuous representations are instead compared with a distance, most often the Euclidean distance $\sqrt{\sum_{j=1}^{d}(x_j - x'_j)^2}$. Both measures are sums over the features, and neither weighs a feature by its relation to the property. A bit that is off in both molecules contributes nothing to the Tanimoto coefficient, whereas every bit set in either molecule counts; in the Euclidean distance, every feature that differs between the two molecules counts. Chapter~\ref{chap:flaws} returns to this point in Equation~\ref{eq:distance_split}.


% TODO: check (TH lens): similarity uses only the features, so corrupted labels do not change it. Few relevant features spoil the similarity; noisy labels spoil what the neighbors tell us. Separate the two.
Pairwise distance comparisons rely on the assumption that the underlying features contain relevant structural information. When the labels are corrupted or the important features are scarce, similarity scores end up dependent on compounded false or irrelevant information, and the assumption that similar molecules have similar properties breaks down. \citet{Bajusz2015} found that the Tanimoto, Dice and Cosine coefficients and the Soergel distance rank molecules almost identically on binary fingerprints. We take this to mean that changing the formula cannot compensate for a weak representation.

There are many downstream tasks which rely on the concept of similarity. The second half of this thesis relies on many such tasks, and as such focuses on identifying representations that agree with this principle. 

% TODO: check (TH lens): what is 'persistent' noise? Noise in most labels, or noise shared by neighbors from the same lab or series?
One of the challenges to handling noise is distinguishing it from activity cliffs, defined as a pair of structurally similar molecules whose chemical properties differ by a large amount \citep{Maggiora2006, Stumpfe2012}. While there is no universal definition of an activity cliff \citep{vanTilborg2022}, by definition they rely on a molecular similarity measure. The MoleculeACE benchmark \citep{vanTilborg2022} uses three measures: the Tanimoto coefficient on ECFPs, the Tanimoto coefficient on scaffold fingerprints and a scaled Levenshtein distance on SMILES strings. A pair forms a cliff if its similarity exceeds 90\% under at least one of them and the two molecules differ tenfold or more in $K_i$ or EC$_{50}$ \citep{vanTilborg2022}. Across the thirty MoleculeACE targets, molecules in at least one cliff pair make up between 7\% and 52\% of a dataset \citep{vanTilborg2022}. These activity cliffs, which potentially contain the most important information for model prediction, can be confused with noise. Another barrier to distance-based reasoning is persistent noise across labels. The task of trying to determine if a molecule's value matches that of its neighbors becomes a lot harder when all of the neighbors have some form of noise as well.

% TODO: check (TH lens): a one-sentence recap before the noise section would help: every method relying on similarity, noise detection included, is only as good as the representation.
As the number of dimensions grows, the distances to the nearest and to the furthest point become increasingly similar \citep{Beyer1999}. Molecular representations are routinely high-dimensional and sparse. When many of their features carry no information about the property, distances computed over them are dominated by those features.

\section{Noise in molecular data}

Real-world cheminformatics datasets contain label noise arising from multiple sources across the drug discovery pipeline. Although noise is often modeled as being random, or drawn from a Gaussian distribution, other varieties of noise must be considered when working with experimental data. To properly model experimental noise, we must first understand where the noise is coming from.

% TODO: check (TH lens): conformal prediction assumes exchangeability and calibrates on labels that are noisy too; it does not model the label distribution. Check what Kolmar2021 says, and cite a source for conformal.
One key issue with QSAR models is the standard ML assumption that labels represent true values, as most chemical datasets lack sufficient replications to provide a robust statistical representation of the underlying distributions \citep{Kolmar2021}. A single measurement, or even multiple measurements for a given label, will not guarantee that this label accurately reflects the population mean \citep{Kolmar2021}. Most ML models rely on the assumption that the training data is, in fact, an accurate reflection of the population. Therefore, they treat these potentially noisy labels as point values rather than distributions, which opens the door to overfitting to noise. However, Bayesian methods such as Gaussian processes (GPs) do not make this assumption, and neither does conformal prediction \citep{Kolmar2021}.

Studies involving noise, and specifically the quantification of noise robustness, typically approach label noise by adding independent, zero-mean, Gaussian error to the labels. However, there's evidence to suggest that noise from experimental assays takes on different forms. \citet{Kruger2012} rejected normality of the differences in binding affinity between orthologous targets by Anderson--Darling test, found them approximately Laplace, and found that differences between assays of the same ligand and target closely resemble them. \citet{Kalliokoski2013} examined the widest disagreements among 16,844 pairs of repeat pIC$_{50}$ measurements and found that in most cases, the wide gap between repeat values was due to annotation error rather than to an imprecise assay. 

Although numerous studies have explored the effects of noise on ML algorithms for molecular property prediction, comparisons between different molecular representations remain limited. \citet{Kolmar2021} examined the predictive limits of QSAR in the presence of noise, showing that by learning the underlying trends rather than fitting to noise, the models can achieve predictions more accurate than those of their training data. They assumed experimental noise could be modeled as Gaussian-distributed, using the Central Limit Theorem to argue that aggregated experimental measurements from multiple independent error sources exhibit a Gaussian distribution. However, \citet{Heid2023} distinguish random noise, which affects all molecules alike, from systematic noise that differs between regions of chemical space. Using synthetic noise applied only to nitrogen-containing molecules, they showed that mean-variance estimation can detect it.

Beyond just the distribution, the assumptions of independence and zero mean may fail as well \citep{Lambert2023autocorrelated}. \citet{Bentz2013} examined P-glycoprotein transport measurements from 23 laboratories and found that, among the laboratories using cell lines, 62\% of the variance in log digoxin efflux ratio was due to differences between laboratories. When you pool data from different sources, each source has the potential to carry a shared offset. These sources usually contain families of related compounds, breaking the independence assumption. The zero-mean assumption is not assured either, especially when true values fall above or below an assay's detection limit, called censoring. \citet{Svensson2025} count censored labels in 15 industrial assays, of which 13 carry censored labels and eight sit between a quarter and two thirds censored. Chapter~\ref{chap:robustness} tests noise conditions that vary the distribution, the correlation, and the bias of injected error separately, at a matched quantity of noise for every condition except censoring.

% TODO: check (TH lens): this is one kind of federated learning, knowledge distillation, as Chapter 5 calls it. Better to name it in the row.
Label noise enters bioactivity data at every step from the assay plate to the database. Table~\ref{tab:noise_sources} lists these sources, with examples of each and the kind of error each tends to produce. The kind of error describes how the error is distributed across molecules, which is the property that the noise conditions in Section~\ref{sec:noise_injection} vary.
\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering
\small
\begin{adjustbox}{max width=\textwidth,max totalheight=0.78\textheight}
\begin{tabular}{@{}P{0.16\linewidth}P{0.46\linewidth}P{0.30\linewidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Source} & \textbf{Example} & \textbf{Kind of Error} \\
\midrule
Assay & Edge, row and column effects from evaporation or temperature gradients, and errors in liquid handling \citep{Makarenkov2007}; carryover between wells \citep{Inglese2006} & Error that varies with plate position \\
Instrument & Higher relative error near a detection limit \citep{Zhang1999}; autofluorescence of the test compound \citep{Simeonov2008} & Larger error for weak binders; a systematic bias; labels clipped at the limit \\
Biology & Seeding density, growth rate, cell cycle and serum properties \citep{Hatzis2014}; cell passage number \citep{Postnikova2018} & Variation between repeats of the same experiment \\
Aggregation across sources & ChEMBL, BindingDB and PubChem pool hundreds of laboratories \citep{Gaulton2017, Gilson2016}; independent $K_i$ measurements of one compound--target pair differing by 0.44 log units on average \citep{Kramer2012} & An offset shared by each laboratory or source, hard to separate from the true value without metadata \citep{Fourches2016} \\
Federated learning & Teacher models label a shared public set, and a student model learns from those labels \citep{Bassani2023} & The teachers' prediction errors on top of the measurement error \\
Annotation & Mis-registered structures, wrong stereochemistry and salt forms \citep{Fourches2010}; 0.1--3.4\% of structures incorrect, depending on the database \citep{Young2008}; ambiguous protonation states and counterions \citep{Bento2020} & A label attached to the wrong molecule; distinct molecules merged \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{adjustbox}
\caption[Sources of label noise in bioactivity data.]{Sources of label noise in bioactivity data, with reported examples and the kind of error each source produces.}
\label{tab:noise_sources}
\end{table}

Although the general assumption in much of the noise analysis and mitigation literature is that noise, specifically label noise, will have a negative effect on the model, this is not necessarily the case. There are multiple instances of using noise as a method of regularization. In linear regression, appending observations of pure noise shrinks the coefficient estimates, an implicit regularization, although not an optimal one \citep{Ullah2024}. Furthermore, adding artificial noise drawn from a Gaussian distribution to the activation layers of a neural network is considered another form of regularization \citep{Camuto2021}. These studies indicate that the presence of noise may act as a form of regularization in some contexts. Neither corrupts the labels of real observations, so whether label noise regularizes QSAR and quantitative structure--property relationship (QSPR) models remains open.

\subsection{Artificial noise injection}\label{sec:noise_injection}

Our objective is to evaluate the robustness of QSAR models against label noise. To do so, we ``inject'' artificial noise into the labels during training while test data remain free of noise. Validation labels are given noise of the same type and quantity as the training labels, drawn independently. This artificial noise simulates real-world experimental noise. The clean target label $y_i$ is replaced by a noisy label $\tilde{y}_i = y_i + \epsilon_i$, where $\epsilon_i$ is the artificial noise injected at the index $i$, the value of which is determined by a specified noise strategy. As we increased the quantity of noise, we measured how much predictive performance fell. 

We explored seven different ways of adding noise, which we refer to as noise conditions and which are illustrated in Figure~\ref{fig:noise_conditions} and summarized in Table~\ref{tab:noise_conditions}. Three conditions vary the shape of the distribution from which the error is drawn, and three concentrate the error on some molecules or share it within Bemis--Murcko scaffold families. The seventh condition, censoring, clips labels at a limit.

We define a scaffold family as the set of molecules that share a Bemis--Murcko scaffold, the ring systems and linking chains left once all other side chains are removed \citep{Bemis1996}. 

% TODO: check (TH lens): where does 0.14 come from? It is 1/sqrt(50); say so.
\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth,height=0.72\textheight,keepaspectratio]{F1_noise_conditions.png}
\caption[Label distributions under the seven noise conditions.]{Label distributions under the seven noise conditions, for 2,000 synthetic labels in 40 random groups that stand in for scaffold families. a)--g) Clean (gray) and noised (color) labels at a noise level of 0.5 times the standard deviation of the clean labels (25\% of labels clipped under censoring). h) Standard deviation of the mean error within a group divided by that of all errors, for every condition except censoring; a value near 1 means a group's labels move together, and noise that ignores the groups gives about 0.14 with about 50 labels per group.}
\label{fig:noise_conditions}
\end{figure}

% TODO: check (TH lens): here tau is in label units, but Chapter 3 lists tau as fractions (0 to 1.5). Say here that tau is the noise level times the standard deviation of the clean training labels.
For a given label, every condition except censoring delivers the same expected quantity of noise at a set noise level. These levels are defined as a fraction of the standard deviation of clean training labels. The noise added to the label of molecule $i$ is
\begin{equation}
\epsilon_i = \frac{\tau}{G}\,s_i z_i,
\qquad
G = \sqrt{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n} s_j^{2}}\;\;\sigma_z,
\label{eq:dose_matching}
\end{equation}
where $\tau$ is the target standard deviation of the noise, in the units of the label, and $z_i$ is
drawn from the condition's distribution at unit scale. The multiplier $s_i$ is $1$ for most
molecules and $\lambda = 3$ for those the condition affects, which are a random 10\% of molecules
under outlier and whole scaffold families covering 20\% of the split under grouped-wider. The
constant $\sigma_z$ is the standard deviation of $z$, equal to $1$ for the Gaussian,
$\sqrt{\nu/(\nu-2)}$ for the Student-$t$ and $\sqrt{2}$ for the Laplace. The factor $G$ divides both
of these out, so that $\epsilon$ has standard deviation $\tau$ in expectation whatever the
distribution and whichever molecules are affected.

Grouped-shifted widens no molecule's error, and instead gives every scaffold family $g$ an offset of
its own,
\begin{equation}
\epsilon_i = \frac{\tau}{G}\left(\sqrt{\rho}\,b_{g(i)} + \sqrt{1-\rho}\,w_i\right),
\label{eq:grouped_shifted}
\end{equation}
with one draw $b$ per family and one draw $w$ per molecule. The two variances sum to $\tau^{2}$, and
the family offset carries a share $\rho$ of that total.

% TODO: check (TH lens): why 5 degrees of freedom? If there was a reason, give it in a few words; a reviewer will ask.
% [TH lens edit 2026-10-10: AI table row: states what Kruger2012 measured (related targets; assays resemble them), matching the Laplace row and the Noise in molecular data paragraph.]
% TODO: check (TH lens): the 20% share has no source. Were other shares tried? If not, say the value was not varied; a reviewer may challenge it.
We selected the parameter values from published measurement errors wherever possible, and Table~\ref{tab:noise_conditions} notes where no published estimate exists. We found no published estimate of how large a share of a dataset is affected by a difference between laboratories, so under grouped-wider we chose to draw scaffold families until they covered $20\%$ of the training molecules.
\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering
\small
\begin{adjustbox}{max width=\textwidth,max totalheight=0.78\textheight}
\begin{tabular}{@{}P{0.15\linewidth}P{0.30\linewidth}P{0.17\linewidth}P{0.30\linewidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Condition} & \textbf{What It Changes} & \textbf{Value} & \textbf{Source for the Value} \\
\midrule
Gaussian & Shape: every label's error is Gaussian & -- & The standard assumption in noise-robustness studies \\
% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
Student-$t$ & Shape: heavier tails than Gaussian & $\nu = 5$ & Differences in binding affinity between related targets are non-normal, and differences between assays of the same ligand and target resemble them \citep{Kruger2012}; no published estimate of $\nu$, so we chose $\nu = 5$ \\
Laplace & Shape: sharper peak and heavier tails than Gaussian & -- & Approximately Laplace for differences in p$K_i$ between related targets, which resemble differences between repeated measurements \citep{Kruger2012} \\
Outlier & Error $\lambda$ times wider on a random share of molecules (Huber's contamination model \citep{Huber1964}) & $10\%$ of molecules; $\lambda = 3$ & $1$--$10\%$ of routine data contain gross errors, upper bound taken \citep{Hampel2001}; for one solubility method, inter-laboratory error is about three times intra-laboratory error \citep{Avdeef2019} \\
Grouped-wider & Error $\lambda$ times wider on whole scaffold families & Families covering $20\%$ of the training molecules; $\lambda = 3$ & No published estimate of the share; $\lambda$ as for outlier \citep{Avdeef2019} \\
Grouped-shifted & Every scaffold family gets its own offset; no error is widened & $\rho = 0.62$ & Laboratory identity explains $62\%$ of the variance in log digoxin efflux ratio across the laboratories using cell lines \citep{Bentz2013} \\
Censoring & Labels past a limit are set to the limit; the level is the fraction clipped & Set by the noise level & Censored labels in industrial assays \citep{Svensson2025} \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{adjustbox}
\caption[The seven noise conditions and their parameters.]{The seven noise conditions and their parameter values. $\nu$ is the number of degrees of freedom of the Student-$t$ distribution, $\lambda$ the widening factor (Equation~\ref{eq:dose_matching}) and $\rho$ the share of the noise variance carried by the scaffold-family offset (Equation~\ref{eq:grouped_shifted}). For censoring, the noise level is the fraction of labels clipped.}
\label{tab:noise_conditions}
\end{table}

\subsection{Learning with noisy labels}\label{sec:lnl_background}

In this thesis, methods for learning with noisy labels (LNL) fall into three groups, listed in Table~\ref{tab:noise_terms}: noise detection, noise mitigation and noise-robust training. The groups are defined by the general way in which noisy labels are detected and then by what is done with them. \citet{Song2022} sort deep learning methods for noisy labels differently, into five groups by the part of training they change: robust architectures, robust regularization, robust loss functions, loss adjustment and sample selection. Figure~\ref{fig:data_cleaning} shows how a noise mitigation method is evaluated.

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering
\caption[Terms for methods that handle noisy labels.]{Terms used throughout
this thesis for methods that handle noisy labels. A noise mitigation method is
named by both halves: what selects the molecules, and what is then done to
them. The group follows from the two.}
\label{tab:noise_terms}
\begin{tabular}{lp{0.60\textwidth}}
\toprule
\multicolumn{2}{l}{\textit{What selects the molecules}} \\
\midrule
Detector & A score inferred from the features and the labels, saying how wrong
each label looks. The score is that molecule's suspicion score. \\
Known flag & Already recorded in the data, with nothing inferred: a label at
the assay limit, how many times a molecule was measured, which source reported
it, which scaffold family it belongs to. \\
Every molecule & No selection. The method acts on all the labels. \\
\midrule
\multicolumn{2}{l}{\textit{What is done to them}} \\
\midrule
Delete & The molecule leaves the training set. \\
Down-weight & The molecules and labels are kept and each molecule carries a weight. \\
Move the label & The label shifts toward a target by some distance. The target
is a neighbors' mean, a predicted value, a group mean or an assay limit. \\
\midrule
\multicolumn{2}{l}{\textit{The groups}} \\
\midrule
Noise detection & A detector alone, with nothing done afterwards. \\
Noise mitigation & A detector, a known flag or every molecule, and then one of
the three actions. \\
Noise-robust training & Nothing is selected and the data is untouched. The
loss, the kernel, a noise term, the training loop or the model changes. \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}


\begin{figure}[!htbp]
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=0.95\textwidth,height=0.72\textheight,keepaspectratio]{figures_code_input/data_cleaning.png}
    \caption[Evaluation of a noise mitigation method.]{Evaluation of a noise mitigation method, with QM9 as the example. A random forest trained on noisy training labels is compared with one trained on labels corrected by a Bayesian model; the test labels stay clean, and both forests are scored on them by $R^2$ (higher is better).}
    \label{fig:data_cleaning}
\end{figure}

No single method is expected to win. Averaged over all problems, no learning algorithm outperforms another \citep{Wolpert1996}. Neighbor-based cleaning relies on the similar property principle. It dates back to editing a training set by its nearest neighbors \citep{Wilson1972}. However, activity cliffs break that principle, and QSAR models frequently fail to predict cliffs \citep{Dablander2023}. Chapter~\ref{chap:flaws} tests the principle, and Chapter~\ref{chap:noise} tests neighbor-based noise mitigation with the adaptive hybrid representation as the feature space.

\section{Implementation of the noise conditions}

% TODO: check (TH lens): Rust and Python generators do not give the same draws from one seed. Same draws or same statistics? Say what was compared and where the record is.
The noise conditions used in this thesis are primarily implemented by NoiseInject, an open-source Python package that helps the user inject  noise onto labels. Experiments done with QM9 in Chapter~\ref{chap:robustness} use a separate Python and Rust pipeline with its own Rust implementation of the same conditions. The two implementations have been confirmed to deliver the same noise using uniform seeds. 

\subsection{The NoiseInject package}\label{sec:noiseinject_pkg}

NoiseInject (version 0.3.0) evaluates model robustness and uncertainty calibration under artificial
label noise, for both regression and classification. It operates on arrays of labels and on the
predictions of any externally trained model, which makes it model-agnostic, and its core depends only
on NumPy, pandas, SciPy and scikit-learn. Results are returned as \texttt{pandas} data frames with one
row per noise level and one aggregate summary row. The software is available under an MIT license
and archived on Zenodo \citep{noiseinject}.

\subsubsection{Regression noise}
The regression injector sets a noise shape (Gaussian, Student-$t$ or
Laplace) and a targeting rule (uniform, grouped-wider, grouped-shifted, outlier or censoring)
separately. Each named condition is one combination of the two with its parameters, and the name is recorded with every result.
The requested noise $\tau$ is the noise level times the standard deviation of the clean training labels. The
injector solves for the scale $\tau / G$ in closed
form with Equation~\ref{eq:dose_matching}, and a level of zero returns the
labels unchanged. The affected molecules are selected by a separately seeded random number generator, so they are
the same at every noise level while the noise draws change. Under
grouped-wider, whole scaffold families are added in random order until they cover as close as possible
to $20\%$ of the training molecules.

% TODO: check (TH lens): real potency censoring falls mostly on the inactive side, as Chapter 7 says. Why clip the largest labels here? And are censored labels flagged ('>') in the real data?
Censoring is parameterized by the fraction $f$ of labels clipped, with its limit at the $(1-f)$ quantile
of the clean training labels. On the training split the censored
set is the $k = \mathrm{round}(fn)$ largest labels; on any other split it is whichever
molecules lie past the training limit.

Each injection also returns a record of the target and delivered root-mean-square noise, the factor
$G$, the solved scale, the mean of $\epsilon$, the fraction of molecules affected, the group sizes and the random seeds.
The noise is checked against a tolerance band, described in Additional file~\ref{app:add:dose_tolerance}, and a draw outside it raises a warning.

% TODO: check (TH lens): 'level' means the standard deviation in Chapter 3 but the variance here. Say what a level of 1.0 and 0.5 mean in standard deviation (one unit, about 0.71 units).
Under uniform and grouped-shifted targeting every molecule gets the same scale, so the package flags these scales
as degenerate instead of reporting a rank correlation of zero.
A separate injector sizes the noise against a unit supplied by the user. In Chapter~\ref{chap:noise} this unit is set to be the standard deviation of a model's out-of-fold residuals on
the clean training labels. It either adds Gaussian noise or shifts $2\%$, $5\%$ or $10\%$ of labels. The Gaussian noise has variance the level times the square of the unit. Here the level scales the variance, not the standard deviation as in Equation~\ref{eq:dose_matching}. Each shift is the level times the unit, with a random sign or upward only. It can also add
Gaussian noise whose standard deviation follows a per-molecule covariate or a hidden
log-normal draw, scaled to the same mean variance.

\subsubsection{Classification noise}
The package provides six label-flipping strategies: uniform flips,
flip rates set by class frequency, asymmetric flip rates for binary tasks, a random flip rate for each sample,
a flip rate for each class, and flips directed by a confusion matrix. Because the realized
flip rate usually differs from the nominal probability, a binary search
finds the probability that changes a target fraction of labels.

% TODO: check (TH lens): why 'approximately'? Retention exceeds 1 if noise raises the score, and drops below 0 if R2 turns negative. And 'less directly correlated': by how much, against which metrics?
\subsubsection{Robustness metrics}
For regression, the package computes $R^2$, the root mean squared error (RMSE) and the mean absolute
error at every noise level. For classification, it computes accuracy, together with macro- and weighted-averaged precision, recall and F1
score and a per-class breakdown. Robustness is read from the
retention curve $\mathrm{ret}(x) = m(x)/m(0)$, where $x$ is the noise
level. Here $m$ is a metric bounded above by 1 for which higher values are better: $R^2$ for regression, and accuracy, F1, precision or recall for classification. The primary summary is the normalized area under this curve, \aucnorm{}, defined in Equation~\ref{eq:aucnorm} of Chapter~\ref{chap:robustness} and
evaluated by the trapezoid rule. It is the mean fraction of clean performance retained across the
noise levels and lies approximately within $[0, 1]$. It is also less directly correlated with clean-label $R^2$ than other robustness metrics we tested. A
secondary summary fits $\mathrm{ret}(x) \approx \exp\!\left(-(x/\tau_W)^{\beta}\right)$ to the curve. The shape parameter
$\beta$ separates curves that hold and then collapse ($\beta > 1$) from curves that fall early and
then plateau ($\beta < 1$). The parameter $\tau_W$ sets the scale of the decay, and the fit requires at least four
noise levels. The
package also reports the clean baseline and the retention at the highest noise level, flags any
curve that falls below zero retention, and can withhold the curve summaries for
models whose clean baseline is too low.

\subsubsection{Uncertainty metrics}
For a model that returns a predicted standard deviation
$u_i$ for each sample, the package computes, at each noise level, the Spearman correlation between $u_i$ and the
absolute error $|y_i - \hat{y}_i|$, the empirical coverage $\frac{1}{n}\sum_i \mathbb{1}\left[|y_i -
\hat{y}_i| \le k u_i\right]$ at $k = 1$ and $k = 2$, against Gaussian targets of $68.3\%$ and
$95.4\%$, the mean interval width $\frac{1}{n}\sum_i 2u_i$, and the expected calibration error (ECE),
\begin{equation}
\mathrm{ECE} = \sum_{b=1}^{10} \frac{|B_b|}{n}\,\left|\bar{u}_b - \overline{|y - \hat{y}|}_b\right|,
\label{eq:ece}
\end{equation}
computed over ten equal-count bins $B_b$ of $u$; all of these use the clean test labels. Under Gaussian errors a calibrated model has a mean absolute error of $\sqrt{2/\pi}\,u \approx 0.80\,u$, so its ECE is about $0.2\,\bar{u}$, not zero. The Spearman
correlation between $u_i$ and the injected noise $|\epsilon_i|$ can only be computed on the corrupted molecules,
where the noise is known, and is reported
within each noise level. A correlation pooled across levels is also returned, but it
mostly reflects that uncertainty and noise both rise with the level, so it
does not measure whether a model can identify which molecules are corrupted.

Three optional wrappers produce $u_i$ from a point predictor: split-conformal prediction around any
scikit-learn-compatible model, optionally scaled by the label spread of each molecule's nearest
training neighbors; an exact Gaussian process with a Tanimoto or RBF kernel through \texttt{Gauche}
\citep{gauche}; and Monte Carlo dropout on any \texttt{PyTorch} network \citep{gal2016}.

\section{Evaluation methodology}\label{sec:evaluation}

Chapter~\ref{chap:robustness} reports predictive performance as $R^2$ on held-out molecules and robustness as \aucnorm{}, defined in Equation~\ref{eq:aucnorm}. Chapters~\ref{chap:flaws} to~\ref{chap:noise} state the metric used for each dataset where it is introduced, and report results as per-fold scores and their spread across folds, with no significance tests.

% TODO: check (TH lens): Chapter 3 groups by chirality-blind scaffold with each acyclic molecule alone; Chapter 5 keeps stereochemistry and puts acyclic molecules in one empty-scaffold group. Two differences; say which applies where.
No split or fold in this thesis is random, and no group of molecules is split between training and test. Random splitting inflates performance estimates for molecular data, because it places structurally similar molecules on both sides of the train/test boundary \citep{Yang2019, Wallach2018, Landrum2023SIMPD}. For small molecules the group is the Bemis--Murcko scaffold family described in Section~\ref{sec:noise_injection}. Section~\ref{sec:robustness:methods} gives the splits of Chapter~\ref{chap:robustness}, and Section~\ref{sec:method:datasets} gives the folds of the later chapters and the groups used for datasets that are not small molecules.

\section{Conclusions}

% TODO: check (TH lens): 'across the board': which effects?
A QSAR model consists of two parts, the molecular representation and the model itself. Measures of similarity depend on the representation as well, and a representation with uninformative features generates negative effects across the board. Label noise coming from experimental assays, while often assumed to be Gaussian in literature, can take on a variety of forms. For this reason, we defined seven artificial noise conditions. Methods for noisy labels fall into noise detection, noise mitigation and noise-robust training. No method works on every dataset, all of them show promise conditionally. 

\chapter{Noise robustness across molecular representations and model architectures}\label{chap:robustness}

\textit{The work in this chapter is adapted from Punt, A., Hanser, T., Werner, S., and Morris, G.M., NoiseInject: A Benchmarking Framework to Study Noise Robustness in QSAR Models, to be submitted to the Journal of Cheminformatics. Most of the figures, tables, and text in this chapter are therefore either identical or similar to the content of that manuscript. The authors' contributions are given in the Statement of Contribution.}

\section{Introduction}


In this chapter, we inject the noise conditions of Chapter~\ref{chap:background} into training labels and measure how much predictive performance each pairing of representation and model retains. While there have been many investigations into the effect of noise on molecular property prediction, direct comparisons between how different molecular representations respond to label noise remain limited. The choice of molecular representation certainly affects predictive performance. \citet{Deng2023} evaluated 62,820 models and found that fixed representations, in most datasets RDKit 2D descriptors, outperformed representations learned from SMILES and molecular graphs. Whether these conclusions translate to noise robustness is our question. Between different types of label noise, \citet{Heid2023} found no difference in model performance between error distributions with the same mean and standard deviation. We expand on this by considering alternative patterns of noise, namely noise concentrated in structural groups or on a sparse subset of samples.

This chapter addresses the questions set out in Chapter~\ref{chap:intro}, specifically how the choice of representation and model architecture explain the variance in predictive performance and in noise robustness, what is the impact of the type of label noise, and whether uncertainty estimates track label noise.

\section{Methods}\label{sec:robustness:methods}

We paired six representations with thirteen base models, as well as several variant models, across four prediction tasks. The hyperparameters of every model are given in Additional file~\ref{app:noise:sec:hyperparameters_default}.

\subsection{Datasets}\label{sec:robustness:datasets}

Although we are investigating the effects of label noise stemming from experimental settings, for the purposes of testing we inject controlled amounts of artificial noise into clean labels. Data were extracted from the QM9 molecular property dataset distributed by PyTorch Geometric \citep{Ramakrishnan2014}, which contains 130,831 small organic molecules with pre-computed quantum-mechanical properties obtained from density functional theory (DFT) calculations at the B3LYP/6-31G(2df,p) level of theory. We selected the gap between the highest occupied and the lowest unoccupied molecular orbital (HOMO--LUMO gap) as the prediction target in QM9. We assumed that QM9 labels were free of noise, notwithstanding approximations in the level of theory. Each experiment was replicated 10 times with a distinct random seed and a randomly selected subset of $N = 10{,}000$ molecules, split 80/10/10 into 8,000 training, 1,000 validation and 1,000 test molecules. All models were fitted on those 8,000 training molecules, except the Gaussian processes, which were fitted on a random subset of 5,000 of them; the validation and test molecules were unchanged.

We repeated these experiments on three molecular property datasets with experimentally determined endpoints. From the OpenADMET initiative \citep{openadmet}, we used LogD and Caco-2 efflux permeability, with $N = 5{,}039$ and $N = 2{,}161$ respectively. We also selected hERG $K_i$ data from ChEMBL \citep{Zdrazil2023}, following a protocol inspired by \citet{landrum2024}: filter for binding assays, deduplicate by median pChEMBL value, and remove compounds with an inter-assay standard deviation greater than 1.0 log unit, resulting in $N = 1{,}415$ compounds. Each of the three was partitioned into five folds by grouped $k$-fold cross-validation (CV), and each fold's training block gave up a further grouped fifth as a validation set for early stopping. A model therefore fits between 3,194 and 3,364 of the 5,039 LogD molecules, depending on the fold, so the Gaussian-process subsample never applies to these datasets.
