Unlike QM9's 10 replicates, which are 10 independent draws from one pool, each assay dataset is a single fixed set in which every molecule is tested exactly once and nothing is redrawn.

Log $D$ is the logarithm of the distribution coefficient of a molecule between octanol and water, usually measured at pH 7.4. It counts every charge state, whereas log $P$ counts only the neutral form, and a basic amine that is protonated at that pH therefore has a log $D$ below its log $P$ \citep{Bhal2007}. Caco-2 cells come from a human colorectal carcinoma and grow as a layer that models absorption in the gut. Permeability across the layer is measured in both directions, and the efflux ratio is the permeability from the basolateral to the apical side divided by that in the other direction \citep{Hubatsch2007}. In this chapter the Caco-2 label is the efflux ratio on a logarithmic scale. A ratio above about 2 suggests the compound is pumped out by a transporter such as P-glycoprotein. The hERG gene encodes a cardiac potassium channel. Drugs that block it can prolong the QT interval and cause arrhythmia, so hERG binding is screened early in drug discovery \citep{Sanguinetti2006}. The hERG $K_i$ dataset holds binding affinities for this channel, as p$K_i$ values.

All splits and CV folds were grouped by the chirality-blind Bemis--Murcko scaffold, implemented such that each distinct acyclic molecule forms its own group, and groups are filled in random order. Prior to modeling, all molecular structures were sanitized and canonicalized using \texttt{RDKit} to remove invalid valence states and standardize atom and bond typing \citep{rdkit}. Prediction targets were standardized to zero mean and unit variance. Experiments that track uncertainty were run on 10 replicates on QM9 and five folds on each assay dataset.

\subsection{Representations}

In this study, we evaluated six molecular representations: extended-connectivity fingerprints of diameter four (ECFP4), Avalon, Sort \& Slice (SNS), physicochemical descriptors (PDV), ChemBERTa and Molecular Hypergraph Grammar GNNs (MHG-GNNs). The same six were used on QM9 and on the three assay datasets. We used the standard ECFP with radius $r = 2$ with $d = 2048$ bits \citep{rogers2010, rdkit}. Sort \& Slice takes the same substructures as ECFP4, but it ranks them by prevalence and selects the top $L$ ($L=1024$) \citep{sns}. We fitted the Sort \& Slice vocabulary on the training molecules alone, refitting it for each QM9 replicate and for each CV fold.  Avalon is a 2,048-bit fingerprint, generated with the Avalon toolkit through RDKit \citep{Gedeck2006, rdkit}. The PDV representation is a vector of 200 physicochemical descriptors computed with RDKit's \texttt{MolecularDescriptorCalculator} \citep{rdkit, Cherkasov2014}; the 200 are listed in Additional file~\ref{app:noise:sec:pdv_descriptors}, and the list is fixed in our code rather than taken from RDKit's default descriptor set, which grows between releases. ChemBERTa embeddings are the mean over the non-padding token embeddings of the \texttt{DeepChem/ChemBERTa-77M-MTR} checkpoint, producing 384 features \citep{Ahmad2022}. MHG-GNNs are a GIN-based autoencoder pre-trained on about 1.38 million PubChem molecules using $\beta$-VAE loss, producing 1024-dimensional embeddings through iterative message passing and found in the published \texttt{mhggnn\_\allowbreak pretrained\_\allowbreak model\_\allowbreak 0724\_\allowbreak 2023} checkpoint \citep{kishimoto2023}. We z-score normalized PDV, ChemBERTa and MHG-GNN per feature, using the training molecules' mean and standard deviation; ECFP4, Avalon and Sort \& Slice reach the model as built.

\subsection{Models}

To understand model behavior in the presence of label noise, we explored a range of model families. We fitted tree ensembles, a support vector machine, Gaussian processes and neural networks. Several are variants of one another, differing in the probabilistic machinery they add, and Additional file~\ref{app:noise:sec:hyperparameters_default} gives every configuration with its settings.

Random Forests (RFs) are one of the most common choices for QSAR modeling thanks to their robustness and interpretability \citep{Svetnik2003}. Quantile Regression Forests (QRFs) hold the distribution of training labels in each leaf and compute quantiles over all trees \citep{Meinshausen2006}. While we used 100 trees for RF, we observed a noisy quantile estimate with 100 trees for QRF, prompting us to use 300 instead. To observe the effect of the quantile mechanism, we then fit a second RF at 300 trees so the two models were identical in all other settings. 

We also used eXtreme Gradient Boosting (XGBoost) \citep{Chen2016}, Light Gradient-Boosting Machine (LightGBM) \citep{ke2017lightgbm}, and Natural Gradient Boosting (NGBoost) \citep{Duan2020}. NGBoost extends deterministic gradient boosting by treating the parameters of a chosen parametric distribution as regression targets and learns them through boosting with a natural gradient update rule \citep{Duan2020}. NGBoost relies on scoring off validation data, which differs by dataset. On QM9 the validation split is already held out of every fit. However, on the three assay datasets NGBoost holds out its own scaffold-grouped fifth of the rows it is handed, which already exclude the validation molecules.

Support vector machines (SVMs) are a well-established baseline in QSAR modeling \citep{Vapnik1995, Cortes2015}. We used a radial basis function (RBF) with $C = 1.0$ and $\gamma = 1/(d\,\mathrm{Var}(X))$, where $d$ is the number of features and $\mathrm{Var}(X)$ the variance of all training feature values, as the kernel, as it accommodates continuous vectors. 

Gaussian processes (GPs) are a non-parametric class of models that use a kernel to produce a Gaussian predictive distribution over every data point \citep{Obrezanova2007, Rasmussen2005}. We fitted three variants of GPs, each with a constant mean and a scaled kernel. The first uses an RBF kernel and runs on all six representations. The second uses the Tanimoto kernel \citep{Ralaivola2005, moss2020} and was run on ECFP4 alone; it is not reported in this chapter. The third is a heteroscedastic variant in which the RBF process is given a second network that predicts the observation noise of each molecule. One of the major limitations of using GPs in practice is computational scaling; inference has $O(N^3)$ time complexity and $O(N^2)$ memory, where $N$ is the number of training points \citep{Rasmussen2005}. This can be mitigated with sparse GP approximations, but these introduce approximation error and can be more difficult to tune \citep{quinonero2005}. As such, we used a standard GP and only fit on at most $5{,}000$ training molecules, drawn by a seeded sample. Due to the size of the datasets, this cap only affected QM9. We implemented these GP models in \texttt{GPyTorch} \citep{gpytorch}, with the Tanimoto kernel taken from the \texttt{Gauche} framework \citep{gauche}.

We tested two deterministic feed-forward architectures, both fully connected and both implemented in PyTorch \citep{Paszke2019}: (i) NN-$\alpha$ with two hidden layers of sizes 128 and 64, and dropout after each hidden layer and (ii) NN-$\beta$ with two hidden layers, each of size 128, with dropout applied before the output layer. Each model was trained using the Adam optimizer with early stopping on validation loss. We used Bayesian transformations to convert NNs into Bayesian neural networks (BNNs), and implemented them using \texttt{PyTorch} \citep{Paszke2019} with ReLU activations and dropout regularization ($p=0.2$). Additional file~\ref{app:noise:sec:hyperparameters_default} gives their full widths, dropout fractions, learning rates and batch size.

% TODO: check (TH lens): tuned on which molecules and which labels, clean or noisy? Say tuning never used test molecules.
To get these networks producing uncertainty estimates, we applied four probabilistic transformations to each architecture, producing eight probabilistic networks. The first replaces every linear layer with a Bayesian layer carrying a Gaussian prior $\mathcal{N}(0, 0.1^2)$ on its weights, producing BNN-$\alpha$ and BNN-$\beta$. They are trained on an evidence lower bound whose  Kullback–Leibler (KL) divergence term is divided by the number of training molecules. While this is a straightforward approach, it is computationally expensive. We explored alternative techniques like our second variant, where we add an output head that predicts a variance to the BNN and fit the negative log likelihood of a Gaussian with that variance \citep{kendall2017}. The third, Variational Bayesian Last Layer (VBLL), replaces the output layer with a Bayesian last layer that produces mean-field variational posteriors $q(\mathbf{W}) = \mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{\mu}_W, \text{diag}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_W^2))$ over the last-layer weights and trains by maximizing the evidence lower bound. This can be thought of as minimizing the reconstruction loss plus KL divergence $D_{\text{KL}}(q(\mathbf{W}) \| p(\mathbf{W}))$ from a standard normal prior, scaled by $1/N$ \citep{Harrison2024}. Because only its output layer keeps a learned observation-noise parameter, VBLL networks report only epistemic uncertainty per molecule, with one aleatoric variance for the whole fit. The fourth makes observation noise a function of the input. All eight probabilistic networks estimate their predictive distributions from 100 stochastic forward passes. Each network and its Bayesian counterpart were tuned to the same set of parameters so that NN-$\alpha$ against BNN-$\alpha$ and NN-$\beta$ against BNN-$\beta$ differ by the Bayesian layers alone. 

The thirteen base models are RF, QRF, XGBoost, LightGBM, NGBoost, SVM, the RBF-kernel GP, and NN, BNN and VBLL in each of the two architectures. The six variant models are RF (300 trees); GP (het.), the heteroscedastic GP; BNN-$\alpha$ (var.\ head) and BNN-$\beta$ (var.\ head), the two Bayesian networks with a variance head; and VBLL-$\alpha$ (het.) and VBLL-$\beta$ (het.), the two heteroscedastic VBLL networks.

\subsection{Noise conditions and levels}

% TODO: check (TH lens): restate the seven conditions in one or two lines here rather than send the reader back to Chapter 2.
The seven noise conditions are defined in Section~\ref{sec:noise_injection}.

We controlled noise quantity using noise levels, representing a fraction of the standard deviation of clean training labels. We ran experiments across seven levels, $0$, $0.2$, $0.3$, $0.5$, $0.75$, $1.0$ and $1.5$. We added the noise to the raw label and standardized the target afterwards, using the clean training mean and spread. To control the quantity of noise for censoring, we recorded every label above the $k$th largest clean training label as that limit and used this level as the fraction clipped, at $0$, $0.1$, $0.2$, $0.25$, $0.3$, $0.4$ and $0.5$. Censoring is common in pharmaceutical assays, where a third or more of labels can be censored \citep{Svensson2025}.

% TODO: check (TH lens): a small table of which noise conditions ran on which models, representations and datasets would show where each comparison comes from; the next paragraph's lists are hard to hold in mind.
We ran Gaussian, grouped-wider and grouped-shifted noise on every model and representation pairing. The
remaining four conditions ran on selected pairs.

% TODO: check (TH lens): AUC_norm is used here before the Evaluation subsection defines it; say in a few words what it is, or point forward to its definition.
On QM9, Laplace, Student-$t$ and outlier noise were run on eight models, namely RF, NGBoost, SVM, the GP, GP (het.), BNN-$\alpha$ (var.\ head), VBLL-$\alpha$ (het.) and VBLL-$\beta$ (het.), each on ECFP4, PDV and ChemBERTa. On the three assay datasets they were run on the same models and representations except VBLL-$\alpha$ (het.). Censoring was run on five pairings, namely RF and GP (het.) on ECFP4, and NGBoost, the GP and BNN-$\alpha$ (var.\ head) on PDV. On the three assay datasets, \aucnorm{} under censoring was computed for the same five pairings, except BNN-$\alpha$ (var.\ head) on PDV for hERG $K_i$, whose clean $R^2$ was below 0.3 in every fold. The uncertainty statistics under censoring on the assay datasets cover seven models, namely QRF, NGBoost, the GP, GP (het.), BNN-$\alpha$ (var.\ head), BNN-$\beta$ (var.\ head) and VBLL-$\alpha$, each on ECFP4, PDV and ChemBERTa.

\subsection{QM9 data pipeline}

QM9 \citep{Ramakrishnan2014}, loaded through PyTorch Geometric, was processed by a separate pipeline where the Python code loads and splits the data, and computes several representations. The data is then passed to the Rust portion of the pipeline which injects the label noise, standardizes the labels, computes the remaining representations, and sends the processed data back to Python which trains and scores the models. 

Each replicate, using its own random seed, randomly selects a subset from QM9, which is split by a new scaffold split, and the same grouping defines the scaffold families targeted by the grouped noise conditions.


Python and the Rust program exchange the data through memory-mapped binary files on disk, one record per molecule, as described in Additional file~\ref{app:add:mmap}.

We compute the quantity of noise, the censoring limit and the affected scaffold families using only the clean training labels. Validation labels get all three, with the amount in label units, from an independent draw seeded from the training seed. The program checks that validation received as much noise as training. Test labels are never corrupted, but every split records the noise scale each molecule would have received, so that held-out molecules can be scored against the pattern applied in training.

For every molecule, the program records its canonical SMILES, clean and noisy labels, standardized value, injected noise, noise scale and noise pattern. Each run also writes a summary of the noise parameters and the amount of noise delivered. The run halts if a record holds a different molecule when it is written than when its noise was drawn, or if any molecule has an all-zero ECFP4 or Avalon fingerprint, which marks a failed featurization. A self-test mode runs the same noise checks on a label file alone.

\subsection{Uncertainty estimation}

Up to this point we have been concerned with how noise affects the predictive performance of QSAR models. However, label noise should also affect the uncertainty these models report. Some models return only a point prediction, while others also return an estimate of uncertainty, either as a predicted distribution or as the spread across an ensemble \citep{Lanini2024UNIQUE}. Furthermore, some report a single value of uncertainty while others report one that can be decomposed into an aleatoric and an epistemic component \citep{kendall2017}. The aleatoric component describes underlying noise the model associates with the data, while the epistemic component represents the uncertainty within the model's fit. We sought to determine if, as expected, the aleatoric component rises with injected label noise while the epistemic component does not.

For the BNN and VBLL transformations, we took the two components over $T = 100$ stochastic forward
passes \citep{kendall2017}:
\begin{equation}
\hat{u}^2_{\text{ale}}(x) = \frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\hat{v}_t(x),
\qquad
\hat{u}^2_{\text{epi}}(x) = \frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\big(\hat{\mu}_t(x) - \bar{\mu}(x)\big)^2 ,
\label{eq:sampling_split}
\end{equation}
where $\hat{\mu}_t(x)$ and $\hat{v}_t(x)$ are the mean and the variance returned for molecule $x$ on
pass $t$, and $\bar{\mu}(x)$ is the mean over passes. Here $\hat{v}_t(x)$ is the predicted observation
noise variance. It varies per molecule in the variance-head and heteroscedastic variants, and is one
number for the whole fit in the VBLL transformation. A full-BNN without a variance head returns no
variance, so its uncertainty is epistemic alone.

The other models follow the same split. For GPs, the epistemic component is the posterior variance of the latent function and the aleatoric component is the learned observation noise \citep{Rasmussen2005}. All variants of the GP produce one number for the whole fit except heteroscedastic. For QRF models, each tree holds multiple training molecules. So for a given molecule, the aleatoric component is the variance of the labels in the molecule's given leaf. The total aleatoric uncertainty is those variances averaged over the trees. The epistemic component is the variance of the leaf means across the trees. NGBoost predicts one variance for each molecule from a single fit, which is aleatoric alone \citep{Duan2020}. In every model, the total uncertainty is the sum of the aleatoric and epistemic components.

No held-out label carries injected noise, so every comparison against the injected noise was made on
training molecules. Each training set was divided into five folds grouped on Bemis--Murcko scaffolds, and each
molecule was scored by a refit that excluded its own fold. For NGBoost on QM9, three of the five folds
were scored to limit its cost. No uncertainty was calibrated after fitting.

\subsection{Evaluation}
We compute statistics on the held-out molecules, for QM9 the test split and for three assay datasets the held-out fold of the scaffold CV. Different combinations of dataset, model, representation, noise condition, and noise level are examined in each scenario. We used the coefficient of determination ($R^2$) throughout. For molecule $i$, let $y_i$ be the clean label, $\hat{y}_i$ the prediction, $\epsilon_i$ the noise injected into its label and $u_i$ the predicted uncertainty.

% TODO: check (TH lens): which other metrics, and how correlated with clean R2 was each, next to AUC_norm's -0.10? The metric was chosen on the same results it reports.
To evaluate the effect of label noise, we tracked predictive performance retention under increasing artificial noise. For each QSAR pairing we recorded $R^2(\tau)$ at each noise level $\tau$, and normalized it to obtain the retention ratio $R^2(\tau)/R^2(0)$. To quantify robustness, we used the normalized area under this retention curve,
\begin{equation}
\aucnorm = \frac{1}{\tau_{\max}} \int_0^{\tau_{\max}} \frac{R^2(\tau)}{R^2(0)}\, d\tau,
\label{eq:aucnorm}
\end{equation}
evaluated using the trapezoidal rule over the seven noise levels, $\tau \in \{0,\allowbreak 0.2,\allowbreak 0.3,\allowbreak 0.5,\allowbreak 0.75,\allowbreak 1.0,\allowbreak 1.5\}$, each a fraction of the standard deviation of the clean training labels. While we tested other metrics of robustness, we found \aucnorm{} to be less directly correlated to clean-label $R^2$ predictions. For censoring, we sweep over a set of fractions to clip the labels prior to normalization. 

An \aucnorm{} value near 1 indicates that the QSAR model in question has retained predictive performance with artificial noise, meaning it is more robust to noise. Since we normalize the curve by its own clean-label predictive performance, a model with a low clean $R^2$ has less to lose, so \aucnorm{} is reported beside clean $R^2$. We excluded replicates with a clean $R^2 < 0.3$ from the robustness analysis, as performance retention ratios become unstable when the clean-label denominator approaches zero. The excluded configurations are listed in Additional file~\ref{app:noise:sec:excluded_configurations}. On QM9 one \aucnorm{} is computed per replicate, and we report the median over the 10 replicates. On the three assay datasets one value is computed per scaffold fold, and the median over the five folds is reported. 

To compare the relative effect of the 13 base models across datasets and noise conditions, we compared their rankings using Spearman's rank correlation coefficient ($\rho$) between clean $R^2$ and \aucnorm. We compared probabilistic models with their deterministic counterparts using a two-sided Wilcoxon signed-rank test paired on the replicate, at $\alpha = 0.05$, separately for each representation and noise condition. This test was run only on QM9, as with five scaffold folds the
smallest attainable $p$ is 0.0625.

% TODO: check (TH lens): with sequential sums of squares the shares can depend on the order. Is every model, representation and replicate combination present after the excluded replicates? If so, say the order does not matter.
We conducted a separate analysis of variance (ANOVA) for each noise condition to identify the
relative contributions of molecular representation and model architecture to both predictive
performance and noise robustness. For a given metric $y$, either $R^2$ at a noise level of 1.0 or
\aucnorm:
\begin{equation}
y_{fmjr} = \mu + s_r + \alpha_f + \gamma_{m(f)} + \beta_j + (\alpha\beta)_{fj} + (\gamma\beta)_{m(f)j} + e_{fmjr},
\end{equation}
where $s_r$ represents the effect of replicate $r$, $\alpha_f$ represents the effect of model family $f$, $\gamma_{m(f)}$ represents the effect of model architecture $m$ within its family, $\beta_j$ represents the effect of
molecular representation $j$, $(\alpha\beta)_{fj}$ and $(\gamma\beta)_{m(f)j}$ are the interaction terms, and $e_{fmjr}$ is the
residual. The proportion of variance explained by each term was calculated as the
effect size
\begin{equation}
\eta^2_{\text{term}} = \frac{SS_{\text{term}}}{SS_{\text{total}}},
\end{equation}
using Type I (sequential) sums of squares, entered in the order replicate, model family, model architecture within family, molecular
representation, interaction. The 10 QM9 replicates provide the residual. The uncertainty on each share is the 95\% interval of $\eta^2$ over 1,000 bootstrap resamples of whole replicates. These shares describe the models and representations tested here, not the choice of model or representation in general. 

ANOVA relies on the assumption that observations both within and across groups are independent. Two features of this study break that assumption. First, each QM9 replicate uses the same data split and the same noise draw for every model and representation, so observations from one replicate are not independent of each other; we therefore entered the replicate as a block. Second, across the whole set of models in this study, some pairs produced similar predictions that are not independent, so a method present in several near-identical versions would count several times towards the share of model architecture. To handle this issue, we grouped the models into families. We considered grouping pairs of models that share a method or an architecture, and compared each pair on clean $R^2$ and on \aucnorm{} under the three noise conditions run on every representation, separately on each of the six representations, which gives 24 comparisons per pair. A pair was grouped when the mean difference between the two models was smaller than their standard deviation across replicates in at least two thirds of these comparisons. Additional file~\ref{app:noise:sec:model_redundancy} gives the details of this grouping. Five families met this criterion: RF with quantile regression forests (QRF; 16 of 24), XGBoost with LightGBM (23 of 24), NN-$\alpha$ with BNN-$\alpha$ (20 of 24), NN-$\beta$ with BNN-$\beta$ (18 of 24), and VBLL-$\alpha$ with VBLL-$\beta$ (20 of 24). VBLL did not group with the plain or Bayesian network of its own architecture (4 to 10 of 24), as its clean $R^2$ was lower by 0.02 to 0.09, and NGBoost did not group with XGBoost or LightGBM (2 and 3 of 24). The two architectures of the plain networks, and of the Bayesian networks, also met the criterion (18 and 17 of 24); we grouped by architecture instead, which the evidence supports equally well. The 13 base models therefore form eight families. Within these families, the differences between models accounted for at most 1.0\% of the variance in any outcome and noise condition on QM9, and up to 15.1\% of predictive performance on clean labels on the three assay datasets. Additional file~\ref{app:noise:sec:tukey_within_families} reports Tukey's honestly significant difference test, which we also ran between the members of each family, separately for each representation. With 10 replicates it detects differences smaller than the variation between replicates, so we report it beside the size of each difference rather than use it to form the families.

Across representations, we considered grouping SNS with the ECFP4 fingerprint, as both encode overlapping circular substructure information. The two differed by more than their variation across replicates on clean $R^2$ for every model (0 of 13), so all six representations were kept as separate levels. The comparison is given in Additional file~\ref{app:noise:sec:representation_redundancy}.

% TODO: check (TH lens): Methods divides by the mean aleatoric component, but Results speak of 'the aleatoric growth' (0.43; 0.61 to 1.14). Check the denominator in the code and make both say the same.
To assess the interaction between uncertainty estimation and label noise, we posed three questions. First, whether uncertainty rises with label noise, as measured by the mean $u_i$ over held-out molecules at the highest noise level
divided by its value with no added noise, in each replicate or fold. The second pertains to models that report separate aleatoric and epistemic components. We divided the increase of mean epistemic uncertainty between the highest level of noise and clean labels by the mean of the aleatoric component to determine how each responds to label noise. A ratio near 0 means the added noise was attributed to the labels while a ratio of 1 or above means the epistemic component grew as much or more. We also compared the mean aleatoric component with the standard deviation of the injected noise. The third question we had was whether per-sample uncertainty values track injected label noise, as measured by the Spearman correlation between $u_i$ and $|\epsilon_i|$ over out-of-fold training molecules, per fold and per noise level. We also report the Spearman correlation between $u_i$ and the absolute error of the prediction. Finally, we asked whether dividing a molecule's out-of-fold absolute error by $u_i$ ranks the corrupted labels better than the error alone, and report the difference between the two Spearman correlations with $|\epsilon_i|$.

\section{Results}

\subsection{Variance decomposition}

% TODO: check (TH lens): true because all six representations are computed without labels and the two embeddings are frozen; say so, since a representation learned on the noisy labels would be affected.
Considering the two main components of a QSAR model, the molecular representation and the model architecture, we sought to understand how each contributes to both predictive performance and noise robustness. ANOVA decomposition divides the variance between model family, model architecture within its family, molecular representation, the pairing of the two, the replicate, and a residual. We ran this decomposition for Gaussian noise, the standard model, as well as methods whose injected noise is related to the feature space, grouped-wider and grouped-shifted. Figure~\ref{fig:variance} and Table~\ref{tab:variance} give the results. For predictive performance measured in $R^2$ at a noise level of 1.0, model family and molecular representation account for comparable shares of the variance, 26.6\% and 29.0\% under Gaussian noise. However, for noise robustness, quantified by the normalized area under the R$^2$ retention curve (\aucnorm; higher values indicate greater robustness), model family accounts for 47.2\% of the variance while molecular representation for 8.1\%. This pattern is observed across noise conditions and makes sense, as label noise does not impact the features. The interaction between model and molecular representation explains 19.9\% of the variance in \aucnorm, more than twice the 8.1\% explained by representation alone, meaning  representation acts on noise robustness mainly through the model it is paired with. As such, we primarily compare representations within each model.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth,height=0.72\textheight,keepaspectratio]{F2_variance_decomposition.png}
\caption[Variance decomposition of robustness and predictive performance on QM9.]{Share of the variance explained by each term, on the QM9 HOMO--LUMO gap: a) robustness (\aucnorm);
b) predictive performance ($R^2$ at a noise level of 1.0). Whiskers are 95\% bootstrap intervals over replicates. Model within family, at most 1.0\% in every decomposition on QM9, is not drawn.}
\label{fig:variance}
\end{figure}

Figure~\ref{fig:three_outcomes}a compares the variance decomposition on both clean and noisy labels. For predictive performance, the artificial label noise lowers the impact of the choice of model family, while the replicate and residual take more. This means that the variance is left more up to chance and to the underlying variation within the data itself with label noise, which is the expected behavior. Robustness divides the variance a bit differently. Representation explains 8.1\% of the variance in \aucnorm, against 31.1\% of clean $R^2$, while model family rises to 47.2\%. The replicate nearly disappears from \aucnorm, likely because each replicate's curve is divided by that replicate's own clean $R^2$, so a difficult split lowers both and cancels out.

We include two terms which stem from the data rather than the model, the residual and replicate terms. The replicate measures how much of the variance can be attributed to the train and test split, which in this case is a scaffold split. On QM9 we replicated each experiment 10 times with different random draws of $N=10{,}000$, each time giving a new scaffold split. On the assay datasets, a replicate refers to one of the five scaffold CV folds. A large replicate term in ANOVA indicates that the folds differ from one another. The residual is variance that no other term accounts for.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth,height=0.72\textheight,keepaspectratio]{F2d_three_outcomes_every_dataset.png}

\caption[Variance shares of clean performance, noisy performance and robustness on all four datasets.]{Share of the variance explained by each term on a) the QM9 HOMO--LUMO gap, b) LogD, c) Caco-2 and d) hERG $K_i$, for clean $R^2$, and for $R^2$ at a noise level of 1.0 (0.75 on Caco-2, whose clean $R^2$ is only about 0.5, so little is left to lose at 1.0) and \aucnorm{} under Gaussian noise. Whiskers are 95\% intervals from 1,000 bootstrap resamples of replicates: 10 on QM9 and five scaffold folds on each assay dataset. The values and their intervals are in Additional file~\ref{app:noise:sec:variance_intervals}.}
\label{fig:three_outcomes}
\end{figure}

While we observe similar patterns between noise conditions, the predictive performance of grouped-shifted noise in particular varies significantly between replicates, resulting in a larger share for the replicate. We find that 28.4\% of the variance in \aucnorm{} and 51.0\% of the variance in $R^2$ are due to the replicate, with model family falling to 32.7\% and 8.4\%. This indicates that the offset applied to each scaffold may account for more of the variance in both R$^2$ and \aucnorm.

\subsection{Predictive performance and noise robustness}

The variance decomposition tells us what aspect of QSAR modeling affects predictive performance and robustness, however it does not tell us about the characteristics of individual models. Figure~\ref{fig:curves} shows that all models' performance degrades as the labels get increasingly corrupted with noise. The loss remains small until the artificial noise reaches about half the spread of the clean training labels, and then it begins to drop. The rate it drops and the quantity of noise retained is not necessarily correlated to the model's predictive performance on clean labels. Figure~\ref{fig:decoupling} shows that across the 13 base models under Gaussian noise at ECFP4, clean $R^2$ and \aucnorm{} are barely correlated. The Spearman correlation is $-0.10$ ($p = 0.73$). If anything, the lower a model's predictive performance on clean labels, the less it has to lose as the labels get noisier.

Figure~\ref{fig:decoupling} also lets us examine the link between predictive performance and noise robustness by model family. The forests, boosted trees, SVM, and GP all retain relatively high predictive performance on clean labels while maintaining relatively strong robustness to noise. The plain NNs and BNNs are among the highest in predictive performance on clean labels, and are the four least robust. The two variational networks sit between these groups, with lower predictive performance on clean labels and middling noise robustness. Standard feed-forward networks can make strong predictions under clean labels, however they are not a safe bet under noisy ones.

NGBoost is a standout case in the sense it is highly dependent on its paired molecular representation. Under Gaussian noise it has the highest \aucnorm{} on every one of the six representations, yet also the lowest in predictive performance on four of the six representations. It is one of the worst performing models with respect to $R^2$ on clean data. Its high robustness is partially due to the fact that when you start with a low predictive performance, you don't have as much to lose. However, it maintains shallow robustness curves across representations. On ECFP4 NGBoost's predictive performance ranks lowest up to a noise level of 0.75 and stays in the lower half at every noise level. However, on PDV it starts second lowest and ends highest, surpassing the other models between noise levels 1.0 and 1.5. So when paired with the right features, NGBoost has the potential to make better predictions under higher quantities of label noise. On grouped-shifted noise, RF has higher \aucnorm{} values than NGBoost on ECFP4, Avalon and ChemBERTa. Even then, NGBoost reports the highest noise robustness for PDV, MHG-GNN and Sort \& Slice. While NGBoost performs well under higher label noise, LightGBM performs well under smaller quantities of noise. Its performance drops sharply after noise level 1.0.

As we've seen, the choice of representation can have an impact on noise robustness, but that impact is determined by model family, represented by the interaction term between model and representation in Figure~\ref{fig:grid}. Some models are more impacted by representation than others, though. In general, networks span a wider range of \aucnorm{} across the six representations than any of the other models. Between ECFP4 and PDV under Gaussian noise, the four plain and Bayesian networks differ by between 0.037 and 0.065 of \aucnorm, while no tree or kernel model shifts by more than 0.011. VBLLs are an exception, as no two variational networks differ by more than 0.019.

\subsection{Noise conditions on the HOMO--LUMO gap}

Many studies involving experimental label noise rely on the assumption that noise can be modeled by a Gaussian distribution. We decided to vary the distribution which noise is drawn from, as well as the methodology by which it's injected into specific labels. Five of the seven conditions draw each label's error on its own, so the error is random. Grouped-shifted gives a whole scaffold family a shared offset, and censoring pulls every label above a limit down to that limit, so in both the error is systematic. We controlled the quantity of noise by adding it in proportion to the spread of the clean labels on the training set, so a difference between two conditions is a difference in pattern rather than amount. One condition, censoring, could not be held to a set amount as its levels count the fraction of labels clipped.

% TODO: check (TH lens): 'small' compared to what? Give the size from the assay shape table, e.g. the largest change in AUC_norm, and note the BNN-alpha (var. head) exception on Caco-2.
We compare Gaussian noise on the HOMO--LUMO gap to Laplace, Student-$t$ and outlier noise in Figure~\ref{fig:deep}. The shape of the distribution from which the noise was drawn made little difference. SVM reported higher \aucnorm{} values on non-Gaussian label noise, 0.937 under Gaussian noise against 0.947, 0.952 and 0.955 under Student-$t$, outlier and Laplace noise on PDV, while NGBoost lost \aucnorm{} under every one. NGBoost's relatively stronger performance on Gaussian makes sense as it fits a per-molecule scale under a Gaussian likelihood, which the other distributions violate. Table~\ref{tab:assay_shape} shows that on the three assay datasets with ECFP4, SVM gains \aucnorm{} under all three distributions, and NGBoost loses it in all but one case. The size of the shift stays small, which is why these results coincide with \citet{Heid2023}, who found no difference between error distributions drawn at a matched mean and standard deviation. The shape of the noise does not change how much performance a model keeps, but it does change which model keeps the most.

While alternative shapes of noise may be accurately modeled by the Gaussian distribution, we do not find that is the case for systematic label noise. Table~\ref{tab:condition_cost} shows that, relative to Gaussian, grouped-shifted noise lowered the \aucnorm{} across all models and representations. The models struggle to make predictions when the noise is isolated on molecules of specific scaffold families instead of being applied randomly across every sample. The variational networks retained the most predictive performance, while the plain, deterministic NNs lost the most. The predictive abilities of BNN-$\alpha$ degraded at a slower rate compared to its deterministic counterpart on every representation except PDV, while BNN-$\beta$ did so only on MHG-GNN and PDV. Grouped-wider noise changed no model beyond its replicate spread except QRF on Avalon and MHG-GNN, which makes predictions particularly well under this noise condition.  Censoring created the largest drop in predictive performance. In Table~\ref{tab:censoring}, \aucnorm{} was 0.12 to 0.18 lower for the same QSAR models than under Gaussian noise. Censoring levels count clipped labels, though, so the two are on different scales.

Most of these observations are being made at the upper end of the noise range. With ECFP4, six of the seven most robust
models start within 0.015 of one another in $R^2$ for clean labels, stay together up to a noise level of 0.5 and
separate only above 1.0.
Figure~\ref{fig:curves} shows them finishing between 0.692 for LightGBM and 0.741 for RF. We ranked the models by predictive performance at each noise level. Of the 13 base models, RF started ranked seventh, but by level 1.0 it was ranked first. Figure~\ref{fig:rank_level} shows that SVM, on the other hand, falls from first to fifth. Because \aucnorm{} weights the whole range from 0 to 1.5 equally, and the first half of it separates little, it has the potential to understate just how much the pattern of the noise costs. However, choosing to look at performance of a set level has its drawbacks, as the ranks shift at a different pace between noise conditions.

Table~\ref{tab:censoring}.}
\label{fig:deep}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Robustness on the three assay datasets}

% TODO: check (TH lens): 0.6 is not a tested level; say it falls between the tested levels 0.5 and 0.75.
% TODO: check (TH lens): add a one-sentence recap of the QM9 findings before moving on: model family mattered more than representation, systematic noise cost most, noise shape changed little.
So far, we have only discussed results coming on HOMO--LUMO gap from QM9, a computed property whose labels carry no measurement error. We repeated these experiments on three experimentally-obtained datasets,
LogD, Caco-2 \citep{openadmet}, and hERG $K_i$ \citep{Zdrazil2023}, replicating our process of adding artificial noise. Unlike QM9, the experimental endpoints carry their own measurement noise in both training and test labels; the injected artificial noise therefore adds on top of an unknown noise floor. Repeat measurements of the same compound have a standard deviation of about 0.54 log units for p$K_i$ \citep{Kalliokoski2013, Kramer2012}. The hERG $K_i$ labels have a spread of 0.915 log units, so a noise level of 0.6 of that spread is about one unit of that estimated laboratory error. Figure~\ref{fig:assay} gives each model's \aucnorm{} under each noise condition on the three endpoints, with its clean R$^2$ beside it.

Table~\ref{tab:assay_shape} shows that, as on QM9, the noise distribution does not change the predictive capabilities of a model. Laplace, Student-$t$ and outlier noise were run on seven models, RF, the two GPs, SVM, NGBoost, BNN-$\alpha$ (var. head) and VBLL-$\beta$ (het.), on ECFP4, PDV and ChemBERTa. Each delta below is measured from the same model's \aucnorm{} under Gaussian noise. RF and the two GPs never lose
more than 0.03 of \aucnorm{} under these three conditions or under grouped-wider noise, on any dataset or representation. SVM and NGBoost again move in opposite directions, SVM never loses \aucnorm{} under any of the four, while NGBoost loses under all four on Caco-2 and hERG $K_i$. The two networks move furthest in both directions. It's worth remembering that while we are injecting artificial noise, the assay measurements carry their own source of noise whose properties are unknown, so no ``clean'' label is truly clean.

hERG $K_i$ & SVM & 0.859 & +0.051 & +0.035 & +0.025 & +0.055 \\
hERG $K_i$ & GP & 0.831 & +0.022 & +0.034 & +0.025 & +0.030 \\
hERG $K_i$ & GP (het.) & 0.843 & +0.010 & +0.036 & +0.015 & +0.021 \\
hERG $K_i$ & BNN-$\alpha$ (var. head) & 0.809 & -0.034 & -0.032 & -0.063 & -0.009 \\
hERG $K_i$ & VBLL-$\beta$ (het.) & 0.836 & +0.007 & -0.047 & +0.005 & -0.002 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{adjustbox}
\caption[Robustness under the noise distributions on the three assay datasets.]{\aucnorm{} under Gaussian noise, and its change under each other noise condition, for a subset of models on the three assay datasets with ECFP4; negative is a loss. Medians over five scaffold folds.}
\label{tab:assay_shape}
\end{table}

In Figure~\ref{fig:assay}, of the three noise conditions run on every model, grouped-shifted noise gives the lowest \aucnorm{} values on every assay dataset. Under it, RF ranks first or second for \aucnorm{} with every representation on LogD and with four of six on hERG $K_i$. $R^2$ at a noise level of 1.0 depends more on clean performance. By that measure, SVM ranks first to third on LogD, while RF ranks fifth to ninth across datasets.

LightGBM has particularly varied results across the datasets. On LogD, it has relatively high predictive performance and middle-of-the-pack noise robustness. However, on hERG and Caco-2, it loses the most with increased noise, particularly on Gaussian and grouped-shifted noise. The effects are not as pronounced on grouped-wider, although it still ranks among the least robust there.  XGBoost degrades in the same way on hERG and Caco-2.

While the results are not identical between the datasets, Figure~\ref{fig:transfer} shows some shared patterns. The ranks in this paragraph are among the 13 base models, while the figure ranks among all 19. In general, the relative \aucnorm{} model rankings match between QM9 and LogD, as well as between Caco-2 and hERG $K_i$ with every representation except ChemBERTa.  RF is the most consistent, among the 13 base models under Gaussian noise it ranks between first and seventh for \aucnorm{} on every dataset and representation, second or third on QM9 and first on LogD at five of six. The GP has the highest predictive performance on clean labels on all three assay datasets with nearly every representation, and ranks third to ninth for \aucnorm. NGBoost, the most robust model on QM9, ranks first to third on LogD, second to tenth on Caco-2 and sixth to ninth on hERG $K_i$. LightGBM falls furthest, ranking 10th to 13th on Caco-2 and hERG $K_i$ at every representation while being among the highest in predictive performance on clean labels. VBLL-$\alpha$ is the opposite, ranking first or second for \aucnorm{} on hERG
$K_i$ and last on LogD at every representation, from one of the lowest clean $R^2$ values. This fits the pattern of the NNs being exceptionally varied.

In Figure~\ref{fig:assay}, the forests, RF and QRF, sit in the middle for predictive performance on clean labels, but they are the most reliably robust to noise across all datasets. Their rank barely changes between noise conditions, so no pattern of noise suits them better than another. Caco-2 is the exception, where RF ranks third to seventh rather than first or second.

Other models perform conditionally well, and in some cases that condition is the representation they are paired with. The GP has some of the higher predictive capabilities on clean labels and under Gaussian noise across assay datasets. It is the highest on LogD and Caco-2 under grouped-wider noise as well, but under grouped-shifted noise it falls. The choice of representation has little impact on GP, moving its \aucnorm{} less than repeating the split does on every dataset.

There is a slight consideration with using both \aucnorm{} and predictive performance side-by-side. \aucnorm{} measures the fraction of its own clean $R^2$ that a model keeps, so predictive performance on clean labels can work against it. A model that already predicts poorly has little to lose and often scores a higher \aucnorm, while a model that starts with strong performance has more to lose. The GP and NGBoost are the clearest cases. The GP has among the highest predictive performance on clean labels and a middling \aucnorm, while NGBoost has the highest \aucnorm{} on QM9 from one of the lowest clean $R^2$ values. While NGBoost's predictive performance is highly dependent on its representation, its robustness is not. On QM9 it is both the most robust model and the lowest ranked model for predictive performance up to a noise level of 0.75 with ECFP4. But with PDV it's able to retain some predictive performance under noise, finishing highest across all the models at noise level 1.5.

Upon first glance, SVM does not seem like a standout, however, it is the model whose standing is better with non-Gaussian noise. RF, the GP, NGBoost and SVM are the four models run under every condition except censoring. In Table~\ref{tab:assay_shape}, SVM has the lowest \aucnorm{} of the four under Gaussian noise on LogD and the second lowest on Caco-2 with ECFP4. It rises to first or second under Laplace, Student-$t$ and outlier noise and under grouped-wider noise in all but one case. Gaussian noise is actually the one condition it is worst at. Its \aucnorm{} stays in the middle, partially because it starts high, and it ranks among the five highest for predictive performance at a noise level on every assay dataset under every noise condition.

The feed-forward networks are the most variable, although in general they have some of the highest predictive performance under clean labels and are generally not robust to noise. They are also dependent on the choice of representation. VBLL networks perform poorly on clean labels, are the least robust to noise on LogD, yet are the most robust on Caco-2 and hERG $K_i$. The gradient-boosted trees, LightGBM and XGBoost, were also quite variable, with strong predictive performance on clean labels and poor noise robustness on Caco-2 and hERG $K_i$.

The quantity of noise is an important factor as well. At a noise level of 0.5 the models still rank by predictive performance much as they do on clean labels, while by 1.0 the order has changed, most of all on hERG $K_i$ and then QM9. The order of models by \aucnorm{} is broadly similar across noise conditions, with the forests near the top under each.

% TODO: check (TH lens): eight paragraphs of ranks with no take-home. Close the section with one or two plain sentences, e.g. forests most reliably robust, boosted trees lose most on Caco-2 and hERG Ki, networks vary most.
While it is clear that QSAR model performance drops with label noise, different models drop faster than others. How much noise a dataset tolerates before anything happens is itself a property of the dataset. Up to a noise level of 0.3, three tenths of the spread of the clean labels, the models are largely unaffected by the noise on the HOMO--LUMO gap and on LogD, where every base model with ECFP4 still keeps at least 93\% of its clean $R^2$; on Caco-2 one of the 13 and on hERG $K_i$ four of the 13 are already below 90\% at that level. By a noise level of 0.5 the lowest retained fraction is 90\% on the HOMO--LUMO gap against 76\% on hERG $K_i$, as Figure~\ref{fig:curves_all} shows. Boosted tree ensembles are some of the first to fall. On Caco-2 with ECFP4, LightGBM has among the highest predictive performance on clean labels. It is also the first tree ensemble to fall below 90\% of that performance, at a noise level of 0.5. By level 1.5 it retains 26\% of its original $R^2$, compared to 63\% for RF on ECFP4. XGBoost drops off at the next recorded noise level. However, NGBoost is the opposite, having some of the lowest predictive performance on clean labels and the flattest curve with injected label noise across all models. On the HOMO--LUMO gap it never falls below 90\% of its clean $R^2$ at any level tested. The networks drop at an inconsistent rate. VBLL-$\alpha$ is the earliest to fall on LogD, dropping below 90\% of its clean $R^2$ at a level of 0.5 and reaching 38\% by 1.5. Yet on Caco-2 and hERG $K_i$ it is among the flattest models.

The variance decomposition of predictive performance in Figure~\ref{fig:three_outcomes} and Additional file~\ref{app:noise:sec:variance_intervals} tells us how much the choice of model and representation matters under clean labels. Extending that concept, we are also interested in how these shares change under increasing label noise and compare to the variance decomposition of \aucnorm. The replicate and residuals account for more variance across all datasets. On Caco-2 and hERG $K_i$ the replicate, which on these datasets is one of five scaffold folds, is the largest share of predictive performance before and after noise is injected. Conversely, it falls well below the choice of model family for robustness. On every dataset the interaction between model and representation explains more of the variance in \aucnorm{} than representation alone.

Models are ordered by their rank on the QM9 HOMO--LUMO gap. Ranks are among all 19 models; the six variant models are not drawn.}

\end{figure}

\subsection{Probabilistic and deterministic counterparts}

To further explore the impact of model architecture on noise robustness, we examined deterministic and probabilistic variants of the same core model architecture. The hypothesis was that a model that reports a distribution rather than a point value may be more robust to small amounts of injected label noise. We compared variants across three sets of base models, shown in Figure~\ref{fig:variants_change}: two NNs labeled NN-$\alpha$ and NN-$\beta$ and RF. For each NN, we transform a standard deterministic network into a BNN. The BNNs with a variance head run at different settings from their Bayesian bases, so they are compared only for uncertainty. We compared RF to its probabilistic counterpart, QRF, with the same baseline architecture and hyperparameters. Comparisons of this nature are only possible when the variants carry the same underlying settings, which Additional file~\ref{app:noise:sec:hyperparameters_default} lists. NN-$\alpha$ and BNN-$\alpha$ therefore share one setting, as do NN-$\beta$ and BNN-$\beta$, and QRF is compared with a random forest of the same 300 trees.

% TODO: check (TH lens): RF and QRF share the same trees, so why is QRF less robust? Say what QRF's point prediction is (median of the leaf labels or their mean).
We compare RF and QRF directly with the same underlying architecture. QRF had lower \aucnorm{} on QM9 under Gaussian and grouped-shifted noise on every representation, and changed it little under grouped-wider noise, with almost no change in clean $R^2$. Additional file~\ref{app:noise:sec:rf_vs_qrf_assay} gives the assay datasets. There, QRF was less robust than RF under grouped-shifted noise on every dataset and representation, and under Gaussian noise on LogD and hERG $K_i$. Under grouped-wider noise it was more robust on Caco-2 and mixed elsewhere.

% TODO: check (TH lens): 'only because' is a causal claim; was it shown? If not, hedge it here and in the Conclusions sentence 'only by lowering clean R2'.
The Bayesian transformations that we used to transform feed-forward deterministic NNs into probabilistic ones produced a slightly larger \aucnorm, but only because they lowered predictive performance on clean labels. BNN-$\beta$ kept more \aucnorm{} than NN-$\beta$ in most combinations on every assay dataset, and BNN-$\alpha$ on all assay datasets. On QM9 the Bayesian transformations made no significant change on either predictive performance or \aucnorm{} for either network.

counterpart at the same settings, on every dataset: counterpart minus base, the median of paired differences over 10 replicates
on QM9 and five scaffold folds on the assay datasets. Color is the representation and shape the noise
condition. A filled mark changed in the same direction in every replicate or fold.}
\label{fig:variants_change}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Uncertainty under increasing label noise}\label{sec:uq}

Until now we've discussed how much predictive capabilities a model is able to retain while its training labels are corrupted with noise. Now, we consider how confident the model reports its own predictions are in the presence of noise, and whether or not those estimates match the increased label noise. \citet{Kolmar2021} found that the mean predicted uncertainty derived from the GPs increases with the amount of label noise in the training data. We go beyond the population level and instead ask if a model's per-sample uncertainty tracks label noise. One key sign that a model is able to handle noise is its ability to track it, represented by the per-sample Spearman correlation between predicted uncertainty and noise magnitude.

Table~\ref{tab:uncertainty_rise} shows that, as expected, mean predicted uncertainty is positively correlated with Gaussian label noise for most models and representations. There are a few exceptions, namely BNN-$\beta$ on PDV and VBLL-$\beta$ (het.) on Avalon for QM9. BNN-$\alpha$ fails to rise in at least one fold on every assay dataset, though with PDV only on LogD and Caco-2, and both full-BNNs with a variance head fail on MHG-GNN for hERG $K_i$, and BNN-$\beta$ with a variance head also fails on ChemBERTa and on Caco-2. \citet{Kolmar2021}'s finding therefore holds for almost every probabilistic model on the computed property, and for most of them on measured labels.

\subsubsection{Aleatoric and epistemic attribution}

While this tells us that the models are reportedly less certain under noisy data, we don't know what the model is attributing this uncertainty to. To answer this question, we decompose uncertainty estimates into their aleatoric and epistemic components wherever possible. We expect to see aleatoric uncertainty rise with injected label noise, while the epistemic component should remain relatively constant, as it is the aleatoric component that describes uncertainty in the underlying data. The models in Table~\ref{tab:uncertainty_rise} whose frameworks allowed for this decomposition include full-BNNs with a variance head, the heteroscedastic GP, and QRF. The two heteroscedastic VBLL networks also report both components, and they separate them more often than any other model, though not on every dataset and representation. They are not drawn in the figures, as they were not scored out of fold.

Figure~\ref{fig:decomposition} shows that the full-BNNs with a variance head behave as expected. The epistemic component grows by at most 0.43 of the aleatoric growth in every configuration where the ratio can be computed. Despite QRF's noise robustness, both of its aleatoric and epistemic components rise with noise and on some representations, the epistemic component actually rises at a faster rate.

Unlike the predictive performance and robustness, the representation actually dictates whether decomposition works. For QRF, the epistemic growth is 0.61 to 1.14 times the aleatoric growth on the three fingerprints, 1.02 to 1.44 times on PDV, and 1.29 to 2.04 times on ChemBERTa and MHG-GNN. Under Gaussian noise the heteroscedastic GP keeps its epistemic growth below a quarter of its aleatoric growth on PDV, ChemBERTa and MHG-GNN except MHG-GNN on QM9, but not on ECFP4 under grouped-shifted noise, where the two grow by similar amounts.

The noise conditions follow the pattern found in robustness, the three conditions which alternate the noise distribution show similar decompositions to those under Gaussian noise, while grouped-shifted raises the epistemic share of the heteroscedastic GP and of QRF in every pairing of dataset and representation where they were run. This scaffold family-based offset may be appearing as structure in the data rather than label noise to the models.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth,height=0.72\textheight,keepaspectratio]{F6_decomposition.png}
\caption[Aleatoric and epistemic uncertainty against label noise on QM9.]{Aleatoric and epistemic components of the predicted uncertainty against noise level,
one panel per model, on the QM9 HOMO--LUMO gap with ECFP4 under Gaussian noise. Each value is the mean over the molecules
of one out-of-fold pass; lines are medians over folds and bands their range. A component that is one value
per fit is not drawn (Table~\ref{tab:uncertainty_rise}).}
\label{fig:decomposition}
\end{figure}

The aleatoric component indicates that label noise increased, but Figure~\ref{fig:aleatoric_injected} shows that it doesn't tell us by how much. For each unit of added noise it rises by roughly 0.4 to 1 unit, depending on model, dataset and representation. The aleatoric component can therefore compare noise between datasets or noise levels, but it does not estimate a dataset's internal error.

Uncertainty estimation breaks under censoring as the process of clipping the labels narrows their spread, which in turn shrinks the total uncertainty. None of the models tested treat a clipped label as a noisy one, so they actually grow more confident while losing information.
