\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth,height=0.72\textheight,keepaspectratio]{F6b_aleatoric_against_injected.png}
\caption[Aleatoric uncertainty against the added noise.]{Predicted aleatoric standard deviation against the standard deviation of the added noise,
under Gaussian noise. Rows are PDV (top) and ECFP4 (bottom); columns are the QM9 HOMO--LUMO gap, LogD, hERG $K_i$
and Caco-2. Each line is one model: the median over folds of the mean over the molecules of one out-of-fold
pass, with a band spanning the folds. The dashed line is equality. The heteroscedastic Gaussian process was
not run on QM9 with PDV, and the two heteroscedastic variational networks were not scored out of fold.}
\label{fig:aleatoric_injected}
\end{figure}

\subsubsection{Per-sample uncertainty and label noise}\label{sec:uq_per_sample}

Finally, we ask whether a model's uncertainty on one molecule tracks the noise on that molecule's label. We assess this using the Spearman correlation between predicted uncertainty and the size of the injected noise over out-of-fold training molecules.

Since Gaussian, Laplace, Student-$t$ and grouped-shifted conditions add noise from the same scale to every label, there is not much for uncertainty to find. It's the same situation with outlier noise, under which a tenth of the data is chosen at random for extra noise, and with grouped-wider in which each scaffold family falls in a single fold, so a model makes predictions based on unseen scaffolds and has no basis for estimating uncertainty. The only case where per-sample uncertainty tracking is possible is censoring, as the noise is dependent on the value of the label itself. The correlations are then large and mostly negative, meaning the model is least uncertain on the labels that were clipped most. On QM9 with half the labels clipped, on PDV, they run from $-0.84$ to $-0.80$ across folds for NGBoost and from $-0.47$ to $-0.41$ for the GP, and on LogD with half the labels clipped every fold of every model except the two GPs is at $-0.29$ or lower. We divided each molecule's error by its uncertainty and found that the division ranked the clipped labels better than the error alone on QM9 and on most of LogD. Models are most confident on the clipped labels, probably because the clipping piles many labels into one value, which the model learns to tightly predict. Ironically, per-molecule uncertainty can be used in reverse to identify clipped labels as the labels a model is most certain about are the most likely to have been clipped.

\section{Conclusions}

We investigated how a variety of molecular representations and models perform under increasing label noise. We used ANOVA variance decomposition across three noise conditions on four datasets to show that molecular representation accounts for a larger share of the variance in predictive performance than in robustness. Robustness to label noise, as measured by \aucnorm, the normalized area under the $R^2$ retention curve, is more impacted by the choice of model. The representation each  model is paired with explains more variance than the representation alone. As such, a representation should not be chosen independently of the model it will be used with, and some models benefit from alternative molecular representations across both predictive performance and noise robustness. Model family was the primary factor in every decomposition of \aucnorm{} except one, Caco-2 under grouped-shifted noise, where the scaffold fold took the larger share.

Grouped-shifted and censored noise, the two conditions in which the error is systematic, corrupt QSAR model predictions the most. Changing the shape of the distribution from which noise is drawn barely has an impact on model performance, a conclusion which is in agreement with \citet{Heid2023}.
% TODO: ALIGN: 'made no significant change on QM9': the thesis runs no significance tests, so 'significant' may read as a test. Say 'barely changed' or give the score pair.
Figure~\ref{fig:conditions} shows that low quantities of noise do not make much of an impact. On the HOMO--LUMO gap every base model keeps at least 93\% of its clean $R^2$ up to a noise level of 0.3. Grouped-shifted noise separates from the other conditions only from a level of 0.75. Models' performance begins to degrade more rapidly on the assay datasets than on the HOMO--LUMO gap, which makes sense as assay data contain inherent experimental noise. Tree and kernel models maintained consistent \aucnorm{} across all representations, while the plain NNs and BNNs depended on the representation they were given, as did the predictive performance of NGBoost. Across datasets and noise conditions, RF and QRF were reliably robust, although their predictive performance on clean labels was middling and on Caco-2 they struggled under Gaussian noise. QRF was the slightly less robust of the two, falling below RF under Gaussian and grouped-shifted noise. Other models performed well only under some conditions. The GP had the highest predictive performance on clean labels and under independent noise but fell behind when the noise was shared within a scaffold family, NGBoost predicted well only with the right representation, and SVM led in predictive performance under scaffold-shared noise on some datasets. Transforming a feed-forward network into a Bayesian one increased \aucnorm{} on the assay datasets only by lowering clean $R^2$, and made no significant change on QM9, so switching to a tree-based model is the stronger move under label noise.

% [alignment 2026-10-10: qualifier from the Introduction and Results added]
% TODO: ALIGN: 'which is not necessarily the case for predictive performance or noise robustness' clashes with your first Ch3 conclusion paragraph, which says representation explains a larger share of predictive performance than of robustness. Suggest: 'The success of a split also depends on the representation.'
With regard to uncertainty estimation, mean predicted uncertainty rose with label noise for almost every probabilistic model on QM9 and for the majority of models on the assay datasets. Of the models whose aleatoric and epistemic components can be split per molecule, the BNNs with a variance head, the heteroscedastic GP and the heteroscedastic VBLL networks correctly attributed error from the injected noise as aleatoric, though not on every dataset and representation, while QRF read that error as epistemic. The success of a split is also dependent on the representation, which is not necessarily the case for predictive performance or noise robustness. The aleatoric component rises by roughly 0.4 to 1 unit for each unit of noise added, so it can compare noise between datasets or between noise levels, but it does not measure a dataset's internal error. Outside censoring, no model's per-sample uncertainty identified which labels had been corrupted. Under censoring the models' most confident predictions are the clipped labels themselves, and dividing a molecule's error by its uncertainty picked out the clipped labels better than the error alone.

QM9 served as a relatively clean baseline as its properties are computed. The assay datasets containing experimental measurements on the endpoints LogD, Caco-2 \citep{openadmet}, and hERG $K_i$ \citep{Zdrazil2023} are smaller and cover a more narrow range of the chemical space. As the labels in these data are experimentally-derived and pooled from different laboratories, they contain some unknown amount of noise. We are then adding artificial noise on top of the existing experimental noise. These validation datasets supported the QM9 findings for the forests, with the boosted
trees XGBoost and LightGBM the notable exceptions, degrading on the external datasets. Future work should extend this type of noise robustness benchmarking to classification tasks and larger experimental datasets with better-known uncertainty estimates.

When training on noisy experimental data in the context of QSAR, it is important to keep in mind that a model's robustness to noise is primarily driven by its own mechanism of training, and to an extent the molecular representation the input data comes in, oftentimes with respect to the model. Of the models tested, RF was the most consistent choice across the four datasets, ranking between first and eighth for \aucnorm{} on every dataset, representation and noise condition. Where a predictive distribution is wanted, its probabilistic counterpart QRF maintains RF's predictive performance and nearly all of its noise robustness. What QRF cannot do is say where that uncertainty came from. Beyond the forests, the better choice depends on how much noise there is. LightGBM and XGBoost predict well on clean labels and lose the most as noise rises, so they suit data you trust, while NGBoost and the VBLL networks start lower and hold what they have, except the VBLL networks on LogD.

% TODO: ALIGN: this is a methods aside (Z'-factor, plate effects, confident learning) rather than a takeaway of the chapter. Keep, shorten or move?
In this research, we define a noise level as a fraction of the spread of the clean training labels. It is therefore relative to the dataset rather than an absolute measurement error, and cannot be mapped onto a given dataset exactly. There are other mechanisms used to estimate the noise in a dataset directly. When an assay's controls are available, the Z$'$-factor can be used to estimate noise \citep{Zhang1999}. You can also use screening data to test for row, column and plate effects, which produce systematic rather than independent error \citep{Caraus2015}. When you lack controls, replicates, and metadata, a label can be checked against the data itself. Confident learning, developed for classification, flags labels by comparing each label to the class probabilities a model predicts for that molecule when it was left out of training \citep{Northcutt2021}. You can use the point at which the model error matches the data's error to generate a predictive limit \citep{Kolmar2021, Crusius2024}. Beyond formal methods, you should consider how a dataset was assembled. How many independent replicates were done, how many laboratories contributed, whether assay protocols were consistently applied, and how varied are the compounds in chemical space?

The first limitation of this study is data availability. While HOMO--LUMO gap from QM9 is a good baseline as its computed labels carry no measurement error, it is only a single task. As the noise is meant to replicate experimental data, this analysis would be improved by more quality-controlled, relatively clean and well-replicated experimental data across different targets. However, data of that nature are hard to find publicly.

Second, \aucnorm{} is a ratio to clean $R^2$, so a model that starts from a low clean baseline has less to lose, and a few replicates scored above 1.05. We tried other measures of noise robustness, but they were either too strongly correlated with clean $R^2$ or did not capture the right quantity. The change in $R^2$ at a single noise level, for example, misses the shape of the curve. \aucnorm{} is not a perfect metric, but it describes the whole curve, and across the 13 base models it is barely correlated with clean $R^2$.

Third, it is hard to draw conclusions that hold across so many conditions. No model or representation wins everywhere, although RF comes closest. When working with cheminformatics data, you don't know in advance how a model and representation will perform on the given prediction task. The underlying data is probably noisy, but you don't know how noisy it is or what shape that noise takes.

Given that, our recommendation is to test a handful of models against a handful of representations rather than tuning a single pairing, since on every dataset the pairing explains more of the variance in robustness than the representation does alone. The pattern of the noise should also guide that choice. Under Laplace, Student-$t$ and outlier noise, RF was usually the most robust model and SVM usually ranked second on the assay datasets. NGBoost lost \aucnorm{} under all three, except Laplace noise on LogD. Under grouped-wider noise, QRF was the most robust model and SVM was close behind. NGBoost lost \aucnorm{} on every assay dataset. Grouped-shifted noise was the hardest condition. Every model lost \aucnorm{} on every dataset and representation. RF lost the least of the models that also predicted well, and on the HOMO--LUMO gap the plain networks lost the most. Gaussian noise was the only condition with a clear specialist. NGBoost was the most robust model on the HOMO--LUMO gap under Gaussian noise with every representation, but not on Caco-2 or hERG $K_i$.

These results follow from the loss function each model fits. NGBoost fits a per-molecule scale under a Gaussian likelihood, so it loses ground when the noise is not Gaussian. SVM's loss grows only linearly outside its margin, so large errors cost it less, and it gains under the same conditions. Each boosting stage fits the residuals of the stage before it, and those residuals are largest in the noisy families. The GP is the most affected by error that is correlated with structure, because its kernel measures structure. None of this matters if the data are clean, as on the HOMO--LUMO gap and LogD little separated the models below a noise level of 0.3.

In this chapter each model was given a standard molecular representation, and the choice of representation explained relatively little of the variance in noise robustness. We observe that this choice does impact the performance of different models to varying degrees, and the representations seem to capture different signals on each dataset that we tested. Chapter~\ref{chap:flaws} asks how well these representations compare molecules on a single dataset.

\chapter{Limitations of the similar property principle}\label{chap:flaws}

\section{Introduction}

% [TH edit 2026-10-10: added sentence on why features matter for noisy labels]
The previous chapters have been focused on the impact of label noise. Before we delve into techniques that are meant to train on, identify, and mitigate such label noise, we first focus on a tangential point, molecular similarity. All of the aforementioned noise-adjacent methods, as well as the QSAR modeling itself, rest on the similar property principle (SPP) of Section~\ref{sec:mol_similarity}. The SPP posits that structurally similar compounds display similar chemical properties \citep{Johnson1990}. This serves as the conceptual foundation for QSAR modeling. The concept of molecular similarity is also used in various contexts to compare neighboring molecules. This comes into play when identifying noisy molecules that in theory stick out from their neighbors. A label is judged against the labels of its nearest molecules, so the features that decide which molecules are nearest also decide which labels look noisy. There are some important exceptions to the SPP, namely activity cliffs (ACs) \citep{Stumpfe2012, vanTilborg2022}. Furthermore, there is no single way to define similarity between compounds. This is where the principle begins to break down in practice. When defining similarity between two molecules, we typically rely on some pairwise mathematical function, or coefficient, that takes in two molecular representations as input. Two factors vary here, the representation and the coefficient. \citet{Bajusz2015} compared eight coefficients and found Tanimoto, Dice, Cosine and Soergel the best and largely equivalent, while similarities derived from Euclidean and Manhattan distances were not recommended on their own. In this chapter we ask if the standard methods of comparing molecules satisfy the SPP, that is, whether they quantify how similar two molecules actually are.

% TODO: check (TH lens): ECFP can include stereochemistry; it is off by default. Say 'in their default settings', matching the corrected sentence in the ties section.
General-purpose molecular representations are designed to work across chemical domains. Consequently, when considering data from a specific domain, many of their features show little to no variance. Some representations assign separate molecules identical feature vectors. Since common fingerprints do not encode stereochemistry \citep{orsi2024map4c}, they are often unable to differentiate stereoisomers, which can have substantially different measured activity \citep{Nguyen2006}. As these coefficients compare every feature, irrelevant features dilute the result. Different representations also disagree about which molecules are close based on the information they carry. 

The problem of whether neighboring molecules can predict chemical activity has been measured before. The modelability index of \citet{Golbraikh2014} scores a binary dataset by a class-weighted share of nearest-neighbor pairs that share an activity class, producing a single value for how strongly the similarity assumption holds on that particular combination of representation and data. It is not clear whether a low value is due to the data being intrinsically hard to model or to the representation not capturing adequate features for the task. Other work in this area relates to activity cliffs. \citet{Sheridan2020} compared experimental error, activity distribution and activity-cliff metrics as predictors of how well a dataset can be modeled, and found the cliff metrics the strongest of the three. However, they found that these metrics cannot separate real cliffs from those produced by uncertain activity measurements. \citet{MedinaFranco2013} points out that some of these activity cliffs are artifacts of the chosen representation, similarity threshold, or noise in the data. Activity cliffs are distinct from noise, as they carry some of the most valuable information about the interaction between structure and activity. None of these measures ask specifically about whether the specific features within these representations matter for the property at hand. 

% [TH edit 2026-10-10: added three sentences: task-agnostic vs task-learned representations]
We first consider how much the representation impacts similarity measures. \citet{Riniker2013} benchmarked 12 fingerprints and two simple baseline fingerprints for similarity-based virtual screening on 88 targets from three public collections, and found that the screening performance of the 12 fingerprints was highly correlated. Only the two baselines differed significantly from the rest. Nearly all of 25 pretrained neural embeddings were found to have a negligible improvement over ECFP across 25 datasets; only CLAMP was significantly better \citep{Praski2025}. These comparisons, like this thesis, use representations fixed before the task is known, such as ECFP or a frozen pretrained embedding. Such a representation was never tuned to the property, so it need not give a smooth property landscape. We would expect a representation learned on the task's labels, such as a network's last hidden layer, to give a smoother one, in which the remaining cliffs are more likely real. However, when looking into the differences between the structure of fingerprints, \citet{Huber2026} found that fingerprints which store raw counts are more effective at defining similarity than folded fingerprints when compared against a graph-based structural reference. Folding introduces bit collisions, which disproportionately distort similarity for molecules with many set bits \citep{Huber2026}. ACs follow the same pattern: across 15 configurations of representation and similarity measures on 3 datasets, the representations disagreed about which pairs form cliffs \citep{DabrowskiTumanski2026}, supporting the point \citet{MedinaFranco2013} makes about ACs being artifacts of their representation. 

Any method that incorporates similarity measures, such as AC detection, or cleaning a training set by comparing each sample's label with its nearest neighbors' labels, whether by removing the samples that disagree \citep{Wilson1972} or by smoothing their labels, inherits this choice, as do the noise estimation and mitigation methods of this thesis. \citet{Coupry2022} learn a molecular similarity from graph embeddings, but independently of any endpoint. \citet{Zheng2000} did choose the similarity for the task. They predict each molecule's activity as the average over its $k$ most similar molecules by Euclidean distance over descriptors, and choose the descriptors by simulated annealing to maximize the leave-one-out $q^2$. \citet{Itskowitz2005} also optimize the number of neighbors and their weighting. Both select features for a nearest-neighbor model of one endpoint, whereas this thesis selects features across several representations.

The purpose of this chapter is to establish the problem that Chapter~\ref{chap:method} sets out to solve. Section~\ref{sec:flaws:representations} asks, for a given dataset, how much the features of standard representations vary and how many molecules map to identical feature vectors. Section~\ref{sec:flaws:similarity} asks what happens when these features are used to quantify similarity, and Section~\ref{sec:flaws:consequences} follows the consequences for methods built on these similarity measures, including a small sweep of published noise-handling methods.

\subsection{Distance metrics for continuous molecular descriptors}\label{sec:flaws:methods}

While binary fingerprints are often compared with the Tanimoto coefficient introduced in Chapter~\ref{chap:background}, we need separate distance metrics for continuous feature vectors. The small sweep of published noise-handling methods in Section~\ref{sec:flaws:consequences} used the following four:

\begin{itemize}
    \item Euclidean ($L_2$): standard geometric distance, $d(x, y) = \sqrt{\sum_i (x_i - y_i)^2}$
    \item Manhattan ($L_1$): sum of absolute differences, $d(x, y) = \sum_i |x_i - y_i|$, more robust to outlier features
    \item Cosine: angle-based, $d(x, y) = 1 - \frac{x \cdot y}{\|x\| \|y\|}$, invariant to magnitude
    \item Mahalanobis: covariance-adjusted distance, $d(x,y)=\sqrt{(x-y)^\top S^{-1}(x-y)}$, where $S$ is the feature covariance estimated on the training molecules \citep{Mahalanobis1936}
\end{itemize}

% [TH edit 2026-10-10: added the standardization formula]
All features are standardized to zero mean and unit variance prior to distance computation. Each value $x$ of a feature is replaced by $(x-\mu)/s$, where $\mu$ and $s$ are that feature's mean and standard deviation over the molecules.

\section{Results}

\subsection{Flaws in standard representations}\label{sec:flaws:representations}

General-purpose representations are built to span a broad range of chemical domains. When a dataset covers only a small region of the chemical space, a lot of the features within these generalized representations become irrelevant, describing chemistry which is absent from the data at hand. Pairwise comparisons of these representations rely on these irrelevant features.

\subsubsection{Constant and near-constant features}\label{sec:flaws:constant} 

% TODO: check (TH lens): 'single representations' and 'augmentation features' (next section) are used before the hybrid is explained. Define them here without the hybrid, e.g. the representations Chapter 5 selects features from.
To investigate similarity estimation, we first look at a simple measure: how many features vary within a dataset. A single dataset often contains a set of relatively similar molecules. Many features within a generalized representation contain information that describes all, or almost all, of the molecules in the set, failing to capture the variation between them. We define a constant feature to be one that takes a single value in every molecule of a dataset and a near-constant feature to be one that carries the same value in at least 95\% of the molecules. We counted constant and near-constant features from a set of representations described in Table~\ref{tab:dataset_reps} across hERG $K_i$, Caco-2, LogD, QM9, MDR1--MDCK efflux, human liver microsome (HLM) clearance, rat liver microsome (RLM) clearance and the Lhasa hERG blocker training set. MDR1--MDCK efflux and the two clearance datasets come from Biogen \citep{fang2023}. HLM clearance and the Lhasa hERG set are not in Table~\ref{tab:dataset_reps}; their single representations are those with counts in Additional file~\ref{app:add:constant_features}.

% TODO: check (TH lens): say these are frozen pretrained networks that never saw these labels, so the reader knows they are task-agnostic.
% TODO: check (TH lens): Chapter 3 has 1,415 hERG Ki, 5,039 LogD and 2,161 Caco-2 molecules; here 1,412, 5,028 and 2,158. Say why they differ, as the old draft did.
% [TH edit 2026-10-10: added off/on split (counted from rep_cache/herg_ki/ecfp4.npy, no saved script)]
The substructure-based fingerprints have the most near-constant features. Figure~\ref{fig:constant_features} and Additional file~\ref{app:add:constant_features} show that the learned embeddings, which are taken from the last layers of a NN, have none except MHG-GNN. ECFP4 is the worst offender. On hERG $K_i$ (1,412 molecules) 1,815 of the 2,048 features are near-constant. That jumps to 1,997 out of 2,048 near-constant features for the HOMO--LUMO gap on QM9. Almost all of these bits are near-constant because they are absent. On hERG $K_i$, 1,812 of the 1,815 near-constant bits are off in at least 95\% of molecules, and the other 3 are on in at least 95\%. 292 of MHG-GNN's 1,024 features are zero in every molecule across all seven datasets that used it. They come directly from the saved encoder output rather than the data, and the cause was not investigated.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth,height=0.72\textheight,keepaspectratio]{figures_code_input/constant_features.pdf}
\caption[Shares of constant, near-constant and varying features per representation.]{Shares of constant (dark blue), near-constant (mid blue) and varying (gray) features of each representation on eight datasets. The number of features of each representation is in parentheses.}
\label{fig:constant_features}
\end{figure}

% [TH edit 2026-10-10: opening reworded to 'depends on the distance metric'; added the 1 -> 4.36 arithmetic and a take-home sentence]
Which count matters depends on the distance metric, and some metrics ignore constant features. Tanimoto similarity, defined in Equation~\ref{eq:tanimoto} with distance $1-T$, ignores bits off in both molecules, but a bit on in every molecule raises every molecule's measured similarity. It does not use the 105 constant ECFP4 bits on hERG $K_i$, which are all off. Constant features add zero to a Euclidean or Manhattan distance; however, near-constant features are amplified. Standardization, described in Section~\ref{sec:flaws:methods}, turns a bit that is on in a share $p$ of the molecules into $\sqrt{(1-p)/p}$ where it is on. A bit on in half of the molecules becomes 1, a bit on in 5\% becomes 4.36, and a bit on in one of the 1,412 hERG $K_i$ molecules becomes 37.56. Two molecules that differ only in that rare bit are therefore far apart, although they differ in one substructure. That is useful if those features define an AC. If not, they are contributing disproportionately to the measure of similarity. In short, the features that vary least between molecules carry the most weight in a standardized distance.

\subsubsection{Molecules with identical feature vectors}\label{sec:flaws:ties}

A standard fingerprint- or descriptor-based representation can produce the same feature vector for two separate molecules. In turn, models using this representation will produce a single prediction for the molecules with identical features. Identical feature vectors do not always mean that the representation has merged two different molecules, since a dataset pooled from several sources can hold two measurements of the same molecule.

% [TH edit 2026-10-10: stereo sentence corrected: 2D graphs can carry stereo labels; 'more than that' replaced and WL atom labels explained]
Many fingerprints are computed without stereochemistry \citep{orsi2024map4c}. In this research, ECFP4 is built with stereochemistry off, and the 2D descriptor sets (PDV, Mordred) also ignore it. So, as Table~\ref{tab:flaws:ties} shows, almost every molecule they produce identical representations for is a stereoisomer of another. A 2D graph can store stereochemistry as labels on atoms and bonds, but these representations do not read those labels, so they merge stereoisomers. Additionally, methods using the Weisfeiler--Lehman relabeling scheme are bounded by the 1-Weisfeiler--Lehman test described in Chapter~\ref{chap:background}, so some non-isomorphic graphs also receive the same vector. This is seen in the graph-kernel features which tie 105 of 1,412 hERG $K_i$ molecules, 56 of which differ only in stereochemistry and 49 of which are different 2D structures. As Table~\ref{tab:reps_smallmol} records, these Weisfeiler--Lehman features label each atom by its element only, ignore bond orders, and keep only labels found in at least 5\% of molecules. 

Whether or not a learned embedding is able to differentiate stereoisomers is dependent on its input as well as its individual implementation. SMI-TED, a pretrained SMILES encoder, ties 56 hERG $K_i$, 633 LogD and 177 Caco-2 molecules, all stereoisomers. A tokenizer can drop stereochemistry prior to training. Additional file~\ref{app:add:constant_features} calls the ChemBERTa-77M-MTR checkpoint ChemBERTa-2, and Chapter~\ref{chap:robustness} calls it ChemBERTa. Its tokenizer gives 72 of the 1,415 hERG $K_i$ molecules of Chapter~\ref{chap:robustness} the same token sequence as another molecule. Since stereoisomers can differ in measured activity \citep{Nguyen2006}, these representations lack potentially useful chemical information. 

% [TH edit 2026-10-10: names the four kinds of tie]
Two molecules are considered to be tied when a representation produces the same feature vector for both. Table~\ref{tab:flaws:ties} counts ties on hERG $K_i$, LogD and Caco-2 for each dataset's single representations and for its augmentation features. These are the small feature sets added to the hybrid after it is built, described in Section~\ref{sec:method:augmentation}. A tie has one of four causes. It can be a repeat of one compound, with the same full InChIKey, which includes stereochemistry. It can be a failed featurization, giving all zeros. It can be a merge of stereoisomers, which share the first InChIKey block, encoding connectivity only. Otherwise it is a merge of different structures. No tie was a repeat or a row of zeros.

% TODO: check (TH lens): ChemBERTa ties 72 molecules but ChemBERT ties none; say they are different models (256 vs 384 features). Also, are the 72 ChemBERTa ties all stereoisomers?
Apart from the MACCS keys, most tied molecules are stereoisomers. Atom pairs, PDV, Mordred and SMI-TED tie only stereoisomers, and ECFP4, Avalon and topological torsions add a few molecules that differ in more than their stereochemistry. On hERG $K_i$, ECFP4, atom pairs, PDV, Mordred and SMI-TED tie the same 56 molecules, so joining any of these five representations leaves all 56 tied. The WL graph kernel also ties 49 hERG $K_i$ and 26 Caco-2 molecules that are not stereoisomers, and the MACCS keys tie the most molecules on every dataset, mostly not stereoisomers. The 3D augmentation features and the learned embeddings ChemBERT, GROVER, MHG-GNN, MoLFormer and GraphMVP tie none, and joining all of a dataset's representations leaves no molecule tied.

% [TH edit 2026-10-10: added which labels and which molecules the bound uses]
A model has to give every molecule in a tied group the same prediction. The best single prediction for a group is its mean label, so ties set an error that no model can go below,
\begin{equation}
\mathrm{RMSE}_{\min} = \sqrt{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{G}\sum_{i \in G}\left(y_i - \bar{y}_G\right)^2},
\end{equation}
where $n$ is the number of molecules, $G$ runs over the tied groups and $\bar{y}_G$ is the mean label of group $G$. The matching best $R^2$ is $1 - \mathrm{RMSE}_{\min}^2 / \mathrm{Var}(y)$. Both use the measured labels of all molecules in the dataset, with no split into training and test molecules. They therefore bound the error over the whole dataset, not on one held-out fold. On hERG $K_i$ this lowest error is at most $0.134$ p$K_i$ (MACCS keys), small next to the label standard deviation of $0.915$ p$K_i$.

Single groups can still disagree by a lot. Independent p$K_i$ measurements of the same compound against the same target differ by $0.44$ log units on average, with a standard deviation of $0.54$ \citep{Kramer2012}, so a gap larger than $0.54$ inside a tied group is larger than typical measurement noise. On hERG $K_i$, 6 of the 26 ECFP4 groups and 38 of the 153 MACCS groups differ by more than $0.54$. Figure~\ref{fig:flaws:stereo_pair} shows the widest ECFP4 group, a pair of stereoisomers $1.14$ p$K_i$ apart. An ECFP4 model can at best predict their mean, $5.59$ p$K_i$, which leaves each molecule $0.57$ p$K_i$ from its measured value.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth,height=0.72\textheight,keepaspectratio]{figures_code_input/herg_ki_ecfp4_stereo_pair.pdf}
\caption[Two hERG $K_i$ stereoisomers with identical ECFP4 vectors.]{Two hERG $K_i$ stereoisomers with identical ECFP4 vectors, which differ at the carbon bearing the amide nitrogen (\textit{R} in A, \textit{S} in B). The top row shows the structures with their measured p$K_i$ values, and the bottom row shows the 55 of 2,048 ECFP4 bits set in both.}
\label{fig:flaws:stereo_pair}
\end{figure}

% [TH edit 2026-10-10: SALI inside a tied group: gap may be stereo effect or measurement spread]
A tied pair with different labels is also the most extreme activity cliff possible. The structure--activity landscape index of molecules $i$ and $j$,
\begin{equation}
\mathrm{SALI}_{ij} = \frac{|y_i - y_j|}{1 - \mathrm{sim}(i,j)}
\end{equation}
\citep{Guha2008}, is infinite when their similarity is 1 and their labels differ, and here the infinite index is made by the representation. The label gap itself may be a real effect of stereochemistry, a gap between repeat measurements of the size given above, or both, and the index cannot separate them. A method that judges a label by its nearest neighbors sees a tied neighbor at distance 0 with a different label, and may flag a correct label as noise. Section~\ref{sec:flaws:neighbors} returns to this case.

\subsection{Flaws in similarity and other pairwise computations}\label{sec:flaws:similarity} 

Section~\ref{sec:flaws:representations} described some of the limitations of standard representations. Here we ask what happens when two molecules are compared through one of them, as the pairwise comparisons described in Chapter~\ref{chap:background} can violate the SPP.

The Tanimoto coefficient is the most popular similarity metric in cheminformatics \citep{LopezPerez2024}, and is commonly computed on ECFPs, although the coefficient applies to any bit vector. It has been described as the ``coefficient of choice'' for molecular similarity \citep{Willett2006}, while the Morgan fingerprint, ECFP4 in this research, ranks among the strongest fingerprints for virtual screening and target prediction \citep{Capecchi2020}. ECFP4 and ECFP6 specifically, alongside topological torsion, rank among the best of 28 fingerprints tested for ordering diverse structures by similarity \citep{OBoyle2016}. However, it is not a one-size-fits-all solution. Across 96,456 human metabolites, 72.9\% had an ECFP4 nearest neighbor at Tanimoto distance 0 \citep{Capecchi2020}. Stereochemistry had been removed before that comparison, so these are constitutionally different molecules that ECFP4 cannot tell apart. 

% [TH edit 2026-10-10: qualifier for task-learned representations]
A similarity coefficient cannot tell you which features matter to the prediction task at hand, unless the representation itself was trained on that task's labels. And the SPP holds only relative to such a measure. To understand the impact of irrelevant features on similarity measures, we make the following computation. First, we categorize a representation's features into those relevant to the task, $R$, and those irrelevant to the task, $I$, and take the squared Euclidean distance as a sum over both:

\begin{equation}
d^2(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}) = \sum_{j \in R}(x_j-y_j)^2 + \sum_{j \in I}(x_j-y_j)^2 .
\label{eq:distance_split}
\end{equation}

Every non-constant irrelevant feature adds one further term, so the irrelevant portion grows linearly with $|I|$. After standardization each feature contributes the same amount on average over all pairs, so the share of the distance carrying signal is about $|R|/(|R|+|I|)$. When we use a general-purpose representation like ECFP4 on a dataset spanning a small portion of the chemical space, $|I|$ can be a lot larger than $|R|$. Constant features contribute nothing, while near-constant features have a strong impact. The worst-case scenario involves a single rare bit set in only a handful of molecules. After standardization, uncommon bits take on larger values, outweighing common features that may be more relevant. If this bit is irrelevant to the task at hand, the similarity measure suffers. 

Larger representations compound the problem. As the number of features grows, the nearest and farthest molecules end up at nearly the same distance, meaning that these pairwise distances are less able to discriminate, as explained in Chapter~\ref{chap:background}. Two measures of this effect are the relative contrast, defined as the difference between the largest and smallest measured distance divided by the smallest \citep{Aggarwal2001}, and hubness, a measure of how unevenly the molecules appear in other molecules' nearest neighbors \citep{Radovanovic2010}. These errors propagate along larger data pipelines, starting from the representation into similarity, from similarity into the set of neighbors a molecule is compared against, and from there into a prediction of some kind. 

% TODO: check (TH lens): the ECFP4 and descriptor columns use different metrics; say both are Spearman correlations, so scale does not matter, but a column difference mixes representation and metric.
The four measurements below use the six small-molecule regression datasets of Table~\ref{tab:dataset_reps} and HLM clearance. As described in Section~\ref{sec:flaws:methods}, binary representations were compared by Tanimoto distance, and all others by Manhattan distance on standardized features, with constant features removed.

\subsubsection{Activity cliffs}
Chapter~\ref{chap:background} gives the MoleculeACE definition of an activity cliff, which combines three similarity measures. Here we use one. Two molecules are similar if their ECFP4 Tanimoto similarity is at least 0.55 \citep{Hu2015}, and a molecule is a cliff if a similar molecule has a label that differs from its own by more than a set gap. We chose the gap on each dataset so that 15\% of the molecules are cliffs, so the gap follows the spread of the labels. Table~\ref{tab:flaws:distance_summary} gives the cliffs for each dataset. On hERG $K_i$ the gap is 1.76 p$K_i$, and 211 of the 1,412 molecules are cliffs. On QM9 only 1,093 pairs of the 9,978 molecules are similar, and the gap is 0.05~eV, 0.06 times the median absolute deviation of the labels. Because the gap is set by a share of molecules, on QM9 a cliff marks a small label difference, not a cliff in the usual sense. On the three Biogen datasets (RLM clearance, MDR1--MDCK efflux and HLM clearance) it is 0.20 to 0.34 log units, 0.50 to 0.60 times the median absolute deviation.

% [TH edit 2026-10-10: take-home sentence added]
The count of cliffs depends on the representation. We kept each dataset's gap and replaced ECFP4 with each other single representation, calling similar the same number of closest pairs as ECFP4 does. On hERG $K_i$ the number of cliff molecules then ranges from 138 (atom pairs) to 267 (WL graph kernel). Of the pairs that ECFP4 calls similar, only 57\% to 74\% are also among another representation's closest pairs, and on QM9 only 1\% to 24\%. Section~\ref{app:add:flaws} of the Additional files gives these overlaps. So which molecules count as cliffs depends on the representation, not only on the labels.

\subsubsection{Distance and label difference}
We asked whether distance tracks label difference in two ways. Over all pairs of molecules, we took the Spearman correlation between the distance of a pair and the absolute difference of its labels, a one-number summary of the neighborhood-behavior plot of \citet{Patterson1996}; datasets above 2,000 molecules were sampled down to 2,000. Over each molecule's closest pairs only, we predicted each label as the mean label of the molecule's five nearest neighbors, taken from other scaffold groups, and took the Spearman correlation between predicted and measured labels.

% [TH edit 2026-10-10: says where 70 comes from; take-home sentences added]
Across the 70 combinations of dataset and representation (seven datasets, ten single representations each), the all-pairs correlation ranged from $-0.04$ to 0.23 and was below 0.10 in 44 of them. The five-neighbor prediction is a correlation between predicted and measured labels. Table~\ref{tab:flaws:distance_summary} shows that it ranged from 0.29 to 0.89, and Section~\ref{app:add:flaws} of the Additional files gives every representation. On hERG $K_i$ with ECFP4, the all-pairs correlation is 0.06, while the five-neighbor prediction reaches 0.72, with an RMSE of 0.63 p$K_i$ against a label standard deviation of 0.92 p$K_i$.

% [TH edit 2026-10-10: take-home sentences added, incl. ECFP4 vs descriptors]
Figure~\ref{fig:flaws:herg_pairs} shows why the two differ. Of the 996,166 hERG $K_i$ pairs, 93\% have an ECFP4 Tanimoto similarity below 0.20, and only 1.1\% reach 0.55. Over all pairs the median label difference is 0.81 p$K_i$, but above a similarity of about 0.4 it is 0.20 to 0.30 p$K_i$, although 8.3\% of the 774 pairs at 0.8 or above still differ by more than 1 p$K_i$. Mordred shows the same pattern: the closest 5\% of pairs have a median difference of 0.44 p$K_i$, and every other 5\% has 0.78 to 1.11 p$K_i$. The useful information sits in the few closest pairs. The all-pairs correlation is set by the other 95\%, whereas the five-neighbor prediction uses only the closest pairs. So the SPP holds for each molecule's closest neighbors but not across all pairs. Neither ECFP4 nor the descriptor set follows it better on every dataset. Both reach 0.72 on hERG $K_i$. The descriptors are higher on LogD, Caco-2 and the three Biogen datasets, and ECFP4 is higher on QM9.

% [TH edit 2026-10-10: take-home sentence added]
\subsubsection{Agreement on neighbors}
We compared the five nearest neighbors of each molecule, from other scaffold groups, under ECFP4, SMI-TED and Mordred (PDV on Caco-2, where Mordred is not a single representation; on HLM clearance ECFP4 is not a single representation and its stored features were used). On hERG $K_i$ any two of the three share on average 0.47 to 0.52 of each molecule's five neighbors. On the three Biogen datasets and QM9 they share only 0.13 to 0.21, as Table~\ref{tab:flaws:neighbor_overlap} shows. So a neighbor-based method that switches representation compares each label with a largely different set of neighbors.

% TODO: check (TH lens): open with one line on why MCS: it does not depend on any fingerprint, so it is a structural reference to check ECFP4 against.
% [TH edit 2026-10-10: take-home sentence added]
\subsubsection{Agreement with shared structure}
For 500 random pairs per dataset we found the maximum common substructure (MCS) with RDKit \citep{rdkit}, allowing 2 seconds per pair and leaving out pairs that ran out of time. The MCS distance is $1 - n/(a+b-n)$, where $n$ is the number of atoms in the common substructure and $a$ and $b$ are the numbers of heavy atoms in the two molecules. Its Spearman correlation with ECFP4 Tanimoto distance ranged from 0.20 (hERG $K_i$ and HLM clearance) to 0.51 (Caco-2 and LogD). ECFP4 distance therefore agrees only partly with how much structure two molecules share.

% TODO: check (TH lens): a method does not inherit a flaw, it is negatively impacted by it; same wording in the Introduction ('inherits this choice').
% [TH edit 2026-10-10: section take-home added]
In short, close molecules do tend to have close labels, but over all pairs distance barely follows label difference. Representations also disagree on which molecules are close and which are cliffs. Every method in Section~\ref{sec:flaws:consequences} that takes a similarity as input inherits the dilution described here.

\subsection{Consequences for similarity-based methods}\label{sec:flaws:consequences}

% [TH edit 2026-10-10: opening sentence replaced by a recap of the three flaws]
So far this chapter has shown three flaws, each with its own origin. Most features of a general-purpose representation barely vary on one dataset, because the representation was built for all of chemistry. Several representations give different molecules, mostly stereoisomers, the same feature vector, because they ignore stereochemistry. A distance sums over every feature, so over all pairs it barely follows label difference. Representations also disagree about which molecules are neighbors. While this calls into question the predictive limits of QSAR and other structure-based modeling, it also calls into question downstream applications of molecular similarity. Nearest-neighbor methods, where a molecule's neighbors, as measured by similarity, are the inputs to several other processes, are by definition sensitive to the quality of similarity measurements. They are used within other processes like predicting a molecule's activity using its neighbors' labels, detecting outliers, either ACs or noise, defining an applicability domain by distance to the training set \citep{Tropsha2010}, grouping molecules into clusters for data splits \citep{Butina1999}, identifying activity cliffs, and, in federated learning, weighting each site's vote on a molecule by that molecule's distance to the site's own data \citep{Hanser2025}. If this measure of similarity, or equivalently distance, is flawed, then all of these downstream applications will feel the effects of it.

Below, we report a preliminary sweep we ran over 12 noise-related methods. Those that relied on pairwise distance measurements reported the lowest retention of predictive performance under increasing label noise. 

\subsubsection{A small sweep of published noise-handling methods}\label{sec:lnl_motivation}

It is established in Chapter~\ref{chap:robustness} that model family explains the largest share of variance in noise robustness, while representation explains at most 8.1\% on QM9. 

Here, we review a small sweep of published LNL methods, many of which were originally built for image classification with flipped labels \citep{Song2022}, so they do not transfer automatically to continuous labels or cheminformatics domains. We explored various adaptations to get them to fit the tasks at hand and swept various settings relating to distance computation, loss functions, and use of uncertainty estimates. We originally sought out to ask whether training methods built for noisy labels improve robustness and whether representation still matters under them. 

The study tested 12 published LNL methods on the QM9 HOMO--LUMO gap (10,000 molecules, scaffold splits), adding Gaussian noise with $\sigma$ from 0 to 1.0 in steps of 0.1 to the training labels only. The noise was added to the raw gap in eV before the labels were standardized. Its $\sigma$ is therefore in eV, not the noise level of Section~\ref{sec:noise_injection}. The representations were ECFP4, Sort \& Slice \citep{sns}, PDV, canonical SMILES and randomized SMILES. Depending on the method, a configuration can also add the model's own uncertainty estimate or a pairwise distance measure such as Tanimoto, Euclidean, Manhattan, cosine or Mahalanobis. Other methods added an alternative GP kernel or loss function, for 221 configurations in all. Four of the methods attempt to identify noisy labels, smooth or remove them, and retrain on the updated set. These methods include small-loss selection, confident learning, distance-based selection and multistage cleaning. The other eight involve training methodologies that take noise into account (noise-robust training). Table~\ref{tab:flaws:lnl_methods} lists the 12 methods and how each uses a distance between molecules.


\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering
\small
\begin{adjustbox}{max width=\textwidth,max totalheight=0.85\textheight}
\begin{tabular}{@{}P{0.19\linewidth}P{0.13\linewidth}P{0.38\linewidth}P{0.24\linewidth}@{}}
\toprule
Method & Category & Key adaptation & Use of a distance \\
\midrule
Meta-Weight-Net & Meta-learning & Input extended to the loss and the uncertainty; weights updated on the validation set every 5 epochs & None \\
Small-loss selection & Sample selection & One-time selection of the 70\% of molecules with the smallest squared error & Optional: removes molecules far from the selected ones \\
Confident learning & Sample selection & Flags molecules with high error (above the 85th percentile) and low uncertainty (below the 25th) & Optional: also flags outliers by distance \\
Distance-based selection & Sample selection & Removes the 15\% of molecules that are the strongest outliers by molecular similarity & Always: the selection is by distance \\
MentorNet & Curriculum & Online mentor, not pre-trained; input is the loss, its last 5 values and the uncertainty & Optional: distance to the 10 nearest neighbors as a further input \\
Uncertainty curriculum & Curriculum & Staged inclusion by uncertainty, 25\%, 50\%, 75\% then 100\%, after a 20-epoch warmup & None \\
DivideMix & Iterative refinement & Squared-error loss, uncertainty-weighted mixture separation, simplified co-teaching & None \\
Multistage cleaning & Iterative refinement & Removes 10\% per stage over 3 stages, scored by normalized loss plus normalized uncertainty & None \\
Early learning & Iterative refinement & Reweights molecules across epochs by their loss and uncertainty, with geometric decay & None \\
Contrast-to-divide & Iterative refinement & Selects molecules whose predictions vary least when the input is perturbed & Optional: similarity between molecules in the consistency score \\
Heteroscedastic GP & GP extension & A GP (Tanimoto, RBF or Mat\'ern kernel) for the mean and a network for the noise variance, trained jointly & Through the kernel only \\
Evidential kernel & GP extension & Multitask GP predicting the four normal-inverse-gamma parameters & Through the kernel only \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{adjustbox}
\caption[Methods of the exploratory study of noise-handling methods.]{The 12 published noise-handling methods of the exploratory study, adapted for regression on the QM9 HOMO--LUMO gap. ``Optional'' marks a distance that some configurations added and others did not.}
\label{tab:flaws:lnl_methods}
\end{table}


% TODO: check (TH lens): how many of the 221 configurations used a distance at all? For the four optional-distance methods, the clean test is the same method with and without it; if not done, say so.
% [TH edit 2026-10-10: says what the percentage measures; the sigma behind 86.7/84.2/82.7 still needs you]
We measured noise robustness by retention, $R^2$ under noise divided by $R^2$ with clean labels, as a percentage, so a retention of 86.7\% means the model kept 86.7\% of the $R^2$ it reached at $\sigma = 0$. While later research in this thesis uses \aucnorm{}, the area under the retention curve, this sweep predates that change in evaluation metric.
 Retention correlates too closely with clean $R^2$, so it struggles to separate the change in predictive performance from its magnitude. In an analysis of variance over the adapted components, using a distance explained the most variance in $R^2$. All 20 configurations with the lowest retention used a distance, and none of these 20 reached both a clean $R^2$ above 0.6 and a retention above 80\%.

% TODO: check (TH lens): 'even less' than what? Give the share explained by representation in the sweep next to Chapter 3's 8.1%.
The top-performing methods relied on estimating noise from the model's own loss or uncertainty. DivideMix \citep{li2020dividemix} retained the most, 86.7\% (standard deviation 0.4\% across its configurations), followed by small-loss selection \citep{Han2018} at 84.2\% (2.1\%) and the uncertainty curriculum, which adds molecules in order of the model's own predicted uncertainty \citep{Bengio2009}, at 82.7\% (3.5\%). The heteroscedastic GP, a GP whose noise is predicted per molecule by a second model \citep{kersting2007most}, retained 79.0\% at $\sigma = 0.6$, averaged over 15 configurations, with $R^2$ of 0.71 on clean labels and 0.56 at that level. The exploratory study does not record the noise level for the other methods, so this value cannot be ranked against theirs. Regularization alone retained less, 73.0\% for Mixup and 74.0\% for sharpness-aware minimization. Under these methods, the choice of representation explained even less variance than in Chapter~\ref{chap:robustness}.

% TODO: CHECK the new paragraph below on which methods use a distance: the retention of each distance-using method on its own is not in the confirmation report, and the raw sweep results are not on this machine, so the paragraph below names the methods but gives no per-method numbers.
We now outline which of the 12 methods use a distance between molecules, as listed in Table~\ref{tab:flaws:lnl_methods}. Distance-based selection always does, as it removes the molecules that are the strongest outliers by molecular similarity. Four more methods add a distance only in some configurations. Small-loss selection uses it to remove molecules far from the selected ones, and confident learning uses it to flag further outliers. MentorNet takes the distance to the 10 nearest neighbors as an extra input, and contrast-to-divide adds the similarity between molecules to its consistency score. The two GP extensions, the heteroscedastic GP and the evidential kernel, compare molecules only through their kernel. The remaining five methods use no distance at all. As stated above, every one of the 20 configurations with the lowest retention used a distance, while the three methods that retained the most do not need one.

There are various reasons why the methods relying on distance failed. First, a single representation may lack the information a distance needs, with and possibly even without noise. PDVs encode global molecular properties and ECFP4 contains substructure information, and any one of these sources alone may not be sufficient for robust similarity estimation. Second, some representations are sparse, with many features irrelevant to the molecules of a given dataset, as explained in Section~\ref{sec:flaws:similarity}. We sought to address the second with the adaptive hybrid representations in Chapter~\ref{chap:method}.

\subsubsection{Noise detection by neighbors}\label{sec:flaws:neighbors}

Neighbor-based noise detection uses a sample's neighbors to determine whether or not that sample's label is an outlier, an idea that goes back to editing nearest-neighbor training sets \citep{Wilson1972}. As discussed in Chapter~\ref{chap:background}, activity cliffs are outliers in the context of neighbor-based detection, but are not noise. This methodology does not distinguish between the two. The wider literature on filtering noisy labels concerns classification \citep{Brodley1999, frenay2014classification}, but \citet{Martin2021} adapt fourteen classification noise filters to regression, seven of them nearest-neighbor filters that use the neighbors' mean label. They find that the filters improve regression methods on 20 general regression benchmarks with injected label noise, none of them QSAR datasets. 

The neighbor-based noise scores, sample removal, sample weighting, label smoothing and the recovery rate are defined in Sections~\ref{sec:noise:detection} and~\ref{sec:noise:mitigation} of Chapter~\ref{chap:noise}, with the recovery rate in Equation~\ref{eq:recovery}.

% TODO: check (TH lens): the neighbors depend on the representation and the distance metric together, not on 'a distance' alone.
A noise detection method requires a distance that picks the right neighbors, in terms of what is going on structurally. Ideally, those neighbors would also have clean labels. Earlier sections of this chapter argue that the first condition fails, and the second fails whenever noise is spread over the whole training set. A large gap from the neighbors' labels can indicate a noisy label, a noisy neighbor, the wrong neighbors, or an activity cliff. And the score cannot tell you which it is. 

We also used neighbors to clean labels, on 5,000 randomly chosen QM9 molecules (HOMO--LUMO gap, scaffold split) with Gaussian noise on the training labels at $\sigma = 0.3$ and $\sigma = 0.6$. Each cleaning method was judged by retraining a random forest on ECFP4 with clean, noisy and cleaned labels, giving the recovery rate, the share of the $R^2$ lost to noise that cleaning recovers. Neighbor-based scores compared each label with those of the molecule's $k$ nearest training molecules under Euclidean, Manhattan or cosine distance. Table~\ref{tab:code_input_neighbor_recovery} holds only the configurations built on a single representation. This study is separate from the sample-removal study of Chapter~\ref{chap:noise}.

Table~\ref{tab:code_input_neighbor_recovery} shows that with the neighbor-based scores used as sample weights at strength $\alpha = 0.1$, mean recovery was negative for every representation at both noise levels. Of all 860 cleaning configurations, 8 had positive recovery at both noise levels; 6 of these used a single representation, and 4 of those 6 were neighbor-based. Label smoothing toward neighbor or model estimates gave positive recovery in 5.0\% of configurations at $\sigma = 0.3$ and 0.8\% at $\sigma = 0.6$. Mean recovery was negative at every weighting and smoothing strength tried, from 0.1 to 0.7.

\centering

\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
\toprule
 & \multicolumn{2}{c}{Mean recovery (\%)} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{Positive (\% of configurations)} \\
\cmidrule(lr){2-3}\cmidrule(lr){4-5}
Representation & $\sigma = 0.3$ & $\sigma = 0.6$ & $\sigma = 0.3$ & $\sigma = 0.6$ \\
\midrule
ECFP4   & $-3.0$ & $-7.3$ & 33.3 & 16.7 \\

\caption[Recovery with neighbor-based sample weights by representation.]{Recovery rate in $R^2$ on the QM9 HOMO--LUMO gap when neighbor-based noise scores are used as sample weights, for each representation used to find the neighbors, at two levels $\sigma$ of Gaussian label noise. Recovery is the share of the $R^2$ lost to noise that cleaning regains, so negative values mean cleaning made the model worse. Positive is the share of configurations with recovery above zero.}
\label{tab:code_input_neighbor_recovery}
\end{table}

Judging a label by its neighbors usually made the model worse and rarely helped at both noise levels, but it did not always fail. The best configuration at $\sigma = 0.6$ was neighbor-based (PDV, Euclidean distance, local variance ratio score) and recovered +17.2\%. Because noise was added to every training label, the neighbors' labels are also noisy. 

Uncertainty-based methods, which use no neighbors, showed potential at lower noise but degraded at higher noise. Sample weighting showed more promise than label smoothing, but it still usually had an overall negative effect. Over all sample-weighting configurations, not only those in Table~\ref{tab:code_input_neighbor_recovery}, recovery was positive in 14.2\% of configurations at $\sigma = 0.3$ and 4.7\% at $\sigma = 0.6$. The mean recoveries were $-22.9\%$ and $-20.9\%$.

\section{Conclusions}

It is not uncommon that very few features of a standard representation vary across a dataset, especially a smaller dataset. On hERG $K_i$, 1,815 of the 2,048 ECFP4 features take one value in at least 95\% of the 1,412 molecules. Additionally, several representations give different molecules, mostly stereoisomers, the same feature vector, and no model can separate those molecules, whatever their labels. ECFP4 gives 56 of the 1,412 hERG $K_i$ molecules a vector shared with another molecule, all of them stereoisomers.

% TODO: check (TH lens): say which kind of representation this covers: every one tested here is task-agnostic, none built so that distance follows the property.
A pairwise distance metric sums over every feature, so when only a few features matter for the property, the rest dominate the sum. Across 70 combinations of dataset and representation, the Spearman correlation between pair distance and label difference was below 0.10 in 44. Using the five nearest neighbors to predict labels produced Spearman correlations of 0.29 to 0.89 with the measured labels, indicating that the useful information sits in a few close pairs rather than in the distance as a whole.

% [alignment 2026-10-10: 'often rely' changed to match the sweep results paragraph and Ch8 ('all 20')]
In the exploratory sweep of LNL methods, all 20 configurations with the lowest retention used a pairwise distance measure, and judging a label by its neighbors' labels usually made the model worse, as discussed in Section~\ref{sec:flaws:consequences}. The sweep added a distance to some methods designed without one, so poor adaptation may explain part of this.


Table~\ref{tab:flaws:requirements} lists each flaw found in this chapter and the requirement it sets for a representation. Chapter~\ref{chap:method} outlines the development of the adaptive hybrid representation and asks what is the best way to select features for a given prediction task?

\chapter{Building adaptive hybrid representations}\label{chap:method}

\section{Introduction}

% [TH edit 2026-10-10: keeping and leaving out stated as one choice]
So far, Chapter~\ref{chap:flaws} explained how the standard methods of estimating molecular similarity, or conversely distance, are flawed and potentially break the SPP. Specifically, the representations used in these similarity calculations contain irrelevant features that either do not vary or more generally do not pertain to the prediction task at hand. This chapter explores a solution. With the understanding that each representation contains a set of features which contain a certain type of information about that data, we aim to select the most informative features across a larger set of representations, and so leave out the irrelevant ones. We create an adaptive hybrid representation, a set of features bounded by a budget that is selected from several single representations by a search using one dataset and downstream model. The goal is to select a set of features that is customized for a given model and prediction task. As discussed in Chapter~\ref{chap:robustness}, performance can vary significantly across datasets and models. Rather than hope the features you give a model will be useful, this process lets you prescreen them.

% [TH edit 2026-10-10: link to noise chapter added; questions restated instead of pointing to Ch1]
In this chapter we build the adaptive hybrid representation, referred to as the hybrid representation, first reporting on when and why it helps. This chapter asks how to select the best set of features from several representations for a given dataset and model. It also asks whether the method carries over from small molecules to antibodies and reactions. Chapter~\ref{chap:similarity} asks whether distances computed in a hybrid are better than in a single representation. This chapter measures predictive performance on the labels as published, apart from one separate-site experiment with flipped labels, reported in Additional file~\ref{app:add:separate_sites}. Distance measures between hybrids are covered in Chapter~\ref{chap:similarity}, and noise detection and learning with noisy labels in Chapter~\ref{chap:noise}. Chapter~\ref{chap:noise} asks whether noise detection and noise mitigation work better in a hybrid than in the best single representation. It also cleans the labels that the search itself uses, as described in Section~\ref{sec:noise:res:search}.

\subsection{Combining representations}

Not every molecular representation is equal. In fact, they hold very different types of information. RDKit2D descriptors matched or outperformed features derived from GNN embeddings and pretrained language models on many datasets across 62,820 models on a variety of molecular property prediction benchmarks \citep{Deng2023}. Descriptor-based models held 73\% of top-three positions on 11 benchmarks \citep{Jiang2021}. In 1,310 ChEMBL assays, feedforward networks on ECFP fingerprints outperformed graph convolutions and recurrent networks trained on SMILES \citep{Mayr2018}. We know that the choice of representation matters, as is discussed in Chapter~\ref{chap:robustness}, yet the combinations of representation and model are hard to explore by hand \citep{Lanini2023PREFER}. Beyond just molecules, general protein language models predict antibody expression better than antibody-specific ones, while the antibody-specific models are better on binding \citep{Kenlay2024}, and no pathology foundation model is best on every clinical task \citep{ElNahhas2025}. We turn our attention to combining features from these different representations. 

% [TH edit 2026-10-10: restored sentence: joining must combine and filter at once]
Most research in this field relies on a single representation, and few combine features from different representations beyond concatenation. One of the reasons just pooling features from different representations together doesn't work is dimensionality. If you add a standard ECFP4 ($d=2,048$) with Mordred descriptors ($d=1,613$) and an MHG-GNN embedding ($d=1,024$), you end up with 4,685 features, which is far too many considering many datasets from experimental assays contain only a few hundred labeled samples. With such ratios, more features mean weaker predictions \citep{Hughes1968, Bellman1961}. Joining representations also adds the irrelevant features of every representation along with the useful ones. So a method that joins representations has to combine and filter at the same time. The hybrid is built to do both. Therefore, we consider ways of combining representations. 

% [TH edit 2026-10-10: interpretability cost of embeddings added]
The most straightforward method of combining representations is concatenation. This however degrades with non-homogeneous feature sets and obscures which representation is responsible for the score \citep{Stahlschmidt2022}. \citet{Deng2023} found that appending RDKit2D descriptors to GROVER lowered RMSE on only 9 of 24 datasets tested. Late fusion trains one model per source and aggregates the predictions \citep{Steyaert2023}. MOGONET is of this kind, combining per-source predictions in label space \citep{Wang2021}. While late fusion is effective, it loses interactions between the source representations, an issue which is addressed by intermediate fusion, for example cross-attention between sources \citep{Beaude2025}. Multiple kernel learning combines the sources as kernels rather than as whole models \citep{Gonen2011, Cichonska2018}, and produces no interpretable feature sets. \citet{Laufktter2019} found that combining bioactivity-based fingerprints with structure-based ones like ECFP4 improved both predictive performance and scaffold-hopping. Similarly, \citet{Turkina2025} kept, from each of six non-hashed fingerprints, the bits that carried most of a random forest's importance on it, and joined them. The joined bits consistently improved predictive accuracy over the original fingerprints across six datasets. Most of this work involves limited combinations, adding together standard fingerprints and linear representations \citep{Xie2020, Laufktter2019}. Although the features derived from embeddings are not as conceptually straightforward, they contain another axis of information. The cost is interpretability. A selected embedding feature can be traced to its representation, but not to a substructure or a descriptor, as discussed for blueprints below. 

When you want to filter a feature set for the features most important to the prediction task, you can use a dimensionality reduction technique or feature selection methodology. Principal component analysis (PCA) uses linear combinations of the original features, ordered by the variance they explain \citep{Jolliffe2016}. Their combined features are dependent on the original feature units unless each feature is z-scored first, which matters when data from different modalities like fingerprint bits, descriptors and embeddings are joined \citep{Jolliffe2016}. PCA never sees the labels, and components with small variance can still be important in prediction \citep{Jolliffe1982}. Partial least squares (PLS), the supervised counterpart in chemometrics, builds components that covary with the property \citep{Wold2001}. Neither gives components that can be traced to one substructure, descriptor or representation, making them difficult to interpret.

Feature selection keeps a subset of the original features, unlike PCA or PLS which build new ones. Of its three families \citep{Guyon2003}, filter methods score features against the labels before any model is fitted, as minimum-redundancy maximum-relevance selection (mRMR) ranks features by their mutual information with the label minus their redundancy with already-chosen features \citep{Peng2005}. Wrapper methods score subsets of features by fitting the model \citep{Kohavi1997}. Embedded methods select features while the model is fitted, as the lasso does \citep{Tibshirani1996}. Recursive feature elimination repeatedly fits a model and drops the lowest-ranked features \citep{Guyon2002}.

When selecting features, the question is not only what features are most important, but also, what features does the model prefer? One of the ways to answer this is to consider how many features relative to the size of a dataset a given model works best with. As discussed in Chapter~\ref{chap:flaws}, distance-based models such as $k$-nearest neighbors (kNN) and SVM use every feature in every distance computation. Tree ensembles, by contrast, choose one feature per split and can essentially ignore irrelevant ones. \citet{sns}, who created a substructure-based fingerprint called Sort \& Slice, suggest that using labels to select features may result in overfitting. Table~\ref{tab:hybrid_alternatives} compares these alternatives with the adaptive hybrid methodology developed in this thesis. 

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering
\small
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3pt}
\begin{adjustbox}{max width=\textwidth,max totalheight=0.78\textheight}
\begin{tabular}{P{4.2cm}P{2.3cm}P{2.1cm}P{2.3cm}P{2.6cm}}
\toprule
Method & Uses the labels & Keeps original features & Several representations & Retrain to add one \\
\midrule
Concatenation \citep{Stahlschmidt2022} & no & yes & yes & yes \\
Late fusion and ensembling \citep{Steyaert2023, Valsecchi2020} & only if pooling weights are learned & yes & no & no, one more model \\
Intermediate fusion \citep{Beaude2025} & yes & no & yes & yes \\

PCA and PLS \citep{Jolliffe2016, Wold2001} & PCA no, PLS yes & no & yes, on a concatenation & yes \\
