\subsection{The adaptive hybrid representation}

The task of selecting the best features from a set of representations and combining them is not simple. How do you measure what features are ``best''? Are these features ``best'' relative to the representation they came from or to the whole pool of features? Should you select more features from representations that seem to have more signal to begin with, or take a set number of features from every representation? How many single representations should you select features from? How many features should you select? When scoring potential combinations of features, what type of evaluator should you use?

In a dream world, there would be a single answer to each one of these questions that applies to every dataset. Unfortunately we live in reality, and as we discovered, the answer to every single one of these questions is ``depends on the data.'' The task of selecting the best features then became a search space optimization challenge. The question actually became for a given prediction task, defined by the dataset, prediction target, and model, what is the best methodology for selecting features? The hybrid search does not output just a list of features, rather it outputs an ideal search configuration to find features for your data. It tells you how to score features (feature importance score), what rules you should use to select them (allocation method), how to score potential hybrid feature combinations (internal evaluator). In this research, we run this hybrid search multiple times across different maximum number of features (budgets), number of input representations, and models (downstream evaluators) on each dataset. While it is not formal ``training'' in the ML sense, the hybrid search is essentially a feature selection method that is fit on the training data. The representations used in this thesis are listed in Tables~\ref{tab:reps_smallmol}, \ref{tab:reps_reactions} and~\ref{tab:reps_antibodies}.

% [TH edit 2026-10-10: formal Definition paragraph replaces the Formally sentence]
\noindent\textit{Definition.} Take $K \ge 2$ single representations of the same samples, their labels, one downstream model and a budget $B$. An adaptive hybrid representation is a set of at most $B$ of the original features of these single representations. Its features are chosen by a hybrid search on the training samples, for that dataset and downstream model. Each kept feature stays linked to the single representation it came from.

% TODO: check (TH lens): blueprints are not defined until the next section; say in a few words what a blueprint is, or point forward.
 The output of a hybrid search is itself a feature selection method that when run on the given dataset, produces the hybrid representation. This search is run on a single dataset and downstream model, and the resulting hybrid differs by model, dataset, and even split. Its intended use case is when there are multiple single representations that describe different aspects of a molecule, and you want to combine these different features for a single training task. The hybrid ranks features by a fitted model's importance scores, as embedded methods do, and keeps a fixed number of the top-ranked ones, as a filter does. Classical methods treat all features as one flat list. The nearest classical relative, the group lasso, keeps or drops predefined groups of variables whole \citep{Yuan2006}, whereas the hybrid splits the budget among representations as set out in Section~\ref{sec:method:allocation}. Because only original features are kept, each traces back to one representation, which makes blueprints possible.

\subsection{Blueprints and their chemical interpretation}

As explained above, the adaptive hybrid representation relies on a search. A completed search is saved as a blueprint, which records the settings needed to select the features that make the hybrid representation. Because each selected feature traces back to its original single representation and relative importance rank, the search can tell you about your own prediction task. The best configuration is saved for each downstream model and budget, giving one blueprint per pair, allowing us to compare blueprints and see if different models prefer different features. 

Depending on the origin of the feature, some are chemically interpretable. By definition, the PDV contains values that relate back to chemical properties like molecular weight or logP. An ECFP4 bit can be shown as the atom environment that sets it, and a MACCS key stands for a known substructure. Beyond small molecules, the readable unit of an antibody is a sequence position and its residue. This feature-level explainability fails when features are derived from learned embeddings. That will only tell you if the learned embedding itself is useful as a representation, but nothing about the selected feature. 

% [TH edit 2026-10-10: restored explainability sentence + drug safety clause]
While a set of thousands of features may seem overwhelming and irrelevant, identifying substructures or descriptors that recur among the selected features can point to parts of the molecule that may be involved in binding or activity. If the selected features make sense chemically for that endpoint, it's a good sign that the selection mechanism is finding real signal instead of fitting noise. Conversely, if the hybrid search is done over a range of budgets or models, and the blueprints are showing a vastly different set of features, that may be an indication that the features at hand are struggling to provide information about the prediction task. Explainability is a recognized need in drug discovery \citep{JimenezLuna2020}, especially in drug safety assessment when predicting and interpreting toxicity. A predictor that scores proposed molecules in generative design \citep{Olivecrona2017} could also take a hybrid as input, which we leave to future work.

\subsection{Applications beyond small molecules}

The hybrid search is a generalized search that takes in multiple feature matrices from the same samples and a supervised prediction task, and outputs a feature selection method that produces a filtered set of features from those original vectors. The default search objective scores prediction only, meaning the methodology applies to tabular tasks or cases where you have different measurements of the same inputs. Although this was developed with small molecules and QSAR modeling in mind, it is by no means limited to that use case. While there are some functionalities in this research that are tailored to chemical tasks, such as blueprints mapping features back to their chemical interpretations and the use of nearest-neighbor objectives to aid in the computation of molecular similarity, the core functionality of the hybrid process is domain-independent.


One caveat concerns sparse representations such as one-hot encoded sequences, where each feature is set in only a few samples and so scores low importance on its own.
Here, the only one-hot encoding used in the experiments is a single representation on three antibody datasets: nanobody thermostability, change in binding free energy ($\Delta\Delta G$) and CHO polyreactivity. The blocks substitution matrix (BLOSUM) encoding is a single representation on humanness.

While this setup was initially intended for small molecules, we extend this research to antibodies. 
Twelve antibody datasets are used in this chapter, listed in Table~\ref{tab:datasets}. They range from deep mutational scans of one antibody to panels of distinct antibodies. One Buchwald--Hartwig yield dataset tests the method on reactions, a separate domain.  

Another caveat of this setup, discussed in Section~\ref{sec:method:limitations}, is that the hybrid can help only on datasets with enough samples and with single representations whose features carry importance. When all the representations score poorly, there are not enough useful features to pull from. Additionally, if there is only a single representation that carries signal, there is nothing for the hybrid to improve upon, which is shown in Section~\ref{sec:method:results_single}.

\section{Methods}

Figure~\ref{fig:hybrid_search_space} outlines the hybrid search.

    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=\textwidth,height=0.72\textheight,keepaspectratio]{figures_code_input/hybrid_search_space.png}

The outer test fold is set aside before the search starts and is not drawn (Figure~\ref{fig:split_levels}). The inputs (left) are split into five cross-validation folds, and the single representations are scored as baselines. Each fold is searched over importance methods, allocation methods, budgets, internal evaluators and downstream models (center). The best hybrid for each downstream model is stored as a blueprint (right). Pruning is drawn but was not used in this chapter's searches (Section~\ref{sec:method:pruning}).}

\subsection{From single representations to a hybrid}\label{sec:method:build}

The input to the search is $K$ frozen representation matrices $\mathbf{X}^{(k)} \in \mathbb{R}^{n\times d_k}$, $k = 1, \dots, K$, for the same $n$ samples, whose widths $d_k$ range from tens to thousands of features, as well as a label vector $\mathbf{y}$, one group label per sample for cross-validation (for example, its scaffold group), the evaluation metric, the task type, one or more downstream models and a list of budgets. It returns a hybrid of at most $B$ features before any augmentation features added outside the budget, which Section~\ref{sec:method:augmentation} describes. The budget $B$ is a maximum rather than a target. It also returns a blueprint that records the settings necessary to rebuild the hybrid representation on another set of samples, such as another subset of the same dataset.

% TODO: check (TH lens): the leak comes from selecting on all samples before the split, not from pooling features; as written, a reader thinks concatenating representations is the leak.
When combining representations, we are careful to avoid data leakage. Because the feature selection process trains on the features and their labels, pooling all the features prior to selection would leak validation-level information into the training, which is a well-documented bias in feature selection  \citep{Ambroise2002, Vabalas2019}. As such, we run each hybrid search within a cross-validation fold. When a single representation is scored, we impute missing values with training-fold medians. A representation wider than 5,000 features is scored on its own using only its top 500 features (200 on datasets under 1,000 samples). These are the features that a LightGBM model of 50 trees of depth 5, fitted on the training part of each fold, splits on most often. This affects only that representation's score, not the features the hybrid can select from it.

The data are split at three levels within CV runs. Every split keeps each group whole, a scaffold for small molecules, a sequence cluster for antibodies, and some pre-defined grouping for datasets from other domains, as Section~\ref{sec:method:datasets} describes.
With fewer than five groups, the inner and internal splits use as many folds as there are groups, as listed in Additional file~\ref{app:add:fold_counts}. Figure~\ref{fig:split_levels} shows the three levels as nested boxes.

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\small
\begin{tikzpicture}[x=1cm,y=1cm]
    \draw[thick] (0,0) rectangle (14,8.0);
    \node[anchor=north west] at (0.15,7.9) {\textbf{Whole dataset}};
    \draw[fill=gray!15] (0.4,6.2) rectangle (13.6,7.2);
    \node[anchor=west] at (0.55,6.7) {\textbf{Outer test fold}: used once, to score the finished hybrid};
    \draw[thick] (0.4,0.3) rectangle (13.6,5.9);
    \node[anchor=north west] at (0.55,5.8) {\textbf{Outer training part} (the other outer folds)};
    \draw[fill=gray!15] (0.8,4.1) rectangle (13.2,5.0);
    \node[anchor=west] at (0.95,4.55) {\textbf{Inner test fold}: scores each search setting};
    \draw[thick] (0.8,0.6) rectangle (13.2,3.8);
    \node[anchor=north west] at (0.95,3.7) {\textbf{Inner training part}};
    \draw[fill=gray!15] (1.2,0.9) rectangle (6.8,2.8);
    \node[align=center] at (4.0,1.85) {\textbf{80\%}\\[2pt]importance scores};
    \draw[fill=gray!15] (7.2,0.9) rectangle (12.8,2.8);
    \node[align=center] at (10.0,1.85) {\textbf{20\%}\\[2pt]representation scores,\\[2pt]which set the budget split};
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption[The three levels of splitting.]{The three levels of splitting, each inside the one before. Every split keeps each group whole.}
\label{fig:split_levels}
\end{figure}

For each representation $k$, the importance method gives every feature a score $s^{(k)}_j \ge 0$, computed on the 80\% part of the internal split. These scores rank features only within their own single representation. The standalone score $r_k$ is the score of the internal evaluator, trained on representation $k$ in the 80\% part and scored on the 20\% part, in the dataset's metric. These scores cannot be compared across metrics as they stand: a lower RMSE is better and it has no fixed upper limit, whereas a Spearman correlation is higher when better and lies between $-1$ and 1. Before the budget is split, each $r_k$ is therefore converted to a score $\tilde r_k$ on which 0 means the representation predicts no better than a trivial predictor and 1 means it predicts perfectly. The trivial predictor depends on the metric. For Spearman, Pearson, the Matthews correlation coefficient (MCC) and $R^2$, $\tilde r_k = \max(0, r_k)$. For AUROC and the concordance index, $\tilde r_k = \max(0, 2(r_k - 0.5))$. For RMSE, $\tilde r_k = \max(0, 1 - \mathrm{RMSE}_k/\mathrm{RMSE}_0)$, where $\mathrm{RMSE}_0$ is the error of predicting the training mean, and mean absolute error is rescaled likewise against the training median. For accuracy and weighted F1, $\tilde r_k = \max(0, (r_k - r_0)/(1 - r_0))$, where $r_0$ is the score of always predicting the majority training class.

% TODO: check (TH lens): the global pool is introduced only in the allocation section; name it here as one of the five allocation methods and point there.
An optional representation count $N$ keeps the $N$ representations with the highest standalone scores on the fold and discards the rest for that iteration of the search. Each remaining representation receives a weight $w_k = g(\tilde r_k)$, with $g$ set by the allocation method, and a share $b^*_k = B\, w_k / \sum_l w_l$ features, rounded by largest remainder and capped at its width. A representation left out by the representation count, or whose standalone fit fails, receives no features. Thus $\sum_k b_k = \min(B, \sum_k d_k)$ and $b_k \le d_k$, and a representation with $w_k > 0$ keeps at least one feature. The global pool does not use these shares (see below).

Let $S_k$ be the top $b_k$ features of representation $k$ by $s^{(k)}_j$, among those with $s^{(k)}_j > 0$; if none scores above 0, its full $b_k$ is taken. The cut exists because tree importance gives exactly 0 to every feature no tree split on, and filling the budget with those would pick them by their position in the matrix. The hybrid is $\mathbf{H} = [\mathbf{X}^{(1)}_{:,S_1}, \dots, \mathbf{X}^{(K)}_{:,S_K}]$, with $\sum_k |S_k| \le B$, so it can be narrower than its budget.

The hybrid search returns a list of settings for feature selection, referred to here as a configuration. It is scored by fitting the downstream model on the hybrid in each fold of the search and averaging the held-out scores. Each pair of downstream model and budget has its own search, and thus its own winner. Because this score is chosen over a range of selected configurations, it is optimistic \citep{Varma2006, Cawley2010}. However, so is the score of the highest-performing single representation it is compared with, which is also the best of several. The whole search runs inside each outer training fold, and the winning settings are rebuilt there and scored once on the untouched outer test fold, both for the winner at each budget and for each downstream model's best across budgets.

The inner search score minus the outer test score is reported against the budget in Section~\ref{sec:method:results_overfit}.
Two variants of this search are used later. In Chapter~\ref{chap:noise}, under label noise, training quantities use noisy labels while held-out scoring uses clean labels. Under the nearest-neighbor objective, each sample's label is predicted from its five nearest neighbors in the feature space, as in Chapter~\ref{chap:similarity}.

As explained above, a hybrid search returns a list of settings that function as a feature selection method to generate the hybrid representation itself. These settings are recorded in a blueprint that can be used on new folds of the same dataset or even new data. The blueprint contains additional information, including some fields for analysis, meta-data, and scores. These scores are used only for comparative purposes as in this research, the hybrids are rebuilt per-fold and are scored as such. Additional file~\ref{app:add:blueprint_listing} lists every field of a blueprint and its type. The blueprint does not record the specific features that are returned by the hybrid, as it is meant to be re-run using the same settings on new, labeled data. Alternatively, you can reuse the features of a labeled fit and apply them to data with no labels under the assumption that the same features matter between sets. 

The blueprint is extended by an additional explainability layer. Since it does not store feature names, this additional step runs on a completed hybrid, records where each selected feature came from and, wherever possible, what that feature means. An ECFP4 bit is named by the atom environment that sets it, written as a SMILES fragment from a molecule with the bit set. MACCS keys are named by their SMILES arbitrary target specification (SMARTS) pattern and a short name, and descriptor features by descriptor name. Protein composition, descriptor and k-mer features, antibody composition, one-hot, BLOSUM, structural and inverse-folding features and PCA components are named too. MAP4 and DRFP features get only a bit index, and Avalon, Mordred, atom-pair, graph-kernel and learned-embedding features only the representation name and dimension index. The Mordred descriptor names in Figure~\ref{fig:blueprint_logd} were added in a separate step.

\subsection{The search space}\label{sec:method:searchspace}
To run a hybrid search, we first measure feature importance, select how many features to take from each contributing single representation, and choose how to score a candidate feature set. And for each of those steps, there's no one right way to do it. Different prediction tasks prefer different methods of scoring feature importance or candidate features, different sets of input representations, and so forth. The search tries to find the ideal settings for the hybrid feature selection process, tailored to a given dataset and downstream model. All combinations of settings values are explored, as the settings interact. The best budget depends on the downstream model, as shown below for nanobody thermostability. This search grid also tests the chapter's claim that no single setting suits every dataset.

Six settings in total are searched, namely the downstream model, the budget, the representation count, the importance method, the allocation method and the internal evaluator. Table~\ref{tab:search_tiers} gives the values each setting can take, and Table~\ref{tab:decided_grid} the smaller set searched on each dataset in this chapter. The importance methods tested are LightGBM gain, TreeSHAP or DeepLIFT (described below). Feature-level importance depends on the model as well as on the data \citep{Dong2020}, and as such we observe that within the search, a different ranking of features produces a different hybrid output.

The downstream model is trained on the hybrid representation. This model can be a supervised model of any form, classification or regression. We used random forest, XGBoost, LightGBM, SVM, a deep neural network (DNN) and a Gaussian process. Each dataset in this chapter uses two of them. 

% [TH edit 2026-10-10: how ECFP bits and descriptors count against the budget]
The budget is the maximum number of features in the hybrid, taken from eight values (10, 25, 50, 100, 250, 500, 1,000 and 2,000 features). Every feature counts once against the budget, whether it is an ECFP4 bit or a PDV descriptor such as logP. Bits and descriptors from different representations do not compete for the budget directly. As Section~\ref{sec:method:allocation} explains, each representation's share is set first, and its features are ranked only against each other. The one exception is the global pool, an allocation method that ranks features from different representations against each other. When the budget is too small, informative features can be left out. When it is too large, the hybrid picks up too many uninformative features, and on small datasets these give the model room to overfit.

We select a set of up to 10 single representations per dataset, chosen prior to the search as seen in Table~\ref{tab:dataset_reps}. The selections were made based on each representation's relative performance on the prediction task. We took into account what type of information each representation contained, and tried to span a wide set across the selected 10. For example, while there are many high-performing graph-based embeddings in QSAR modeling, for the purposes of testing a feature selection process which takes features from different sources of information, it is better to have a mix of graph-based embeddings, as well as other types of molecular representations like substructure fingerprints, property vectors, sequential embeddings, etc.  The representation count (2, 3, 5 or all) sets how many may contribute features, keeping those with the highest standalone scores on each fold. The number of representation counts to test is searched in the sweep as datasets differ in how many representations have features worth selecting, and pooled feature selection over all representations has no such setting. While we tested various methods of pooling the features, we found computing feature importance scores and concatenation techniques on a global pooled feature set was less often best in preliminary runs than when the feature scoring is kept on a per-representation basis. Additional file~\ref{app:add:design_experiments} reports these preliminary runs. Since the features contain different types of information at different scales, pooling the features too early makes comparisons within the pool challenging. We found that it's better to determine which features are important to each representation first, and go from there.

% TODO: CHECK: says QM9 with LightGBM searched 3, 5 and all representations; Table tab:decided_grid gives QM9 "3, all". One of the two is wrong.
Every allocation method is paired with every count of input representations used. Some datasets performed best with 2 or 3 representations and others with all 10. Some prediction tasks had a very clear preference. For instance, on HOMO--LUMO gap with LightGBM, which searched 3, 5, and all representations, preferred 3 on every fold.

\subsubsection{Feature importance scoring}\label{sec:method:importance}

We run a feature importance scoring method on each single representation, which gives each feature a single positive score. These scores remain relative within a given representation, so they are not compared or normalized between representations. Split-based tree importance is biased toward features with many possible split points \citep{Strobl2007}, which matters here because we use binary fingerprint bits alongside continuous embeddings. No single importance method works best across datasets and downstream models. After testing a wider variety of methods found across literature, we settled on three methods whose rankings did not correlate strongly with each other and whose selected features performed well as model input. Additional file~\ref{app:add:design_experiments} gives these tests.

We score features using LightGBM gain importance, SHapley Additive exPlanations (SHAP) values for tree ensembles (TreeSHAP) and DeepLIFT (Deep Learning Important FeaTures) \citep{Shrikumar2017}. LightGBM gain measures how much a feature reduced the training loss where trees split on it \citep{ke2017lightgbm}. TreeSHAP gives each feature's exact Shapley value for each molecule's prediction from a tree model \citep{Lundberg2020}. DeepLIFT measures how much a feature moves the output of a small network, trained with Adam \citep{Kingma2015}, away from its output on an all-zero reference input, which after standardization places every feature at its training mean \citep{Shrikumar2017, Kokhlikyan2020, Ancona2018}. On Caco-2, the median share of features scoring above 0 was 30\% for LightGBM gain, 70\% for TreeSHAP and 90\% for DeepLIFT (preliminary, over 10 representations and 3 folds). Table~\ref{tab:importance_methods} gives the settings of each method, including the pre-screen of representations wider than 2,000 features. Each dataset searches one, two or all three importance methods depending on its individually-decided search settings, given in Table~\ref{tab:decided_grid}.

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering
\small
\begin{adjustbox}{max width=\textwidth,max totalheight=0.78\textheight}
\begin{tabular}{@{}P{0.15\linewidth}P{0.24\linewidth}P{0.22\linewidth}P{0.17\linewidth}P{0.16\linewidth}@{}}
\toprule
Method & Model fitted per representation & Score of one feature & Classification labels & Representations over 2{,}000 features \\
\midrule
LightGBM gain & LightGBM, 50 trees, depth 6 & Summed loss reduction of its splits; 0 if never split on & One model on the class labels & 2{,}000 highest-variance features fitted; the rest score 0 \\
TreeSHAP & RF, 50 trees, depth 10 & Mean absolute SHAP value over training molecules & Binary: positive class; multiclass: mean over classes & As for LightGBM gain \\
DeepLIFT & NN, ReLU layers of 64 and 32 units, 100 epochs of Adam, learning rate 0.001 & Mean absolute attribution against an all-zero reference (Rescale rule) & Binary: positive class; multiclass: mean over classes & Every feature scored \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{adjustbox}
\caption[Settings of the importance methods.]{Settings of the importance methods, fitted per representation and fold. Unlisted settings are library defaults.}
\label{tab:importance_methods}
\end{table}

\subsubsection{Feature allocation methods}\label{sec:method:allocation}

The allocation method decides how many of the $B$ features each representation contributes while the importance method decides which. Of the five methods searched, four give each representation a fixed share computed from its rescaled standalone score (linear, squared, softmax and rank-decay allocation). The fifth, the global pool, lets the candidate features offered by every representation compete in one importance ranking.

% TODO: check (TH lens): was this assumption measured? The even split over the same representations, in the ensembles results, tests it; point to it here.
The four fixed-share methods compute budget shares from the standalone score on internal validation. Only linear allocation makes the share proportional to that score. All four assume that representations with stronger predictive performance contribute more valuable features. This assumption fails when a strong representation relies on a few select features. In that case, a large share of features will be given to that representation, picking up on weaker features and potentially missing out on stronger ones from other representations. Fixed shares ignore complementarity, so two representations carrying the same information both receive a share. Only the global pool ranks features from different representations against each other, however ranking feature importance on a global pool is dubious as the features are not necessarily appropriate to compare. 

The first method of feature allocation we tried was a greedy forward selection, in which the hybrid is built one block of features at a time  \citep{Kohavi1997, Guyon2003}. In each round, the next block is added from representations in order of which improves the internal validation score. This in theory lets it choose features for how well they work together. In practice, this is very expensive. With blocks of 10 features and a budget of 500 and 10 representations, it needs up to 500 model fits per configuration and fold against 10 for a score-based method. It was also less accurate, in preliminary runs on Jain antibody neonatal Fc receptor (FcRn) retention it placed 16th of 17 allocation methods, with a Spearman correlation of 0.360 against 0.453 for linear allocation. Additional file~\ref{app:add:design_experiments} gives these runs, and we therefore removed it from the search.

The fixed-share methods differ only in how $w_k$ is computed from $\tilde r_k$, as seen in Table~\ref{tab:alloc_methods} with a worked example in Table~\ref{tab:alloc_example}. Because every rescaled score lies between 0 and 1, softmax cannot give one representation most of the budget. Across all 10 single representations the best takes at most 23.2\%. Rank decay uses only the order: best representation receives 27.3\% and the tenth 1.8\%, whatever the gaps between their scores.

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering
\small
\begin{adjustbox}{max width=\textwidth,max totalheight=0.78\textheight}
\begin{tabular}{@{}P{0.13\linewidth}P{0.24\linewidth}P{0.14\linewidth}P{0.24\linewidth}P{0.19\linewidth}@{}}
\toprule
Method & Weight $w_k$ & Fixed setting & How features are chosen & When a representation receives 0 features \\
\midrule
Linear & $\tilde r_k$ & none & Top $b_k$ of its own ranking & $\tilde r_k = 0$ \\
Squared & $\tilde r_k^{\,2}$ & power 2 & Top $b_k$ of its own ranking & $\tilde r_k = 0$ \\
Softmax & $\exp\big((\tilde r_k - \max_l \tilde r_l)/T\big)$ & $T = 1$ & Top $b_k$ of its own ranking & never from its score \\
Rank decay & $e^{-0.3\rho_k}$, $\rho_k$ = rank by score, 0 for the best & decay 0.3 & Top $b_k$ of its own ranking & never from its score \\
Global pool & $\tilde r_k^{\,2}$, sizing its offer to a pool of $2B$ candidates & power 2; pool $2B$ & Top $B$ of one joint ranking over all offered candidates & $\tilde r_k = 0$, or none of its candidates in the joint top $B$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{adjustbox}
\caption[The five allocation methods searched.]{The five allocation methods. $w_k$ is the weight computed from representation $k$'s rescaled standalone score $\tilde r_k$, $B$ is the budget, $b_k$ is the share of representation $k$, $\rho_k$ is its rank by score and $T$ is the softmax temperature.}
\label{tab:alloc_methods}
\end{table}

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering
\begin{adjustbox}{max width=\textwidth,max totalheight=0.78\textheight}
\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}
\toprule
Method & $\tilde r_1 = 0.6$ & $\tilde r_2 = 0.5$ & $\tilde r_3 = 0.2$ \\
\midrule
Linear & 46 & 39 & 15 \\
Squared & 55 & 39 & 6 \\
Softmax & 39 & 35 & 26 \\
Rank decay & 44 & 32 & 24 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{adjustbox}
\caption[Worked example of the four fixed-share allocation methods.]{Worked example of the four fixed-share allocation methods for a budget of 100 features and three representations. Each entry is the number of features a representation receives, with shares rounded by largest remainder.}
\label{tab:alloc_example}
\end{table}

% [TH lens edit 2026-10-10: AI text. Added the bit-versus-continuous fairness point for the one joint ranking; Strobl2007 and the bias statement are already in sec:method:importance. Hedged with 'can'.]
% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
The global pool works in two steps. First, each representation offers a number of candidate features, set by its squared weight $\tilde r_k^{\,2}$ as a share of a pool of $2B$ candidates. Each offer is capped at the representation's width and holds only features that score above 0. Second, the same importance method is fitted once on all the offered features joined together, and the hybrid keeps the top $B$ that score above 0. How many features a representation contributes is therefore decided by this joint ranking, and it can be none. A feature's rank also depends on what the other representations offered. In this joint ranking, fingerprint bits compete directly with continuous descriptors and embedding values. Split-based tree importance is biased toward features with many possible split points \citep{Strobl2007}. With LightGBM gain or TreeSHAP, a binary bit can therefore rank below a continuous feature that carries no more information.

\subsubsection{Search configuration and internal evaluation}

% [TH edit 2026-10-10: evaluator scores single representations (code: find_best_hybrid.py 4978-4989), not candidate hybrids]
The most useful features for an RF may not be the most useful for an SVM, and vice versa. The relative importance of a feature is dependent on the model \citep{Dong2020}. Within the search grid, we use an internal evaluator to fit on one single representation at a time. This process is separate from the final downstream model evaluation. In a standard search, four internal evaluators are used: RF, DNN, LightGBM, and the matched evaluator. The matched evaluator scores each single representation with the same model type as the downstream model. Each internal evaluator is fitted on the internal training fold and scores on the internal validation fold within that CV iteration using the dataset's specified evaluation metric. 

In the full grid, a random forest, LightGBM or DNN downstream model is searched with three internal evaluators, because its matched evaluator is already one of the other three. An XGBoost, SVM or Gaussian process downstream model is searched with four, because the matched evaluator adds one of its own family. Table~\ref{tab:decided_grid} shows the five datasets that searched fewer. EGFR binding has no random forest evaluator, expression titer and Buchwald--Hartwig yield have two evaluators, and AC-SINS self-association has no Gaussian-process evaluator. SMAC colloidal stability scores the SVM's hybrids without the DNN evaluator. Only the DNN evaluator shares its downstream model's architecture and training settings. Even it draws its 20\% early-stopping samples at random, whereas the downstream DNN splits them by the outer split's groups. Additional file~\ref{app:add:model_settings} gives every evaluator setting.

% [TH edit 2026-10-10: evaluator scores single representations, not candidate hybrids; answers b_k question]
The internal evaluator's scores are the standalone scores $r_k$. Using the allocation methods in Table~\ref{tab:alloc_methods}, they set each representation's share $b_k$ of the budget and, through the representation count, which representations are kept. The internal evaluator does not rank features within a representation. Each finished hybrid is then scored by fitting the downstream model on it. Selecting a feature set by fitting a model on it, and keeping the set that scores best, is known as the wrapper method \citep{Kohavi1997}. This is distinct from a filter method which scores features without fitting a model. 

The search tests every combination of the six settings on a dataset under 5-fold cross-validation. Over every value of Table~\ref{tab:search_tiers}, this is 10,080 configurations with all six downstream models, or 8,160 without the Gaussian process. Additional file~\ref{app:add:search_size} gives the search size. With each dataset's own values in Table~\ref{tab:decided_grid}, the searches reported in this chapter scored 108 to 3,360 configurations per outer fold.

% TODO: check (TH lens): the earlier whole-dataset search that set each grid saw the held-out samples (Evaluation protocol), so 'at worst less informative' is not true; also say whether the 10 single representations were chosen from it.
Each dataset's search space is hand-selected from the full search, made to save compute, as Section~\ref{sec:method:decided_grid} describes. At worst, it finds a less informative hybrid than the full search would have. This decision was made due to time limitations.

% [TH edit 2026-10-10: title changed from Augmentation]
\subsection{Handling small and specialized feature sets}\label{sec:method:augmentation}

% [TH edit 2026-10-10: separates from data augmentation]
Up to this point, we have considered features as coming from a whole representation of a molecule. However, you can compute features about a molecule that are not descriptive or expansive enough to be considered a full representation. Examples of these features include log-likelihood ratios, 2- and 3-D shape features, and pharmacophore features. Others, such as mutation features that record differences from a parent antibody, may carry more meaning beside a full representation. Therefore, on some datasets we add them to the hybrid as augmentation features. These are extra features for each sample, not the extra samples of data augmentation. We call the finished hybrid $\mathbf{H}$ the base and the feature sets that supply extra features the donors. Donors are set per dataset in the Augmentation column of Table~\ref{tab:dataset_reps}:
\begin{itemize}
    \item small molecules: MACCS keys, 3D pharmacophore features, shape features (USR, USRCAT), the Coulomb matrix, topological torsions and, on hERG blocking, MolCLR;
    \item antibodies: mutation features, log-likelihood ratios, amino-acid composition, and the paratope and structural features;
    \item Buchwald--Hartwig: MACCS keys and 3D pharmacophore features of the reactants and of the product.
\end{itemize}
Uncapped augmentation adds the extra features outside the budget, and capped augmentation puts them in place of the weakest base features. In an earlier search of 16 datasets, neither augmented condition beat no augmentation by more than 1\% on any dataset, or 3\% on datasets under about 1,000 samples. The searches in this chapter therefore ran without augmentation, a choice recorded in Section~\ref{sec:method:decided_grid}. Only the nearest-neighbor search of Chapter~\ref{chap:similarity}, on SMAC colloidal stability and HIC retention, keeps uncapped augmentation. Additional file~\ref{app:add:augmentation} gives how donor features are ranked and added, and the three conditions.


% [TH edit 2026-10-10: title changed from Adaptive pruning]
\subsection{Pruning the search space}\label{sec:method:pruning}

% [TH edit 2026-10-10: opening sentence added; says per-fold searches ran unpruned (_shared.py 1419-1432)]
% [2026-10-10] Restored at the author's request: her paragraph from thesis.tex at commit 9763e83, unchanged. Rules checked against DEFAULT_PRUNING_SCHEDULE in src/kirby/find_best_hybrid.py on 2026-10-10 (vote_folds 2; checkpoint 100 with half dropped; allocation best 2 at 20%; representation count best 1 at 10%; importance best 2 at 20%; internal evaluator best 3 at 10%).
Adaptive pruning stops scoring a configuration that is far behind after two of the five folds, since the other three folds would only confirm its loss. The per-fold searches reported in this chapter ran without it and instead used the smaller grid per dataset given in Section~\ref{sec:method:decided_grid}.

Adaptive pruning: every configuration is first scored on the first two folds. At budget 100, within each (model, evaluator, representation count) group, (allocation, importance) pairs with no configuration in the better half are not searched at larger budgets. Each axis is then voted on within groups that fix every other axis: an allocation method is pruned if it is in the best 2 in fewer than 20\% of groups, a representation count if it is best in fewer than 10\%, an importance method if it is in the best 2 in fewer than 20\%, and an internal evaluator if it is in the best 3 in fewer than 10\%. Survivors are scored on the remaining folds.

It is commented out because it shows the older rule (fold 0 only, checkpoints at budgets 100 and 500), not the two-fold rule above. Uncomment once it is redrawn.

\subsection{Search grid per dataset}\label{sec:method:decided_grid}

We identified such a large range of settings in the search space, as seen by every value in Table~\ref{tab:search_tiers}, that running every setting for every fold of every dataset would be costly and frankly wasteful. It is more efficient to adaptively prune the search space, or rely on hard-coded search settings for each dataset. Due to time constraints, we chose the latter as it gave us a bit more flexibility. From earlier searches across each dataset, we identified two downstream models with high scores and reasonable training times. Likewise, we kept the budgets whose ten best configurations scored within 1\% of the best budget (3\% on datasets under about 1,000 samples), widened to at least three consecutive budgets. Importance methods, allocation methods, internal evaluators and representation counts that scored well below the best were dropped.

Two datasets, QM9 and hERG blocking, were too slow for the earlier search over every value. They were searched over a shorter fixed list, called the fast tier (LightGBM gain importance; linear, squared and rank-decay allocation; three or all representations), and their grid stays within it. The search with the nearest-neighbor objective has its own grid, given in Chapter~\ref{chap:similarity}.

\subsection{Evaluation protocol}\label{sec:method:evaluation}

Within each experiment, the dataset is split into outer folds, as set out in Section~\ref{sec:method:datasets}. The search sees only the training samples of each outer fold, and the selected features produced by the search are rebuilt on them and applied unchanged to the held-out samples. Only three experiments limit each held-out feature to the range it takes on the training samples: the comparison with the best single representation, the frequent and rare scaffolds, and the comparison of many and few representations.

% TODO: check (TH lens): the same hybrid scores differently across tables, e.g. nanobody thermostability with the DNN is 0.6764, 0.6788, 0.623, 0.615 and 0.628; say once why (own searches, range limit, budgets, subsets).
Because of time and computational constraints, not every experiment in Chapters~\ref{chap:method} to~\ref{chap:noise} ran on every fold or every dataset. Some experiments here ran on the first three outer folds, most noise experiments in Chapter~\ref{chap:noise} ran on the first outer fold only, and some experiments have no result on some datasets. We report the results that exist, give the number of folds beside each one, and name the datasets where a result is missing. 

% [TH edit 2026-10-10: states where held-out data was and was not seen, incl. whole-dataset grid search]
We compare the hybrid with the best single representation, using all of its features, as this is what a user would otherwise run. This best representation is picked from the search's scores on the outer training samples. It is picked separately for each downstream model and each fold, so it can change between folds. It is then refit on the training samples and scored on the same held-out samples as the hybrid. The hybrid is held to its budget, so the single representation often has many times more features. We record both numbers of features, since part of any difference in score can come from the difference in width. The main result is the hybrid's score minus the score of the best single representation. A positive value means the hybrid scored better, and for RMSE we flip the sign so that this still holds.

No test set exists outside these outer folds. Each reported score is on an outer test fold that the search of that fold never saw. The hybrid's settings and the best single representation are both chosen from scores on the outer training samples only. One choice was made before these searches. Each dataset's grid, including its two downstream models, came from an earlier search of the whole dataset, described in Section~\ref{sec:method:decided_grid}. That earlier search included the samples that the outer folds later hold out.

The last column gives, for each of the dataset's two downstream models, the single representation the search ranked first on the training samples of at least three of the five outer folds.}
\label{tab:datasets}
\end{table}

Section~\ref{app:datasets} of the Additional files gives the shared and per-dataset pre-processing steps, with the counts after each step. The LogD and Caco-2 labels are clipped at their 1st and 99th percentiles, so on Caco-2, for example, 22 molecules share the highest label and 23 the lowest.

As in Chapter~\ref{chap:robustness}, no group is split between folds, and classification folds are also stratified with a fixed seed. Every dataset is split into five outer folds, though some experiments ran on fewer, as explained in Section~\ref{sec:method:evaluation}, As set out in Section~\ref{sec:evaluation}, no split is random. On a given dataset, every representation, model and hybrid is scored on the same folds.


\subsubsection{Groups by domain}
The small-molecule datasets, QM9 included, are grouped by Bemis--Murcko scaffold, maintaining stereochemistry. On QM9 the largest group is the empty scaffold of acyclic molecules, with 1,041 of the 9,978 molecules. 
The two datasets from Biogen \citep{fang2023}, rat microsomal clearance and MDR1--MDCK efflux, have 1.2 and 1.1 molecules per scaffold, respectively, so holding out a scaffold there is essentially like holding out single molecules, meaning in practice it is random. Chapter~\ref{chap:robustness} used a different scaffold key and different curation for its versions of LogD, Caco-2, hERG $K_i$ and QM9, and Section~\ref{app:datasets:ch3} of the Additional files lists the differences.

The reaction dataset is Buchwald--Hartwig C--N couplings from high-throughput plates. Following \citet{Ahneman2018}, it is grouped by additive: each held-out fold holds four or five additives unseen in training, as outlined in Section~\ref{app:datasets} of the Additional files.

In the Koenig deep mutational scan of the anti-vascular endothelial growth factor (VEGF) antibody G6, each variant carries one mutation.
Variants are grouped by their mutated position, producing 225 groups. With all mutations at one position on the same side of a split, a model cannot look up a per-position effect at test time \citep{Vieira2026}. The nanobody and humanness sets are grouped by single-linkage clusters at 95\% sequence identity, the identity threshold \citet{Kenlay2024} used to cluster their pretraining sequences.

The two AbAgym deep mutational scans \citep{Cia2025AbAgym}, EGFR and angiopoietin-2 binding, are grouped by mutated position, as for the Koenig scans, giving 36 and 20 groups. The $\Delta\Delta G$ set joins antibody--antigen entries from SKEMPI 2.0 \citep{Jankauskaite2019} and AB-Bind \citep{Sirin2016}. It is grouped by protein complex, producing 30 groups. The five Ginkgo developability assays are measured on the same panel of antibodies. Two are retention times, in hydrophobic interaction chromatography (HIC) and standup monolayer adsorption chromatography (SMAC). The others are affinity-capture self-interaction nanoparticle spectroscopy (AC-SINS), expression titer, and polyreactivity to Chinese hamster ovary (CHO) cell proteins. We use the five cross-validation folds released with the competition \citep{vanNiekerk2026} as the groups, which makes the outer folds the competition's own.

\subsection{Representations by domain}\label{sec:method:reps}

% TODO: check (TH lens): which concatenation step? The Methods say the internal evaluator's standalone scores split the budget and pick which representations are kept; say if these are the same scores.
Any representation that takes the form of a per-sample fixed-length vector can serve as an input representation in the adaptive hybrid search. The first step in the search is to rank how well each single representation does using the given evaluation. The distinction of ``best'' is dependent on the dataset as well as the downstream model. These scores and relative rankings are later used in the concatenation step of the feature selection process. 

% TODO: check (TH lens): how many samples got an all-zero row, per dataset and representation? They all share one row, so every model and distance sees them as identical.
If an input cannot be parsed or embedded, it gets an all-zero row, so that every representation of a dataset keeps the same rows. In computed descriptors, a missing or infinite value is set to 0, and values are stored unscaled. A pretrained model gives one vector per input, the mean of its last-layer token vectors with padding excluded, unless otherwise stated. 

\subsubsection{Small molecules}
The small-molecule representations in Table~\ref{tab:reps_smallmol} are the fingerprint, descriptor, graph and language-model families described in Chapter~\ref{chap:background}. The only additions are four sets of 3D shape and pharmacophore features, used as augmentation only. All four are computed from a single conformer per molecule, embedded with a fixed seed and optimized with the Merck molecular force field. A single conformer may misdescribe flexible molecules.

\subsubsection{Chemical reactions}
Reaction datasets use the three kinds of representation listed in Table~\ref{tab:reps_reactions}. The condensed graph of reaction (CGR) merges reactants and products into one graph in which the bonds that change are marked \citep{Varnek2005, Hoonakker2011}. Here it is hashed into a circular fingerprint after atom mapping \citep{Schwaller2021rxnmapper}. Reaction language models read the reaction SMILES directly \citep{Schwaller2021rxnfp, Sagawa2023ReactionT5}. Any molecular representation can also be applied to the reactants, to the product, or to their difference. A one-hot encoding of which aryl halide, ligand, base and additive was used carries no chemistry. It is kept because on plate data such identity features can predict yield about as well as chemical descriptors \citep{Chuang2018}.

\subsubsection{Antibodies}
Antibody representations, listed in Table~\ref{tab:reps_antibodies}, start from the heavy- and light-chain amino-acid sequences. Besides one-hot and BLOSUM62 encodings \citep{Henikoff1992}, most are frozen language models, either trained on all proteins or on antibody repertoires only. Most models embed each chain separately and join the two vectors, so their width doubles on datasets with both chains. Several representations read one model in different ways, for instance averaging over earlier layers or over the complementarity-determining region (CDR) residues only, with CDRs found by IMGT numbering \citep{Lefranc2003} using ANARCI \citep{Dunbar2016ANARCI}. Datasets of point variants of one parent antibody also use features that describe each mutation.

\subsection{Models and metrics}\label{sec:method:models}

The downstream models across hybrid experiments include random forest, XGBoost, LightGBM, an SVM, a DNN and a Gaussian process \citep{Rasmussen2005}. Each dataset uses the two that scored best in the earlier search of the whole dataset, defined in Table~\ref{tab:decided_grid}. The hyperparameters are kept consistent across datasets so the differences between scores come from the input. 
We tested the effects of hyperparameter tuning to see what we were missing. Additional file~\ref{app:add:tuning_base_reps} shows that on one outer fold of five datasets, tuning helped the SVM most and made the random forest worse on Buchwald--Hartwig yield.


We do not use the kNN as a downstream evaluator, however it is used across a variety of experiments using the hybrids. It takes $k = 5$ neighbors by default, lowered to the square root of the number of training samples when a fold has fewer than 25. 

One of the inputs to the hybrid search is an evaluation metric, which the downstream model gets scored on. For this research, we used RMSE for regression on small molecules and reaction yields in the label's units. We use Spearman $\rho$ for antibody regression as these assays rank candidates and their scales differ between laboratories. Binary classification uses AUROC, which does not depend on a decision threshold. 

The tables below report the mean over 5 folds. We do not use significance testing here, as 5 fold searches with challenging splits are not sufficient for such analysis. 

% TODO: CHECK compute sentence below ("a CPU node of a shared university cluster with 8 cores and 64 GB"): the processor model of the node is not stated and was not found in the repo; the QM9 node size comes from tests/slurm_scripts/runbook/WORKERS.md, not from the job logs.
Summed over its 5 outer folds, the search of one dataset took 2.3 hours (hERG $K_i$) to 28.2 hours (MDR1--MDCK efflux) on small molecules, and 11.1 hours on Buchwald--Hartwig yield. On antibodies it took 0.4 hours (CHO polyreactivity) to 38.6 hours (the anti-VEGF expression scan). Each fold ran as its own job on a CPU node of a shared university cluster with 8 cores and 64~GB of memory, except QM9, which needed 12 or more cores and at least 120~GB. The times count only each fold's last attempt, so a fold resumed from a stopped run is undercounted. One hERG $K_i$ fold recorded only 90 seconds.

\subsection{Comparators, controls and ablations}\label{sec:method:comparators}

Unless stated otherwise, every experiment below uses the dataset's grid in Table~\ref{tab:decided_grid}, its two downstream models, five outer folds and the dataset's own metric. From each fold's search it takes, for each downstream model, the two single representations and the two hybrids with the best scores on the training samples. Table~\ref{tab:experiment_register} lists every experiment of this chapter.

% TODO: check (TH lens): PCA and ensembles are related methods, not tasks; for example 'Comparisons against other ways of combining representations'.
\subsubsection{Comparisons against related tasks}

\paragraph{PCA}

% TODO: check (TH lens): after standardization a 2,048-bit ECFP4 carries about ten times the total variance of the 200 PDV descriptors, so wide representations dominate the components. Is that intended?
We compare the hybrid against the two best single representations as well as a PCA variant. We pool together the features across every single representation, standardize them on the training samples, and reduce the set by PCA \citep{Jolliffe2016} to each budget of the dataset's grid. The downstream model is fit on the principal components as they are. On each fold, the hybrid is compared with PCA at the hybrid's dictated budget. This way, neither side's width is chosen on the held-out samples. PCA cannot have more components than training samples, and where the budget is larger the two widths differ. 

\paragraph{Ensembles}
We also consider ensemble modeling of a particular form. We fit one model per representation and pool their predictions, a process referred to as late fusion \citep{Stahlschmidt2022}. As the hybrid fits a single model and a fused feature vector, it could be considered a form of early fusion. Every choice for the ensemble is made on an inner 3-fold group split of the outer training samples. The first ensemble uses the same downstream model on every representation, and the second uses each representation's own better model. Each picks its members, the top 3 representations or all of them, and one of seven ways to pool predictions, from the mean and median to trimmed, outlier-removed and score-weighted means. The mean is the high-level fusion of \citet{Turkina2025}, which averages the predictions of one random forest per fingerprint.

\subsubsection{Frequent and rare scaffolds}
Datasets above 2{,}000 samples are first reduced to about 2{,}000 by drawing whole scaffold families with a fixed seed. Scaffolds at or above the 80th percentile of group size form the frequent half, and the rest form the rare half. Each half gets its own search on each outer fold. The hybrid of a search on all the subset's training samples is also rebuilt and scored on each half. 

\subsubsection{Overfitting with the budget}

We use the standard list of budgets described in Table~\ref{tab:search_tiers} plus 4{,}000 and 8{,}000 features. At every budget, we rebuild the winning configuration on the outer training samples and score it on the held-out fold. We measure the selection bias at a budget by looking at the search's own score minus the held-out score. A positive value indicates that the search promised more than the held-out fold gave. We report how the bias changes with the budget, and the budget the search picks on each fold from its training samples alone. We do not compare hybrids wider than the training set against narrower ones by their best held-out budgets, because that choice reads the held-out labels on both sides.

A second check repeats each outer fold's search with one more fold removed from its training samples, for each of the other four folds. The held-out fold is the same and the two searches differ by one fold of training samples. Each search, however, picks its own budget, so the two hybrids can differ in width as well.

\subsubsection{Feature reduction at the same width}

On regression datasets, every method receives $B$ features from all single representations joined, fitted on the fold's training samples. The methods are the two best hybrids at budget $B$ from the fold's search, mRMR \citep{Peng2005}, recursive feature elimination \citep{Guyon2002} and $B$ random features. mRMR ranks features by mutual information and stops at 200 features for run time. It consequently has no result on any small-molecule dataset, where every budget is 250 features or more. Recursive elimination fits a 50-tree random forest of depth 5 and removes 10\% of the features at each step. Each of the dataset's two downstream models scores every method, and only the features differ.

\subsubsection{Internal evaluator}

One search per outer fold is run for each evaluator setting. The matched evaluator belongs to the downstream model's family, while a near-family evaluator pairs tree models with each other, the SVM with a Gaussian process, and the DNN with a narrower DNN. A far-family evaluator pairs tree models with an SVM, the SVM with LightGBM, and the DNN with $k$-nearest neighbors. A fourth setting scores the DNN with a wider DNN, and a fifth searches the dataset's own internal evaluators, listed in Table~\ref{tab:decided_grid}. Each setting's search picks its own budget, and the number of features can thus differ between settings. On every dataset, the SVM's near-family evaluator is a Gaussian process whose length scale started at 1. 

\subsubsection{Importance during training}

% TODO: check (TH lens): feature scaling helps SMAC with the DNN (0.261 to 0.357) but hurts it with the SVM (0.354 to 0.314); why per model? Code fits importance once on all the hybrid's features together; say so.
Importance is computed on the training samples by the method the search picked. Sample weighting is not run for the Gaussian process, which takes no per-sample weights, nor on multiclass tasks. The five conditions are:
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
    \item No weighting: the features as selected.
    \item Feature weighting: standardized features multiplied by the square root of their importance, with the weights normalized to average 1.
    \item Sample weighting: samples weighted by how well their label agrees with their 10 nearest neighbors in the rescaled space.
    \item Combined weighting: feature and sample weighting together.
    \item Above-median selection: only the features strictly above the median importance.
\end{enumerate}
The weight of sample $i$ is $1 - |y_i - \bar y_{N(i)}| / (\max y - \min y)$, clipped to lie between 0.1 and 1, where $\bar y_{N(i)}$ is the mean label of its 10 nearest neighbors by Manhattan distance. Tree splits depend only on the order of values \citep{Hastie2009}, so for tree models feature weighting differs from no weighting only in that zero-importance features drop out. The rescaling does change which samples are nearest neighbors, and so changes sample weighting. Sample weighting down-weights rather than removes a suspect label, unlike the neighbor-based filtering of Section~\ref{sec:flaws:neighbors}. We found no QSAR study that does this, and learned per-sample weights for noisy labels come from general machine learning \citep{shu2019metaweightnet}.


\subsubsection{Calibration}

Split-conformal 90\% prediction intervals are built on the training samples and scored on the held-out samples by the fraction of held-out labels they cover and by their mean width.

\subsubsection{Stability and blueprints}

Selection on fold $m$, for $m = 1, \dots, F$, gives a binary vector $\mathbf{z}_m \in \{0,1\}^d$. Here $d$ is the number of features in all single representations joined, not the size of the union of the selected features. We measure the stability of the selection with the index of \citet{Nogueira2018},
\[
\hat\Phi = 1 - \frac{\frac1d\sum_{f=1}^d s_f^2}{\frac{\bar k}{d}\left(1-\frac{\bar k}{d}\right)}, \qquad s_f^2 = \frac{F}{F-1}\hat p_f(1-\hat p_f).
\]

Here $\hat p_f$ is the fraction of folds that selected feature $f$, and $\bar k$ is the mean number of features selected. $\hat\Phi=1$ means the same features on every fold, and $\hat\Phi \approx 0$ is what random selection gives. Each fold picks its own budget, and the index allows for the resulting difference in set size through $\bar k$. To read a blueprint, each selected feature is traced to its single representation, and its importance in the fitted model is measured with SHAP: TreeSHAP for tree models \citep{Lundberg2020}, the DeepLIFT-based SHAP explainer for the DNN, and Kernel SHAP on 50 held-out samples for the SVM and the Gaussian process \citep{Lundberg2017}.

\subsection{Transfer, collaboration studies and separate sites}\label{sec:method:sites}

We explore various use cases of the adaptive hybrid searches and subsequent representations. First, we ask if the search settings produced by the hybrid on one dataset, contained in the blueprint, transfer to other datasets. Specifically, can you identify important features on a larger dataset and then transfer them to a smaller one? We test this using LogD, with 5,028 molecules, as the larger dataset and hERG $\mathrm{p}K_i$, with 1,412 molecules, as the smaller dataset. Both use the seven representations the two datasets share: Avalon, ChemBERT, ECFP4, GROVER, MHG-GNN, Mordred and SMI-TED. The source settings come from one search on every LogD molecule, none of which is in the target. The closest prior work, Meta-QSAR \citep{Olier2018}, chose the learning method and representation for a new QSAR dataset from results on over 2,700 others and, like this study, reused decisions rather than selected features. 

We also looked at comparisons run inside every held-out fold of the target: the best single representation, a hybrid searched on the target's training molecules as the upper reference, the source settings with features re-selected on the target, and the source's selected features applied unchanged. The last two ask separately whether the search decisions and the chosen features transfer. We use five folds grouped by scaffold family, RMSE in $\mathrm{p}K_i$ units and the two downstream models decided for both datasets, the DNN and SVM.

We then repeat existing data pipelines using the hybrids as the main representation, comparing its performance against the original. First, we repeat the activity-cliff QSAR setting of \citet{Dablander2023} on three targets, with five scaffold folds. Dopamine D2 (6,333 compounds) and factor Xa (3,605) come from ChEMBL, and the SARS-CoV-2 main protease (1,924) comes from the COVID Moonshot project. Each target's search draws on eight representations, which do not include GROVER or SMI-TED. The downstream models are LightGBM and the DNN, and the metric is the mean absolute error (MAE) in log units of activity.

We also count the molecules that a representation cannot tell apart, meaning that two or more molecules get an identical feature row, on every representation of the datasets in Table~\ref{tab:flaws:ties}, and ask whether a hybrid removes the problem. The representation with the most such molecules on hERG $K_i$, the WL graph kernel, is the target. Two hybrids add features of two partner representations to it. One adds ECFP4 and PDV, which are also blind to stereochemistry. The other adds ECFP4 and MHG-GNN, which together give no two hERG $K_i$ molecules the same row. We report the score of each hybrid against the graph kernel alone, and the number of molecules that still share a row in the hybrid.

Federated learning is a technique which trains one model across data held by several parties without moving the data \citep{McMahan2017}. It has been run across pharmaceutical companies \citep{Heyndrickx2024}, whose structures and assay results are commercial assets that cannot be pooled \citep{Hanser2023,Bassani2023}. Our collaborator, Lhasa Limited, uses the federated knowledge-distillation process of Table~\ref{tab:noise_sources} in Chapter~\ref{chap:background} \citep{Hanser2025}. We use their code for the fingerprints, site models and site clustering, and re-implement from it the step that combines the site models' predictions.

% TODO: DECIDE (federated): checked in tests/experiments/federated/federated_helpers.py (fed_fold_selection): the settings come from ONE search run centrally on every site's training molecules, labels and representations together, so the step that picks the settings is not federated; only the per-site feature selection after it is. Author to decide whether to keep the experiment.
We make a slight adjustment to the federated learning process, namely we run feature selection at each of these sites. A single search on the training molecules of all sites together chooses the settings (allocation method, importance method and internal evaluator). Each site then selects features with these settings on its own molecules and reports only feature indices, importance values and one score. A coordinator splits the total budget $B$ across sites in proportion to their scores, with at least one feature for each site whose selection succeeded. The main experiment below instead gives each site an even share, and no experiment compares the two splits, so we cannot say what the site scores buy. We replace a feature taken by another site with the site's next-highest ranked feature, keeping all features in the hybrid distinct. In related work, each client runs mutual-information feature selection on its own data and a server aggregates the results \citep{Cassara2022}.

We run all four separate-site experiments on Caco-2 and LogD, with five folds grouped by scaffold family and three seeds, the DNN and SVM, and budgets of 500 to 2{,}000 features. In the main one, each site holds the same molecules but one family of representations: substructure fingerprints, physicochemical descriptors or pretrained embeddings. It compares the best single family, picked on the training molecules, with one feature selection over all families, and both with one selection per family at an even share of the budget. When a family is narrower than its share, the rest goes to the other families. The even split can, as a result, build fewer features than one selection over all families. 


Three additional experiments, with flipped labels, with one representation per site and with features selected per cluster, are described in Additional file~\ref{app:add:separate_sites}.


\section{Results}

We begin with the simplest comparison: the hybrid against the representations whose features it's built from. Upon seeing it does in fact improve predictive performance, we then explore where the gain is coming from, looking at individual blueprints from hybrid searches. Finally, we explore some simple downstream applications of the hybrid as a standard molecular representation.

\subsection{Performance of the hybrid}\label{sec:method:results_single}

Every number in this section is a held-out score, in the dataset's own metric, over five outer folds unless stated otherwise.

\subsubsection{Against the best single representation}


Table~\ref{tab:hybrid_vs_best_single} shows that on small molecules the hybrid scores better than the best single representation on every dataset, with both downstream models. On the six regression datasets it does so on every fold. On hERG blocking the hybrid is ahead on most folds with each model.

% TODO: CHECK: Buchwald--Hartwig with random forest is an exception (hybrid 15.24 vs best-in-hindsight 14.2 RMSE), unless reactions are not counted as small molecules here.
On every small-molecule dataset in Table~\ref{tab:hybrid_vs_best_single}, the hybrid also beats, with both models, the single representation that was best on the held-out molecules. No user could make that choice in advance.

% TODO: check (TH lens): 'does not improve things globally' is followed by the hybrid beating everything (0.2775 against 0.298 RMSE); what is meant? Perhaps that the gain is not only from finding a better single representation.
It does not improve things globally. On Caco-2 with the DNN, for example, the best single representation in hindsight is PDV. PDV can only be picked after seeing the held-out molecules. It beats the representation picked on the training molecules, but the hybrid beats both, 0.2775 against 0.298 RMSE. To see which representations the hybrid relies on, we shuffled each representation's features in turn on the held-out molecules, as reported in Additional file~\ref{app:add:hindsight_shuffle}. On hERG $K_i$, Caco-2, LogD, rat microsomal clearance and MDR1--MDCK efflux, the drop in score spreads about equally over five to seven representations, none carrying a third of it. On QM9 with LightGBM and on hERG blocking the hybrid leans on one representation, which carries most of the score on QM9 and about half on hERG blocking. The hybrid still beats every single representation there.

Table~\ref{tab:hybrid_vs_best_single} gives the antibody results, which differ from one dataset to the next. On the anti-VEGF expression scan, one of the two largest antibody sets, the hybrid is ahead with the SVM on all five folds. On $\Delta\Delta G$ the mean gain is the largest in the chapter with both models, though the gain moves by more than its own size from fold to fold. On EGFR binding, expression titer and AC-SINS self-association the hybrid's mean is higher with both models, while it wins on only two to four of the five folds.

In an antibody representation, as in a molecular fingerprint, the features that matter are few and far between. The problem is even more complex, as an antibody is larger than a small molecule, and within a dataset the majority of the structure between antibodies remains constant. A language model's embedding of the whole sequence mixes the CDRs that vary with a framework that mostly does not. Many of the antibody representations in Table~\ref{tab:reps_antibodies} were built for this thesis to separate the two. On four antibody datasets the hybrid beats even the single representation that was best in hindsight, and on two of them that representation is one of ours. On the anti-VEGF expression scan it is the CDR readout of ESM3 with the SVM, and on $\Delta\Delta G$ it is the mutation features with the SVM and the change from the parent embedding with the DNN.

% TODO: check (TH lens): what is 'one and a half representations'? It is the effective number, 1 over the sum of squared shares of the score drop; define it here. It equals the count when all shares are equal.
In the $\Delta\Delta G$ hybrid, the representations built for this thesis carry most of the drop in score under shuffling with both models. On the other antibody datasets they carry much less, so they matter most where the antibodies differ by defined mutations. On AC-SINS self-association, another of the four, the hybrid draws mainly on two antibody language models, IgT5 and AbLang-2. Across the antibody datasets the hybrids draw on about one and a half to five representations by the same shuffling, also in Additional file~\ref{app:add:hindsight_shuffle}. On angiopoietin-2 binding the best representation in hindsight with the SVM, AntiBERTy, was never ranked first on any fold's training antibodies.

On nanobody thermostability, SMAC colloidal stability, HIC retention and CHO polyreactivity the hybrid adds nothing. On HIC retention it is behind with the Gaussian process, and here the search picked the same ESM-2 representation on every fold, so the single representation was not the problem. The thermostability label also pools five assays into one.
On angiopoietin-2 binding, whose labels were read off a published heatmap, Table~\ref{tab:hybrid_vs_best_single} shows the hybrid higher with both models on three of five folds. Antibody humanness is a task one representation already solves, so the hybrid has nothing to add.

On the Buchwald--Hartwig reactions in Table~\ref{tab:hybrid_vs_best_single}, the hybrid lowers the RMSE with LightGBM and raises it with the random forest, both by less than the spread across folds. Additional file~\ref{app:add:budget_per_fold} shows that the search drew on only two of the ten representations on every fold. These were the reaction fingerprint and the change in the MHG-GNN embedding from reactants to product. 

\subsubsection{Against ensembles, and many representations against few}\label{sec:method:results_ensembles}

% TODO: check (TH lens): per model, the SVM hybrid beats the same-model ensemble on all five small-molecule datasets it ran on; with the DNN the ensemble wins HKi, Caco-2, RLM and MDR1. Why? Also 'versus antibodies' means versus ensembles.
We score hybrids against late fusion ensembling. We find that an ensemble of one model per representation beats the hybrid with both models on most antibody datasets tested, seen in Table~\ref{tab:method:ensembles}. On the anti-VEGF expression scan the DNN ensemble is ahead of the hybrid on every fold. On small molecules the hybrid holds up better, the same-model ensemble beats it with both models only on hERG blocking, and on the Buchwald--Hartwig reactions. The hybrid is ahead of both ensembles on QM9 with the DNN and on hERG $K_i$ with the SVM, on every fold. The success of hybrids versus antibodies likely hinges on whether or not the interaction between features in different representations matters to the prediction task at hand. For antibodies, most of the representations used are variants of language models that inherently contain similar pieces of information. Averaging them through late fusion ensembling likely smooths out the noise of each model on a smaller training set. However, on small molecules, the representations vary in the type of information they contain, and one model on their selected features can more effectively use those interactions.

When we fix the budget, Table~\ref{tab:method:top_rep_even_split} shows that drawing from several representations beats a single one. Results for all budgets are listed in Additional file~\ref{app:add:top_rep_even_split}. Filled from the top-ranked representation alone, the budget scores worse than the search's hybrid at every budget searched, with both models, on most datasets with a result, and at all but one budget on the rest. The budget splits matter far less for small molecules, an even split over the same representations comes close to the search's split. The split matters more on antibodies, such that at the smallest budget, the search's split is well ahead on nanobody thermostability with the DNN, angiopoietin-2 binding with the SVM and SMAC colloidal stability with the DNN. The even split is well ahead on $\Delta\Delta G$ with the SVM and expression titer with the random forest, and on EGFR binding at every budget. On small molecules, the gain comes more from which representations are drawn on, and from ranking features within each one, than from the allocation rule that sizes their shares.

Out of the four small-molecule regression tasks tested, the hybrid also gains on rare scaffolds, such that each half is searched separately, as seen in Table~\ref{tab:method:scaffold_halves}. On the rare-scaffold half, the hybrid's gain is larger than on the frequent half with both models on LogD as well as Caco-2 and QM9 with specifically the DNN. On QM9 with LightGBM the gain is smaller on the rare half, and QM9 has a rare-half result on only two folds. On hERG $K_i$ the hybrid is ahead in both halves, by less on the rare half, and on hERG blocking it is ahead on the rare half with LightGBM only. On rare scaffolds few training molecules resemble the held-out ones, so a single representation has less to go on.

We compare the hybrids against a standard PCA on all the pooled features, and the results vary by model. With the SVM, the hybrid is ahead on every fold of hERG $K_i$, Caco-2, LogD, rat microsomal clearance and MDR1--MDCK efflux. With LightGBM and XGBoost, it is ahead on every fold of QM9 and hERG blocking. With the DNN the two methods are much closer. The hybrid is still ahead on every fold of QM9, but PCA is ahead on LogD, 0.392 against 0.409 RMSE, and the hybrid wins only two of the five folds of Caco-2. On hERG $K_i$, MDR1--MDCK efflux and rat microsomal clearance the two are nearly tied (within 0.0020 RMSE). Additional file~\ref{app:add:pca_disagreement} compares this with two other experiments, one of which disagrees on LogD and Caco-2.


% TODO: check (TH lens): many fold counts, no PCA table and no one-line take-home; also, why does PCA catch up only with the DNN?
On antibodies neither method is a clear winner. With the SVM, PCA is ahead on 4 of 5 folds of the anti-VEGF expression scan and angiopoietin-2 binding, and on 3 of 5 of $\Delta\Delta G$. With the DNN, PCA is ahead on 3 of 5 folds of the expression scan, and the hybrid on 4 of 5 of $\Delta\Delta G$, 0.354 against 0.239 Spearman.

\subsubsection{Overfitting of the search}\label{sec:method:results_overfit}

We must stop and ask the question, is this method considered overfitting? 

% TODO: check (TH lens): EGFR binding is an antibody set, not one of 'these'; give one score pair for 'small' bias (hERG Ki DNN 0.591 vs 0.592) and say why held-out beats search score on hERG Ki SVM and LogD.
With budgets searched up to 8,000 features, and at half to twice the number of training samples, the selection bias is small on the larger small-molecule datasets. On these datasets the folds also often pick a hybrid wider than their own training set, for example on every fold of EGFR binding with the SVM. The search's internal scores can choose between configurations with a few thousand molecules, but not with 150 to 540 training antibodies, where its choice is partly noise.
