On permuted class labels, selecting features on the full dataset before cross-validation gave \citet{Ambroise2002} an error close to zero, against close to chance with selection redone inside each fold. Apart from the search's own scores in Table~\ref{tab:method:search_optimism}, every hybrid score in this chapter is on samples the search never saw, so the gains above are not this bias. The bias still exists inside the search, and the numbers above show where it is large: on small datasets, where many configurations are compared on few samples \citep{Tibshirani2015}.

Table~\ref{tab:method:one_fold_fewer} shows that leaving one more fold of training samples out of each fold's search makes the held-out score worse. This holds on every small-molecule dataset with a result, with both models. On QM9, hERG $K_i$, Caco-2, LogD and MDR1--MDCK efflux it is worse by 0.009 to 0.037 RMSE. The two searches differ in two ways at once, the training samples and the budget each one picks, so we cannot say which of the two causes the loss. In short, selection bias does not explain the gains in this chapter, since every reported hybrid score is on samples the search never saw. Within the search, however, the bias is large on small datasets, and giving the search fewer training samples makes the hybrid worse.

\subsection{Sources of the gain}\label{sec:method:results_why}

We call into question whether the performance gain we observe across hybrids is more than just due to cutting the number of features. Could it be that the importance is not a factor at all? We also examine its relationship with the downstream model. We run some smaller checks including tuning, testing the search with new embeddings, and calibration.

\subsubsection{Selection at a fixed number of features}

On most small-molecule datasets the hybrid beats other selections of the same number of features, but not on the reactions or on several antibody datasets. We compare it with recursive feature elimination and random features over all single representations joined. Recursive elimination ranks all joined features in one list, a pooled ranking the hybrid avoids, as explained in Section~\ref{sec:method:searchspace}. Figure~\ref{fig:best_number_of_features} shows the hybrid with the lower error at every budget on QM9, Caco-2, LogD and MDR1--MDCK efflux. The exception is hERG $K_i$ with the DNN, where both alternatives beat the hybrid at the two largest budgets. On the Buchwald--Hartwig reactions recursive elimination is ahead at most budgets. On antibodies, Figure~\ref{fig:best_number_of_features_antibodies} shows that the hybrid beats random features at most budgets. Recursive elimination, however, is ahead on some antibody datasets, for example on CHO polyreactivity at every budget. Additional file~\ref{app:add:feature_reduction} also compares mRMR, which ran only at 100 features or fewer and gave mixed results against the hybrid.

\subsubsection{Dependence on the downstream model}

% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
We now look at the budget, meaning the number of features, that each search picked. The dots under each panel of Figures~\ref{fig:best_number_of_features} and~\ref{fig:best_number_of_features_antibodies} show the pick of each fold, and Additional file~\ref{app:add:budget_per_fold} lists them. We did not test whether these picks follow measurable properties of a dataset, so we only describe them.

% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
Many differences between datasets were set in advance by each dataset's search grid, which Table~\ref{tab:decided_grid} lists. For example, the QM9, LogD and hERG blocking searches could only rank features by LightGBM gain. SMAC colloidal stability, HIC retention, expression titer and CHO polyreactivity have fewer than 200 training antibodies, and their grids only held budgets larger than that. Dataset size also does not order the budgets. QM9, the largest dataset, mostly picked fewer features with the DNN than LogD did.

% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
Where the grid left a choice, the downstream model moved the pick as much as the dataset did. On Caco-2 and nanobody thermostability the search picked more features for the DNN than for the SVM. On LogD the DNN took the largest budget in the grid on every fold. It also picked large budgets when budgets up to 8,000 features were allowed, as Table~\ref{tab:method:search_optimism} lists. The SVM's smaller picks fit what Chapter~\ref{chap:flaws} showed for distances. The SVM's kernel is computed over all features, so features that carry no information dilute it, while a network can likely learn to ignore them.

% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
On angiopoietin-2 binding and $\Delta\Delta G$ the search picks at most a few hundred features. On expression titer the random forest's pick varies widely between folds. These small and unstable picks on small antibody datasets are the overfitting of the search described in Section~\ref{sec:method:results_overfit}. No single budget or representation count would suit every dataset and model, which is why both are searched.

% [CUTS 2026-10-10, item 22] Table tab:method:budget_per_fold moved unchanged to Additional file app:add:budget_per_fold.

% [TH lens edit 2026-10-10: AI text. Residual defined from tests/experiments/complexity/c09_formal_analysis.py (cells keyed by (model, budget), lines 249-260; residual = total minus model, budget and interaction sums of squares, line 308). Datasets where budget > model and Caco-2 43.6% read from tab:method:variance_shares. Take-home added.]
% SOURCE: /Volumes/seagate/kirby_results_2026-10-10b/complexity/c06_within_family_gradient/<ds>.json (RERUN-SWAP-DNN)
% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
We then ask how much of the search's own score depends on the downstream model and how much on the budget. On most datasets the downstream model moves the score more than the number of features does. Table~\ref{tab:method:variance_shares} splits the variance in score across a search's configurations into the downstream model, the budget, their interaction and a residual. The residual is the variance between configurations that share the same model and budget. It comes from the other settings of the grid, namely the importance method, the allocation method, the internal evaluator and the representation count. On LogD, for example, the model explains about half of the variance and the budget almost none. The budget explains more than the model on Caco-2, MDR1--MDCK efflux, hERG blocking, nanobody thermostability and antibody humanness. On Caco-2 most of the budget's effect sits in its interaction with the model, so it differs between the two models.

% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
Additional file~\ref{app:add:budget_by_capacity} shows that the budget does depend on the model's own settings. The SVM's setting $C$ controls how closely it may fit the training data. With a small $C$ of 0.01 or 0.1, the search picks the same budget on every fold of LogD, rat microsomal clearance, MDR1--MDCK efflux and Caco-2. With $C = 10$ it picks larger budgets on LogD and rat microsomal clearance, likely because a model allowed to fit more closely can use more features. The size of the DNN's hidden layers does not move its pick in the same way. On LogD it takes the largest budget in the grid at every size, on all folds but one.

Finally, we check the internal evaluator, the model that scores each single representation during the search and so sets its share of the budget. On most small-molecule datasets the choice of evaluator makes almost no difference, as Additional file~\ref{app:add:evaluator} reports. A cheaper model of another family ranks the single representations about the same way as the downstream model, so the search does not need the more expensive one. The exception is QM9 with LightGBM, where an SVM evaluator gives 0.363 RMSE against 0.346 for the matched one.

% [TH lens edit 2026-10-10: AI text. 'well ahead' replaced by 0.353 vs 0.299 from tab:method:evaluator in thesis_rework/stage1_takeaways_bullets/additional_files_body.tex; four-dataset claim re-checked there (matched best for both models on EGFR, HIC-R, Titer, SMAC).]
% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
On some antibody datasets the evaluator matters. On EGFR binding, HIC retention, expression titer and SMAC colloidal stability, the matched evaluator gives the best score with both models. Additional file~\ref{app:add:evaluator} gives an example. On EGFR binding with the SVM, the matched evaluator gives 0.353 Spearman, against 0.299 with a LightGBM evaluator.

\subsubsection{Using importance during training}

We decided to repurpose the importance scores computed in the hybrid search for downstream training tasks. These importance scores have minimal impact on small molecules. Table~\ref{tab:method:importance_training} shows the best of the four conditions gains little over the plain hybrid on any small-molecule dataset, and feature scaling makes LogD with the SVM worse. The selection has already used the importance scores, so on small molecules there is little left for them to add. The impact is a bit more varied on antibodies. Feature scaling helps colloidal stability with the DNN most, and keeping the above-median features also helps $\Delta\Delta G$ with the SVM. Both seemingly carry a regularization effect.

The hybrid process does not depend on existing representations, it can take in any. So we test what happens when we try to add a new small-molecule embedding to the search. A new embedding's features usually help on small molecules, but on several antibody datasets they add little. Additional file~\ref{app:add:new_embeddings} gives the full results. We compare the hybrid's selected features with Gaussian random features swapped into the same places in the same hybrid. No new search is run for the random features, so this is a weaker test than giving a search random features to choose from. On hERG $K_i$, Caco-2, MDR1--MDCK efflux and LogD the embedding's features beat random features for every embedding or all but one. On hERG blocking a few embeddings lose to random features with one of the two models. On EGFR binding and $\Delta\Delta G$ they win for most embeddings. On several other antibody datasets and the Buchwald--Hartwig reactions they win for fewer than half, so another language model of the same sequence adds little.


% TODO: check (TH lens): how much narrower, how much too few? The table is in the Additional files: LogD SVM width 1.403 against 1.652; angiopoietin-2 SVM coverage 0.720 against 0.804.
With 90\% conformal intervals, the hybrid's intervals on small molecules cover close to 90\% of the held-out molecules, as the single representation's do. Additional file~\ref{app:add:conformal} gives the coverage. These intervals are also narrower, for example on LogD with the SVM. On several small antibody datasets the hybrid's intervals cover too few held-out antibodies, fewer than the single representation's, for example on angiopoietin-2 binding and $\Delta\Delta G$ with the SVM. The calibration set is part of a training fold of a few hundred antibodies, and the hybrid chosen on those antibodies is optimistic about them, the same selection bias as above.


\subsection{Blueprints on specific datasets}\label{sec:method:results_blueprints}

We now consider the actual output of the search: the blueprint. 

Figure~\ref{fig:blueprints} shows one fold of each dataset, where several hybrids draw on all ten representations in similar amounts, for example on LogD with the DNN and on the anti-VEGF expression scan with the SVM. However, for other hybrids, one or two representations carry the feature space. On EGFR binding almost all its features come from ESM-2 run on the joined chains.


\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth,height=0.72\textheight,keepaspectratio]{figures_ch5_7/f03_blueprints.png}
\caption[Features taken from each representation.]{Number of features the hybrid took from each single representation, one bar per dataset, on the first outer fold with the dataset's first downstream model. Hatched segments are representations built for this thesis. Dataset short names as in Table~\ref{tab:dataset_short_names}.}
\label{fig:blueprints}
\end{figure}

% TODO: check (TH lens): an amide nitrogen is also bonded to three carbons and is not basic; check whether this ECFP4 bit excludes amides before crediting basicity to amine bits.
With this format, we gain chemical insight into some of the results. LogD is a good example of this, as LogD's hybrid for SVM contains 372 features, 38 of them Mordred descriptors, 36 ECFP4 bits and the rest from eight representations whose features carry no names. Among the descriptors are Crippen's calculated logP (SLogP), the number of basic groups (nBase), several surface areas binned by partial charge (PEOE\_VSA), and counts of secondary amine and aromatic nitrogen environments. Among the bits are tertiary amine environments, in which a nitrogen is bonded to three carbons (\texttt{CN(C)C}), and aryl chloride environments. Unlike logP, which counts only the neutral form of a molecule, logD at pH 7.4 counts every charge state. At that pH a basic amine is largely protonated, which lowers logD below logP \citep{Bhal2007}. A model of LogD should therefore need both lipophilicity and basicity, and the hybrid picked both on every fold. Lipophilicity comes from Mordred's SLogP, and basicity from Mordred's nBase and from ECFP4 amine bits.


\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.85\textwidth,height=0.6\textheight,keepaspectratio]{figures_ch5_7/f03b_blueprint_logd.png}
\caption[Features of the LogD hybrid picked on every fold.]{Number of features the SVM's LogD hybrid picked on all five outer folds. Mordred descriptors are grouped by descriptor family and ECFP4 bits by substructure. Every other representation is one bar.}
\label{fig:blueprint_logd}
\end{figure}

% TODO: check (TH lens): the table contradicts 'do not vary much': stability is 0.273 to 0.577 on small molecules, 0.144 and 0.156 on Buchwald--Hartwig, 0.063 to 0.270 on antibodies, where 0 is random selection.
The selected features do not vary much between folds on the same search. On the stability index of \citet{Nogueira2018} in Table~\ref{tab:method:one_fold_fewer}, the small-molecule hybrids score higher than the antibody and Buchwald--Hartwig hybrids, and all score well below 1. Many features are correlated within and across representations, so a feature on one fold can stand in for a different one on another; an index well below 1 therefore need not mean the hybrid fits noise.

\subsection{Extension studies}\label{sec:method:results_opig}

Table~\ref{tab:method:opig_qsar} compares the hybrid with the single representation the same search ranked first. For every target and model pair except factor Xa with LightGBM, the hybrid has the lower error. With the DNN on factor Xa it is lower on all five folds. These targets have many activity cliffs, which is where a single similarity-based representation struggles most, as discussed in Chapter~\ref{chap:flaws}.

On hERG $K_i$ the graph kernel leaves some molecules sharing a row. Adding features from ECFP4 and MHG-GNN lowers its RMSE with the SVM on all five folds, and no two molecules share a row, as Table~\ref{tab:method:shared_rows} shows. Adding ECFP4 and PDV instead improves it almost as much while some molecules still share a row. Most of the gain therefore comes from the information the added representations give every molecule, and little from breaking the ties.

\subsection{Features chosen across separate sites}\label{sec:method:results_sites}

% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
We now return to the federated learning setting outlined in Section~\ref{sec:method:sites}. There, data are held at separate sites and cannot be pooled, so feature selection has to run at each site. In our main experiment, every site holds the same molecules, but each computes only one family of representations, namely substructure fingerprints, physicochemical descriptors or pretrained embeddings. We compare three ways to choose features. The first uses only the best family. The second runs one selection over all families together, as if they could be pooled. The third runs one selection at each site, each with an even share of the budget.

% [TH lens edit 2026-10-10: AI text. 'well ahead' replaced by the table's LogD SVM values (0.43823 rounded to 0.438, 0.460, 0.493); added that the settings come from one pooled search (stated in sec:method:sites, her text), so only the selection is per site. Author's TODO DECIDE on keeping the experiment left untouched.]
% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
Selecting at each site does as well as one selection over all families, on Caco-2 and LogD with both models. Table~\ref{tab:method:family_sites} shows that both beat the best family and the best single representation. On LogD with the SVM, for example, one selection over all families gives 0.438 RMSE, against 0.460 for the best family. Selecting at each site can, however, end with a different number of features. As Section~\ref{sec:method:sites} describes, the search settings used here still come from one search on the training molecules of all sites together. Only the feature selection after that search runs at each site. Once the settings are chosen, each site can select on its own and send only feature indices.

\section{Conclusions}

This chapter asked how to select the best set of features for a given dataset and model, and whether the method carries over from small molecules to antibodies and reactions. No fixed choice of features or settings works everywhere, but a search can find a good one for each task.

% [alignment 2026-10-10: 'on most datasets' added: on hERG Ki with the DNN, recursive elimination and random features beat it at 1,000 and 2,000 features, as Ch8 says]
On small molecules the hybrid works. With both models, it beat the best single representation on held-out molecules on every dataset, for example from 0.450 to 0.409 RMSE on LogD with the DNN. It even beat the representation that turned out best on the held-out molecules, which no user could know in advance. The gain is not simply from using fewer features, since on most datasets the hybrid also beat other ways of selecting the same number. It comes from combining representations that carry different information. On LogD, the selected features describe lipophilicity and basicity, as a chemist would expect.

The best settings differed between datasets and models. They should therefore be searched for each new task rather than fixed in advance.

% TODO: ALIGN: 'on most of them' = most of the four antibody datasets where the hybrid won; Ch1, Ch8 and the Ch5 results say 'on most antibody datasets'. Pick one scope.
Beyond small molecules the results are mixed. On four antibody datasets the hybrid beat every single representation, but on most of them an ensemble of one model per representation did better. On the Buchwald--Hartwig reactions the hybrid gained nothing.

Ranking features by importance helps to build the hybrid, since the top-ranked features beat random features of the same number on most datasets. Using the same scores again to weight features during training adds little.

\label{sec:method:limitations}
The main limitation is that the hybrid needs data. Its gains are consistent on a few thousand samples but not on a few hundred, where the search's own scores overstate its results. Most of our antibody datasets are that small, so their results say as much about size as about antibodies. The search is also costly, and must be rerun whenever the data, representations or model change. The separate sites were simulated from two public datasets.

This chapter used the hybrid only as input to a model. Chapter~\ref{chap:similarity} asks whether it also gives better neighbors, and Chapter~\ref{chap:noise} whether it helps with noisy labels.

\chapter{Similarity in an adaptive hybrid representation}\label{chap:similarity}

\section{Introduction}

% TODO: check (TH lens): 'inherits this' is vague; he suggests 'is negatively affected by this'.
% TODO: check (TH lens): the thesis is about noisy labels; say here, in one sentence, why distance matters for noise (neighbor-based noise detection is only as good as its distance), not only in the last line.
% [author text, restored 2026-10-10]
Chapter~\ref{chap:method} set the stage for the adaptive hybrid representations. Taking a step back, Chapter~\ref{chap:flaws} showed that in most standard representations the distance between two molecules barely rises with the difference in their labels. Any method that trusts a molecule's neighbors inherits this, such as predicting a label from the nearest neighbors or finding a noisy label by comparing it with its neighbors' labels. The first idea behind the hybrid was to compute distances on the features relevant to the task alone, and this chapter tests that idea.

% TODO: check (TH lens): 'distance between hybrids' reads as distance between two hybrids, not between molecules in a hybrid; and 'relative' label difference, while Methods uses the absolute difference.
A hybrid can be built for similarity in two ways. In the first, the search of Chapter~\ref{chap:method} uses a nearest-neighbor model as its downstream model, and we measure distances in the hybrid it selects. In the second, the search ranks each candidate hybrid by how well each molecule's five nearest neighbors in it predict its label, and fits no downstream model. We ask two research questions:
\begin{enumerate}
    \item Do pairwise distance values between hybrids track their relative label differences better than distance computed using the single representations?
    \item Do the methods built on that distance work better in a hybrid? We test prediction from nearest neighbors, activity cliffs, Bayesian optimization, distance to the training molecules as a guide to prediction error, retrieval of neighbors and clustering.
\end{enumerate}

This chapter uses 14 datasets: six small-molecule, seven antibody and one reaction dataset. Antibody datasets outnumber small-molecule datasets, so we give every result by domain rather than pooled.

\section{Methods}\label{sec:similarity:methods}

% TODO: check (TH lens): why these six? If they were dropped after seeing results, say so; otherwise give the reason.
Datasets, folds (grouped by scaffold family for small molecules) and metrics are as in Chapter~\ref{chap:method}, with five outer folds. Of its 20 datasets, we leave out six: rat microsomal clearance, both anti-VEGF scans, angiopoietin-2 binding, antibody humanness and HIC retention. Table~\ref{tab:similarity:feature_spaces} has no weighted values on Caco-2, hERG blocking, hERG $K_i$, expression titer and SMAC colloidal stability, whose runs measured unweighted distances only. In each outer fold, everything an experiment compares is chosen from searches on that fold's training molecules alone, and then scored on its held-out molecules. Features are scaled on the training molecules: features that only take the values 0 and 1 are left as they are, and the rest are standardized. In the experiments, but not inside the searches, held-out values are cut to the training range as well, which stops a single extreme molecule from dominating a distance. Where a statement covers all 14 datasets, ``molecule'' also stands for an antibody or a reaction.

The fold split, the sample of molecule pairs, the label permutations, the random feature subsets and the k-means starts all use fixed seeds. The spread we report is therefore the spread across the five outer folds of one split. We report the mean over the five folds, and the per-fold values where the spread matters, without significance tests. The simulation of choosing molecules to measure is repeated over three starting sets instead.

\subsection{Feature spaces compared}\label{sec:similarity:spaces}


% TODO: check (TH lens): on which datasets is it not defined, and why? AC-SINS, SMAC, CHO polyreactivity, expression titer and DDG have empty similarity-hybrid cells.
% TODO: check (TH lens): why Euclidean here, when the similarity score and all results use Manhattan? The hybrid is then chosen under one distance and scored under others; say so, and why.
Each outer fold runs three hybrid-style searches on its training molecules. (1) The general search is the standard hybrid search we've discussed thus far. (2) The distance search, in which a k-nearest-neighbor model is used as the downstream model with Euclidean distance and five equally weighted neighbors. Its budgets are capped at 500 features due to computation limitations. And finally (3), the similarity search, described in Section~\ref{sec:similarity:hybrid_similarity}. From these three searches, we take the following feature spaces:
\begin{itemize}
    \item the nearest-neighbor model's best and second-best single representations, ranked by the distance search;
    \item each downstream model's best and second-best single representations, ranked by its general search;
    \item each downstream model's two best hybrids, four in all: the predictive hybrids;
    \item the top hybrid of the distance search: the distance hybrid;
    \item the top hybrid of the similarity search: the similarity hybrid, on the datasets where that search is defined.
\end{itemize}

Unless stated otherwise, ``the single representation'' in the results means the nearest-neighbor model's best single representation, and ``the hybrid'' means the distance hybrid. Retrieval and clustering also score one control: the principal components of all representations joined, with as many components as the hybrid has features.

% TODO: check (TH lens): this title reads like the whole chapter; the section defines the similarity search. Name its aim, e.g. 'Choosing features by how well neighbors predict'.
\subsection{Similarity in a hybrid}\label{sec:similarity:hybrid_similarity}

% [TH edit 2026-10-10: cross-reference to Ch5 search space]
We now describe the similarity search, which is the search of Chapter~\ref{chap:method} with a different score. Each configuration $\theta$ of that search fixes an importance method, an allocation method, a number of representations and a budget $B$, as described in Section~\ref{sec:method:searchspace}. Given single representations $R_1, \dots, R_m$, a configuration builds a feature set $S_\theta \subset R_1 \cup \dots \cup R_m$ with $|S_\theta| \le B$. The search returns $S_{\theta^*}$, where $\theta^* = \arg\max_\theta s(S_\theta)$ and $s$ is the search's score. In the general search, $s$ is a downstream model's score on the search's own held-out folds. In the similarity search, $s(S) = q_5(S)$, namely how accurately each molecule's label is predicted from its five nearest neighbors in $S$, and no downstream model is fitted. All other settings, and the folds, are shared with the general search. When the budget is split between representations, this neighbor score also replaces the internal evaluator.

% [TH edit 2026-10-10: cross-reference to Ch4 (repeat of Ch4 text)]
As discussed in Chapter~\ref{chap:flaws}, \citet{Zheng2000} and \citet{Itskowitz2005} chose descriptors to maximize nearest-neighbor prediction within one descriptor set. We modify this idea by choosing features across several representations. The similarity hybrid tests whether choosing features for how well the neighbors predict a label does better than choosing them for a model's prediction.

% TODO: check (TH lens): does one ECFP bit weigh as much as a descriptor like logP in this distance? 'Less' by how much? The reader needs to know whether fingerprints are pushed aside.
% [TH edit 2026-10-10: cross-reference to Ch4]
The score uses the Manhattan distance of Chapter~\ref{chap:flaws}, on features scaled as above. Each standardized feature adds a term of similar size to this distance. A representation's weight in the distance is therefore roughly its number of features divided by the budget. Features that only take the values 0 and 1, such as fingerprint bits, add less per feature. The neighbor score uses no importance weighting, and we did not compare its Manhattan distance and inverse-distance weights against alternatives.

We compute the score on the held-out molecules of each fold of the search, inside the outer training fold, in order to rank candidates. These molecules act as each other's neighbors. Their groups (scaffold families, for small molecules) are split into up to 5 parts, and each molecule's 5 nearest neighbors are drawn from the other parts. A neighbor from the same scaffold family is an easy match and would inflate the score. This is the only place in the chapter where a molecule's own group is kept out of its neighbors. Each neighbor is weighted by the inverse of its distance \citep{Dudani1976}:
\begin{equation}
\hat{y}_i = \frac{\sum_{j\in N_5(i)} y_j / d_{ij}}{\sum_{j\in N_5(i)} 1/d_{ij}}.
\label{eq:similarity_objective}
\end{equation}
% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
A neighbor at distance 0 receives all of the weight, shared equally among ties. On regression data, $q_5$ is the Spearman correlation $\rho(\hat{\mathbf{y}}, \mathbf{y})$. On binary data, $\hat{y}_i$ is the distance-weighted fraction of neighbors in class 1, and $q_5 = 2(\mathrm{AUROC} - 0.5)$. Both scores put chance at 0, which the budget split needs, as it divides each representation's score by the sum of the scores. Negative scores are first shifted so that the lowest is 0. The related modelability index of \citet{Golbraikh2014}, discussed in Chapter~\ref{chap:flaws}, uses one nearest neighbor and a hard class match.

\subsection{Nearest-neighbor settings}\label{sec:similarity:knn_settings}

% TODO: check (TH lens): the outer folds are grouped by scaffold family, so no training molecule should share a held-out molecule's group. This sentence reads as train/test overlap; say which is true.
Every experiment that makes predictions based off its neighbors' labels or that measures retrieval scores each feature space at every combination of the settings in Table~\ref{tab:knn_settings}, instead of at one chosen setting. A held-out molecule's prediction is the weighted mean of its neighbors' labels,
\begin{equation}
\hat{y}_i = \frac{\sum_{j \in N_k(i)} w_{ij}\, y_j}{\sum_{j \in N_k(i)} w_{ij}},
\label{eq:knn_weights}
\end{equation}
where $N_k(i)$ is the $k$ training molecules nearest to $i$ under distance $d$, and $w_{ij}$ is the neighbor weight. On classification data, each class receives the summed weight of its neighbors. Unlike in the similarity score, a training molecule from the held-out molecule's own group (scaffold family, for small molecules) can be one of its neighbors. The one experiment that does not use these settings is the one on all representations joined, described in Section~\ref{sec:similarity:experiments}.

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering
\small
\begin{adjustbox}{max width=\textwidth}
\begin{tabular}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.17\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.40\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.22\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.13\linewidth}@{}}
\toprule
Setting & Levels & Source & Status \\
\midrule
Distance $d$ & Manhattan; cosine; Minkowski with $p = 0.5$, $d(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}) = \big(\sum_j |x_j - y_j|^{0.5}\big)^{2}$ & Chapter~\ref{chap:flaws} (Manhattan, cosine); this chapter (Minkowski) & Reused \\
Neighbors $k$ & 5, 10, 20 & Standard & Reused \\
Neighbor weight $w_{ij}$ & $1/r_{ij}$, where $r_{ij}$ is the rank of $j$ among the $k$ neighbors of $i$, nearest first; $1/d_{ij}^2$ & None found; \citet{shepard1968two} & New; reused \\
Importance weighting & None; $\mathbf{w} = (\mathbf{s}/\bar{s})^{a}$; $\mathbf{w} = \mathbf{s}^{1/2}$ & \citet{Wettschereck1997} & Modified \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{adjustbox}
\caption[Nearest-neighbor settings]{Nearest-neighbor settings. One row is one setting, with its levels; every combination of levels is scored. In the last row, $\mathbf{s}$ is the vector of feature importances, $\bar{s}$ its mean and $a$ an exponent set by the distance. Minkowski distance is left out on datasets above 5,000 molecules (hERG blocking, LogD and QM9).}
\label{tab:knn_settings}
\end{table}

We also test importance weighting, a modified form of feature-weighted nearest neighbors \citep{Wettschereck1997}. Before the distance is computed, each feature is multiplied by a weight, $\mathbf{x}' = \mathbf{x} \odot \mathbf{w}$. The weights come from the importance scores of the hybrid search, with negative importances set to 0. For a single representation, the importance $\mathbf{s}$ is computed on the fold's training molecules, in most experiments with LightGBM gain. For a hybrid, it is the importance its search used to select the features. There are two exceptions. For distance against label difference, LightGBM gain is refitted on all of the hybrid's features together. For retrieval, the search's importance method is rerun on the hybrid as a whole. We use two formulas for $\mathbf{w}$:
\begin{itemize}
    \item for distance against label difference and the best number of features, $\mathbf{w} = (\mathbf{s}/\bar{s})^{a}$, with $a = 1$ under Manhattan distance, $a = 0.5$ under cosine and $a = 2$ under Minkowski with $p = 0.5$;
    \item for every other experiment that weights, $\mathbf{w} = \mathbf{s}^{1/2}$ under every distance.
\end{itemize}
% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
The two formulas weight features differently. In the first, the exponent $a$ makes each feature's term in the distance scale with its relative importance, $s_f/\bar{s}$, under all three distances. In the second, under Manhattan distance, a feature four times as important as average counts only twice as much. The weighted distances of Sections~\ref{sec:similarity:results_similarity} and~\ref{sec:similarity:results_knn} are therefore the same only under cosine.

% [TH edit 2026-10-10: cross-reference to Ch4 instead of repeating the definition]
\subsubsection{Distance against label difference}
% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
This experiment asks how well distance in a feature space follows the label. It uses the measure of Section~\ref{sec:flaws:similarity}, here on pairs of held-out molecules. For each pair, we compute the Spearman correlation between their distance and their absolute label difference. A positive value means that molecules further apart differ more in their labels. We measure it in each representation alone, unweighted, under every distance of Table~\ref{tab:knn_settings}. We then measure it in the feature spaces of Section~\ref{sec:similarity:spaces}, unweighted and weighted.

% [TH lens edit 2026-10-10: Split long sentences; states that the held-out fold's own scores are not used but its labels were neighbor labels in the other folds (checked: tests/experiments/similarity/s05_knn_prediction.py docstring, 'other folds' held-out scores'; standard 5-fold CV). Counts unchanged.]
\subsubsection{Prediction from nearest neighbors}
% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
We predict each held-out molecule's label from its nearest training molecules, as in Equation~\ref{eq:knn_weights}, and compare hybrid and single representation in two ways. The first comparison was fixed before the run. It uses Manhattan distance, 5 neighbors weighted by inverse rank, and no importance weighting. The second comparison picks a combination for each held-out fold from the held-out scores of the other four folds. The held-out fold's own scores are never used in this pick. In those four folds, however, the held-out fold's molecules were training molecules, so their labels served as neighbor labels. The hybrid side chooses among the four predictive hybrids, the distance hybrid and the similarity hybrid, weighted or unweighted, at every setting of Table~\ref{tab:knn_settings}, up to 216 combinations. The single side chooses among the nearest-neighbor model's two best single representations, unweighted, up to 36 combinations. The two sides therefore differ in two ways at once. The hybrid side has five or six times as many combinations, and only it can use importance weighting. The comparison fixed before the run has neither difference.

\subsubsection{Importance weighting in four models}
% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
We score every feature space with and without importance weighting in four models that use a distance. These are prediction from nearest neighbors, a Gaussian process and an SVM with RBF kernels on regression data, and q-RASAR \citep{Banerjee2022}. In q-RASAR, each downstream model receives four extra features from the nearest training molecules, namely their mean label and its spread, the distance to the nearest one and their mean distance. The four extra features are computed under the three neighbor settings that rank best on the training molecules. A separate experiment weights all representations joined, on regression datasets, once per importance method.

% [TH edit 2026-10-10: cross-reference to Ch4 cliff definition; sentences split]
\subsubsection{Activity cliffs}
% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
An activity cliff is a pair of structurally similar molecules with very different activity \citep{Stumpfe2012,vanTilborg2022}. Only the two hERG datasets measure an activity, but we use the term on all six small-molecule datasets. We define cliffs as in Section~\ref{sec:flaws:similarity}. Two molecules are similar when their ECFP4 Tanimoto similarity is at least 0.55 \citep{Hu2015}. We set the gap so that 15\% of each outer fold's training molecules are cliff molecules. A training molecule is a cliff molecule if it is in a similar pair whose labels differ by more than the gap, or fall in different classes. A held-out molecule is one if at least two similar training molecules have labels spanning more than the gap, so its own label is never read. Each downstream model is fitted on its two best single representations and two best hybrids, and scored separately on cliff and other held-out molecules. The main comparison is the first hybrid against the best single representation.

% [TH lens edit 2026-10-10: Names the applicability domain (Tropsha2010 already cited in Ch4, sec:flaws:consequences) and the toxicity link; long sentence split.]
\subsubsection{Distance against prediction error}
% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
\citet{Sheridan2004} found that a molecule's similarity to the training molecules tracks how well it is predicted. Distance to the training molecules is also a common way to define an applicability domain \citep{Tropsha2010}, as noted in Section~\ref{sec:flaws:consequences}. An applicability domain marks the molecules whose predictions can be trusted. We test whether distance tracks error by fitting each downstream model on all representations joined and on its first hybrid. The error on a held-out molecule is the absolute error on regression data. On classification data, it is one minus the predicted probability of the true class. We correlate this error, by Spearman, with the mean distance to the 5, 10 or 20 nearest training molecules. We do this under every distance, in each feature space of Section~\ref{sec:similarity:spaces}, unweighted and weighted.

\subsubsection{Retrieval and clustering}
% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
Retrieval scores each held-out molecule by its label gap, namely the weighted mean absolute difference between its label and those of its nearest training molecules, at every setting of Table~\ref{tab:knn_settings}. On classification data, the gap is one minus the weighted share of neighbors in its class. For clustering, we fit k-means \citep{Lloyd1982} with 3, 5 and 10 clusters on the training molecules, and assign each held-out molecule to one. Label coherence is one minus the mean within-cluster standard deviation of held-out labels, divided by their overall standard deviation. On classification data, it is the mean share of the largest class per cluster. A lower label gap and a higher label coherence are better, and clusters with fewer than two held-out molecules are left out.

\subsubsection{Three smaller checks}
% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
% scikit-learn version: 1.6.1, pinned for the server runs by constraints.txt and setup.sh (checked 2026-10-10); the version installed on the server was not read.
% QM9 added 2026-10-10: s11_bayesian_optimization/qm9.json (10 Oct 00:34). results.hunt: qm9 'low' (from the metric direction, no label direction recorded), herg_ki 'low', buchwald_hartwig 'high'.
The first of three smaller checks finds the best number of features. At every budget of its search, we take each downstream model's top hybrid and record two scores, its prediction score and how well its distances track label differences. We then find where each peaks.

% TODO: check (TH lens): why the lowest? On hERG Ki the lowest pKi is the weakest binder, the safest molecule; say so. On QM9 the direction was a default, not a choice.
The second check simulates choosing which molecules to measure next, a form of Bayesian optimization, on hERG $K_i$, QM9 and Buchwald--Hartwig only. The search looks for the lowest label on hERG $K_i$ and QM9, and the highest yield on Buchwald--Hartwig. It starts from a set of whole groups holding at least 5\% of the molecules. The groups are scaffold families on hERG $K_i$ and QM9, and additives on Buchwald--Hartwig. A surrogate model then picks 100 molecules one at a time by expected improvement \citep{Jones1998}. We repeat this from three starting sets. The score is the area under the curve of the best label found so far.

The third check asks whether the features a hybrid keeps carry more label information than other features of the same number. For each representation a predictive hybrid draws from, we compare the features it kept against principal components and against a random subset of the same size. Each block of features is reduced to at most 50 principal components. We sum a mutual-information estimate over the components, and subtract the mean over three label permutations. We use the estimators of scikit-learn 1.6.1, which follow \citet{Kraskov2004} for continuous labels and \citet{Ross2014} for classes. This sum counts information shared by two components twice.

% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
\section{Results}
We first ask whether distance in a hybrid tracks label differences more closely than in a single representation, and then whether the methods built on that distance gain from it.

\subsection{Distance against label difference}\label{sec:similarity:results_similarity}

% [TH lens edit 2026-10-10: Says where the pair counts come from; 30 was wrong on four datasets. Take-home added.]
% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
As in Chapter~\ref{chap:flaws}, distance in a single representation says little about how far apart two labels are. For each dataset, we score every pair of one single representation and one distance from Table~\ref{tab:knn_settings}, giving 20 to 30 pairs per dataset. Table~\ref{tab:similarity:single_spread} shows that even the best pair stays weak on every dataset, and varies widely between folds. This best pair is also picked with hindsight on the held-out molecules.

% [TH lens edit 2026-10-10: 'representation' -> 'pair of representation and distance'; 'about half' -> 15 of 30; 'other five small-molecule + Buchwald' widened to the other nine datasets. % [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
On five datasets, some pairs of representation and distance have a correlation at or below zero, so molecules further apart are not more different in their labels. These are MDR1--MDCK efflux, EGFR binding, CHO polyreactivity, AC-SINS self-association and SMAC colloidal stability. SMAC colloidal stability is the weakest, with half of its 30 pairs at or below zero.

% TODO: check (TH lens): a space chosen with the labels is expected to be smoother than fixed fingerprints or pretrained embeddings. Add a task-trained baseline (e.g. a DNN's last hidden layer), or state this as a limit?
% [TH lens edit 2026-10-10: States that only the hybrid side is task-learned, and points to the weighted single representation as the label-aware comparison (importances on training molecules, Methods importance-weighting paragraph).]
% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
% Check 2026-10-10 (fold claim, no table): s02 folds[*].comparisons["knn distance hybrid - knn base rank 1"], Manhattan, unweighted, single against hybrid.
% LogD hybrid higher in all 5 folds; Caco-2 hybrid lower in folds 2 (0.168 against 0.133) and 4 (0.132 against 0.094).
Table~\ref{tab:distance_tracks_activity} shows that distance in the distance hybrid tracks label differences more closely than in the single representation on all 14 datasets. On LogD the hybrid is higher in every fold, while on Caco-2 it is lower in two of the five. The two sides are not built the same way. The hybrid's features are chosen using the training labels. Every single representation is built without these labels, either as a fixed fingerprint or descriptor set or as a pretrained embedding. The weighted single representation of Table~\ref{tab:similarity:feature_spaces} also uses the training labels, through its feature importances, and is compared below.

\input{figures_ch5_7/tab_distance_tracks_activity}

% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
Distance in the predictive hybrids, built for prediction rather than for distance, does not always track label differences more closely than in the single representation. Table~\ref{tab:similarity:predictive_hybrids} shows that on more than half the datasets, at least one predictive hybrid falls below its model's best single representation. On CHO polyreactivity all four do. A hybrid used for distance should therefore come from the distance search.

Weighting the single representation by feature importance closes most of its gap to the distance hybrid on most datasets. Table~\ref{tab:similarity:feature_spaces} shows that on LogD, AC-SINS self-association and Buchwald--Hartwig, it even passes the unweighted distance hybrid. % [TH lens edit 2026-10-10: Restates one pair so the reader need not flip between tables. Values from tab:similarity:single_spread (0.229) and tab:similarity:feature_spaces (0.197, 0.277); other three datasets checked against the same tables.]
% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
We also compare against a stronger single representation. This is the pair of representation and distance whose distance best follows the label, picked with hindsight, as in Table~\ref{tab:similarity:single_spread}. This pick beats the unweighted distance hybrid of Table~\ref{tab:similarity:feature_spaces} on Caco-2, EGFR binding, expression titer and CHO polyreactivity. On Caco-2, for example, it reaches 0.229 Spearman against 0.197. The similarity hybrid, at 0.277, beats it in turn.

% [TH lens edit 2026-10-10: Take-home sentence added.]
% Checked 2026-10-10: QM9 s08 is in the table; the LogD example still stands (DNN rank-1 RMSE 0.4146 at 500, 0.4076 at 2,000; Spearman 0.2315 to 0.1535).
% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
The number of features at which the DNN's hybrid predicts best is often not the one where its distances best track label differences. As Table~\ref{tab:similarity:feature_count} shows, prediction usually peaks at more features than distance does, though on a few datasets the two peak together or prediction peaks at fewer. On LogD, for example, the hybrid predicts better at 2,000 features than at 500, while its distance correlation falls from 0.231 to 0.154 Spearman.

\subsection{Prediction from nearest neighbors}\label{sec:similarity:results_knn}

Table~\ref{tab:nearest_neighbour_prediction} gives the results at the setting fixed before the run. There, the distance hybrid predicts from the five nearest neighbors more accurately on all six small-molecule datasets. Scores for each fold are in Additional file~\ref{app:add:similarity_knn_by_setting}.

\input{figures_ch5_7/tab_nearest_neighbour_prediction}

In Table~\ref{tab:similarity:knn_picked_again}, the feature space and setting are picked again for each fold, and the hybrid still predicts more accurately on all six small-molecule datasets. This second comparison favors the hybrid, however, as the hybrid picks from more combinations and alone can use importance weighting, as explained in Section~\ref{sec:similarity:experiments}.

% [TH lens edit 2026-10-10: Removes the clash with 'depends far more on the dataset than on how the neighbors are found'.]
% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
On the antibody datasets and Buchwald--Hartwig, the two comparisons disagree about where the hybrid wins. At the fixed setting of Table~\ref{tab:nearest_neighbour_prediction}, the hybrid loses on $\Delta\Delta G$, AC-SINS self-association and Buchwald--Hartwig. Table~\ref{tab:similarity:knn_picked_again} shows that with the setting picked again for each fold, it wins only on EGFR binding, $\Delta\Delta G$ and SMAC colloidal stability. SMAC colloidal stability gains in both, from 0.092 to 0.291 Spearman with the setting picked again.

% TODO: check (TH lens): improves and gets worse by how much? Give the two LogD SVM RMSE pairs, as 'from X to Y RMSE'.
% TODO: check (TH lens): weighting helps prediction from nearest neighbors but not the SVM. Why? Was the SVM's RBF length scale re-tuned after weighting? Give a reason, or say it is not explained.
% Checked 2026-10-10 against 10-10b: s10 qm9.json and adme_mdr1.json are complete; QM9 and MDR1-MDCK efflux rows added to both tables.
% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
Multiplying each feature by its importance mostly helps prediction from nearest neighbors. Table~\ref{tab:importance_weighting} marks the exceptions with $^{\dagger}$. In the other downstream models, weighting the single representation gains little or loses, as seen for the SVM in Table~\ref{tab:similarity:svr_weighting}. Giving each model four extra features taken from a molecule's nearest neighbors helps or hurts depending on the setting. On LogD, the SVM improves with ten neighbors under cosine distance and gets worse with twenty.

\caption[Importance weighting in prediction from five nearest neighbors]{Prediction of each held-out molecule from its five nearest training molecules, with and without features multiplied by their importance, in the nearest-neighbor model's best single representation and in the distance hybrid. One row is one dataset; values are means over five outer folds, Manhattan distance, neighbors weighted by the inverse of their rank. The arrow gives the direction of the metric; Buchwald--Hartwig RMSE is in percentage points of yield. $^{\dagger}$ marks a weighted value worse than the unweighted value beside it.}
\label{tab:importance_weighting}
\end{table}

Whether the hybrid wins depends far more on the dataset than on how the neighbors are found, as Additional file~\ref{app:add:similarity_knn_by_setting} shows. Weighting both the single representation and the hybrid shrinks the gain wherever the hybrid wins, on LogD from 0.089 to 0.025 RMSE. Weighting accomplishes part of what the hybrid does. This makes sense, as it carries some mechanism of feature importance without a full re-selection of features. Where the hybrid loses, like on $\Delta\Delta G$ and AC-SINS self-association, that loss is smaller than the spread across folds. Some of these datasets have only a few hundred samples, so we cannot entirely separate dataset size from domain. This would require further investigation on larger antibody datasets.

On the antibody datasets, the distance used changes how much closer the labels of the hybrid's neighbors are, which Section~\ref{sec:similarity:results_other} also compares. Additional file~\ref{app:add:similarity_retrieval_by_setting} gives every setting. On $\Delta\Delta G$ the hybrid's neighbors have labels further apart under all three distances, though at the fixed setting by less than the spread across folds. On the other antibody datasets except EGFR binding, cosine distance gives the hybrid its smallest lead. On SMAC colloidal stability it reverses the result, from labels 0.028 closer under Manhattan distance to 0.057 further apart.

We also ran a simulated Bayesian optimization on hERG $K_i$, QM9 and Buchwald--Hartwig. Starting from a few known molecules, a model repeatedly picks the next molecule to measure, and we score how quickly it finds the best labels over 100 picks. This gives no clear advantage to the hybrid. On each dataset, the best of 20 hybrid simulations narrowly beat the single representation, which was tried only once. On QM9, for example, they scored 253.4 against 260.1, where lower is better, but the other 19 hybrid simulations all did worse than the single representation. The spread between starting sets was also larger than the gap between the two.

\subsection{Activity cliffs}\label{sec:similarity:results_cliffs}

When handling noise, we run into the issue determining if a label that is presumably flagged as some kind of outlier is noise or an activity cliff. If it's noise, you don't want any model fitting on it, and you want to either remove it, down weight it, or correct the label. But if it's an activity cliff, it has the potential to carry very critical chemical information. Because of this, we took some extra steps to try to protect the activity cliffs from being affected by the noise methods in various ways. 

We report activity cliffs on the six small-molecule datasets only, using the definition of Section~\ref{sec:similarity:experiments}. The antibody datasets have too few cliff antibodies to score, for example 11 of the 429 held-out antibodies on $\Delta\Delta G$. Held-out molecules are flagged by the looser rule of Section~\ref{sec:similarity:experiments}, so on some datasets far more than 15\% of them are labelled as cliffs. On LogD and hERG blocking, this is about a third to a half of the held-out molecules in each fold. QM9 has the fewest, at most 13 of about 1,996 per fold.

On LogD, Caco-2, hERG $K_i$ and hERG blocking, each model's first hybrid beats its best single representation on both cliff molecules and the other molecules in Table~\ref{tab:similarity:cliffs}. On Caco-2, hERG $K_i$ and hERG blocking the gain is larger on cliff molecules. On LogD it is slightly smaller, but both models gain on cliff molecules in every fold.

% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
QM9 and MDR1--MDCK efflux have too few cliff molecules to judge, 48 and 49 in all, and only two folds on each hold the 10 needed for a score. On both, Table~\ref{tab:similarity:cliffs} shows the hybrid is better on the other molecules. On cliff molecules it is worse or level, but each model's hybrid wins one of the two folds and loses the other.

\section{Conclusions}\label{sec:similarity:conclusions}

% TODO: ALIGN: the headline (six small-molecule datasets: distance tracks labels, neighbors predict better) is crowded by details: 'similarity hybrid has a value on nine datasets', the four cliff datasets, and the 0.312 vs 0.309 pair.
Chapter~\ref{chap:flaws} found that the distance computed using standard representations barely rises with a change in labels, as it should according to the SPP. Across the six small-molecule datasets, distance in a hybrid built for distance tracks label differences more closely, and its nearest neighbors predict better and have closer labels. Each model's first hybrid also predicts cliff molecules better on LogD, Caco-2, hERG $K_i$ and hERG blocking. The similarity hybrid has a value on nine datasets. On these, Table~\ref{tab:similarity:feature_spaces} shows it tracking label differences better than the unweighted distance hybrid only on Caco-2 and hERG blocking. With both weighted, it is also slightly ahead on QM9 and LogD, for example 0.312 against 0.309 Spearman on LogD.

% TODO: ALIGN: 'However, the hybrid's distance tracks prediction error worse on hERG blocking' is a single-dataset aside in the middle of the importance-weighting takeaway. Cut or move?
There is some benefit to weighting features by importance, it accomplishes a portion of what the hybrids do. On LogD, feature importance weights make the single representation track label differences better than the unweighted hybrid. It doesn't beat the weighted hybrid, though. However, the hybrid's distance tracks prediction error worse on hERG blocking. Except on QM9 and Buchwald--Hartwig, its features also carry no more label information than the same number of principal components.

% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
On the antibody datasets and Buchwald--Hartwig, distance in the hybrid also tracks label differences more closely, but the methods built on it do not always gain. With the setting picked again, the hybrid predicts worse from nearest neighbors on nanobody thermostability, AC-SINS self-association, CHO polyreactivity, expression titer and Buchwald--Hartwig. It retrieves neighbors with less close labels on $\Delta\Delta G$ and Buchwald--Hartwig.


% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
Methods that find noisy labels by comparing each label with its neighbors' labels rest on the distance studied here. Chapter~\ref{chap:noise} tests whether the hybrid's distance makes noisy labels easier to find and to act on.

\chapter{Detecting and handling label noise}\label{chap:noise}

\section{Introduction}\label{sec:noise:intro}

% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
In Chapter~\ref{chap:similarity}, on all six small-molecule datasets, distance in a hybrid built for distance tracked the label more closely than distance in a single representation, and nearest-neighbor prediction improved. Here we ask whether that helps with label noise. Can we find the noisy labels in a QSAR dataset, and does acting on them improve a model's held-out score? We ask this on labels damaged on purpose, where we know which labels were damaged and by how much. We also ask it on the labels as delivered, which carry an unknown amount of experimental error.

% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
Every method here depends on a feature space, because a detector compares a molecule's label with its neighbors' labels or with a model's prediction. Features unrelated to the label still add to the distance, as explained in Chapter~\ref{chap:flaws}, and the adaptive hybrid representation of Chapter~\ref{chap:method} leaves them out. For each method we therefore ask whether it improves the held-out score, and whether it improves the hybrid more than the best single representation.

% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
We sort the methods into noise detection, noise mitigation and noise-robust training, as defined in Table~\ref{tab:noise_terms}. We expand the sweep of twelve published methods from Chapter~\ref{chap:flaws}, as discussed in Section~\ref{sec:lnl_motivation}. We also add methods that select molecules by a known flag, such as a label at an assay limit, which no published method uses. QM9, whose labels are computed rather than measured, is the clean-label control, as described in Section~\ref{sec:noise:tests}.

\section{Methods}\label{sec:noise:methods}

% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
We use the datasets of Table~\ref{tab:datasets} except seven, namely rat microsomal clearance, anti-VEGF scan expression and binding, EGFR binding, angiopoietin-2 binding, antibody humanness and HIC retention. Folds and metrics are as in Chapter~\ref{chap:method}. We add hERG IC$_{50}$, shortened to HIC$_{50}$ in the figures, as listed in Table~\ref{tab:dataset_short_names}. Its labels are hERG pIC$_{50}$ values from ChEMBL, the negative base-10 logarithm of the molar concentration giving half of the maximal inhibition. hERG IC$_{50}$ is the only dataset with real repeat measurements. In this chapter, ``hERG'' on its own means the hERG blocking dataset, whose labels are blocker or non-blocker.

% [TH lens edit 2026-10-10: defines first/second single and hybrid (ranking by inner-fold mean on training rows, second hybrid = next with distinct features: tests/experiments/_shared.py fold_standard_selection, top_base_reps); adds what the clean-label search costs, numbers from tab:noise:res:search_clean_vs_damaged]
% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
In most experiments, the representations are chosen on clean labels. Within each outer fold, a search on the training molecules picks two single representations and two hybrids for each of the dataset's two downstream models. The search ranks them by their mean score over its inner folds. We call them the first and second single representation and the first and second hybrid. The second hybrid is the next one whose selected features differ from those of the first. The ranking uses the training molecules only, so the first need not score best on the held-out molecules. Every method is tested on all four. Only the hybrid's feature ranking and budget split are redone on the damaged labels. The rest of the search uses clean labels, which a real user would not have, so the reported scores may be too favorable. Section~\ref{sec:noise:res:search} measures this by searching on labels with Gaussian noise instead. For most datasets the two searches scored about the same. The largest loss was on hERG $K_i$ with the network, 0.779 against 0.669 RMSE on one fold. Two experiments differ. The expanded published sweep repeats the whole search on the damaged labels for every damage kind, level and draw. The experiment described in Section~\ref{sec:noise:mitigation} compares cleaning before the feature search with cleaning after it.

% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
We keep apart three tasks that are often treated as one, namely finding the damaged labels, sizing the damage, and improving the held-out score. A method can succeed at the first and fail at the third. Figure~\ref{fig:noise:flow} shows how one method is run and scored.

% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth,height=0.72\textheight,keepaspectratio]{figures_ch5_7/d7_noise_flow.png}
\caption[How a noise method is run and scored.]{How a noise method is run and scored in one outer fold. Noise mitigation selects molecules by a detector, a known flag or every molecule, then acts on them. Noise-robust training selects nothing and changes the model. Dashed: the controls.}
\label{fig:noise:flow}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Noise conditions}\label{sec:noise:conditions}

We inject label noise into a regression dataset in six ways, as listed in Table~\ref{tab:noise:kinds}. Four are the Gaussian, outlier, grouped-shifted and censoring conditions of Section~\ref{sec:noise_injection}. Censoring clips labels at a value that imitates an assay's reporting limit. We clip at the top of the label range, the rarer case, as real censoring of potency labels such as hERG $K_i$ falls mostly at the low end. Under outlier noise, a random 5\% of molecules get an error three times wider, against 10\% in Chapter~\ref{chap:robustness}. The other labels keep the ordinary error, so the 5\% carry about a third of the squared error. We leave out the grouped-wider condition and the Student-$t$ and Laplace shapes of Chapter~\ref{chap:robustness}.

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering
\small
\begin{adjustbox}{max width=\textwidth,max totalheight=0.78\textheight}
\begin{tabular}{@{}P{0.20\linewidth}P{0.42\linewidth}P{0.16\linewidth}P{0.14\linewidth}@{}}
\toprule
Damage kind & What is done to the training labels & Unit of the level & Labels moved \\
\midrule
Gaussian & Every label gets Gaussian error (Equation~\ref{eq:dose_matching}) & SD of the clean training labels & All \\
Outlier & As Gaussian, but a random 5\% get error three times wider & SD of the clean training labels & All \\
Grouped-shifted & Every scaffold family gets its own offset (Equation~\ref{eq:grouped_shifted}); small molecules only & SD of the clean training labels & All \\
Censoring & Labels above the $(1 - \text{level})$ quantile of the clean training labels are set to that quantile & Share of labels clipped & About the level \\
Shift in 10\% & A random 10\% are raised by one fixed amount & SD of the clean training labels & 10\% \\
Shift in 10\%, sized by model error & As above, in a different unit & SD of the out-of-fold residual & 10\% \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{adjustbox}
\caption[Damage kinds on a regression dataset.]{The six damage kinds on a regression dataset. SD is standard deviation. The out-of-fold residual is that of a LightGBM model fitted to the clean training labels.}
\label{tab:noise:kinds}
\end{table}

For classification tasks we take a different approach, flipping a share of the training labels. A label can flip uniformly, more often in the rarer classes, or more often in one direction. It can also flip at a rate that varies by molecule, in proportion to class frequency, or along a confusion matrix. With no matrix given, the last rule equals the first, leaving five distinct rules on a two-class dataset.

Most experiments first screen each method at the two levels given in Table~\ref{tab:noise:levels}. Experiments taken further run all six kinds of noise at every level, on datasets of 5,000 molecules or fewer, with three random draws per level.

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule
Damage & Screen & Full run \\
\midrule
Regression, other than censoring & None and 1.0 & 0, 0.5, 1.0 and 1.5 \\
Censoring, share clipped & None and 25\% & 0, 10\%, 25\% and 40\% \\
Classification, share flipped & 10\% & Up to 40\% \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption[Damage levels.]{Damage levels in the screen and in the full run. Regression levels are in the units of Table~\ref{tab:noise:kinds}.}
\label{tab:noise:levels}
\end{table}

% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
Only the training labels are damaged. As in Chapter~\ref{chap:robustness}, every score is against the held-out labels as delivered. Where a search runs on damaged labels, as in the expanded published sweep, its inner folds are scored on damaged labels too.

% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
Three settings use no injected damage, namely the labels as delivered, the real repeat measurements of hERG IC$_{50}$, and QM9 with no noise added. Three experiments add damage of their own, described with each experiment. One of them, on break-even precision, gives 5\% of molecules wide errors and leaves the rest alone, as defined in Section~\ref{sec:noise:mitigation}.

\subsection{Noise detection}\label{sec:noise:detection}

% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
A detector, as defined in Table~\ref{tab:noise_terms}, gives each molecule a suspicion score. The detectors read an out-of-fold prediction, the neighbors' labels, or a model's predicted spread, and are listed in Table~\ref{tab:noise:detectors}. Three spread detectors, the heteroscedastic GP, the variance-head network and NGBoost, never read the molecule's own label. They can only find a wrong label if the damage is tied to structure. A few spread detectors give the spread in the label's units, so it can set a weight or a move.

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering
\small
\begin{adjustbox}{max width=\textwidth,max totalheight=0.78\textheight}
\begin{tabular}{@{}P{0.22\linewidth}P{0.14\linewidth}P{0.40\linewidth}P{0.16\linewidth}@{}}
\toprule
Detector & Reads & Suspicion score of molecule $i$ & Source \\
\midrule
Out-of-fold forest residual & Prediction & $|y_i - \hat y_i| / \sigma_y$, with $\hat y_i$ from a random forest of 100 trees on 5 scaffold-group folds; on classification, $1 - \hat p_i(y_i)$ & \citet{Brodley1999}; \citet{Northcutt2021} \\
Out-of-fold LightGBM residual & Prediction & $|y_i - \hat y_i|$, with $\hat y_i$ from LightGBM on 5 scaffold-group folds & \citet{Brodley1999} \\
Neighbor-label gap & Neighbors & $|y_i - \bar y_{\mathcal{N}}| / \sigma_y$; on classification, the share of neighbors in another class & \citet{Wilson1972, Martin2021} \\
Gap over neighbor spread & Neighbors & $|y_i - \bar y_{\mathcal{N}}| / \max(s_{\mathcal{N}}, c)$, with $c = \max(0.01\,\sigma_y, 10^{-3})$ & \citet{Baserud2020}; \citet{Kordos2012} \\
Cliff-aware gap & Neighbors & $\dfrac{|y_i - \bar y_{\mathcal{N}}|}{\sigma_y} \cdot \dfrac{1}{1 + s_{\mathcal{N}}/\sigma_y}$ & New; framing after \citet{Maggiora2006, Stumpfe2012}; related to \citet{Chawla2006} \\
Gaussian process & Spread and prediction & Posterior standard deviation plus $|y_i - \hat y_i|$, out of fold, each inner fold capped at 1,000 molecules & \citet{Rasmussen2005}; the sum is new \\
Heteroscedastic GP & Spread & Predicted noise standard deviation, from a GP fitted jointly with a small network for the noise & After \citet{kersting2007most} \\
Variance-head network & Spread & Predicted standard deviation, trained with the $\beta$-NLL loss of Equation~\ref{eq:beta_nll} & \citet{Seitzer2022} \\
NGBoost & Spread & Predicted standard deviation, 50 boosting stages & \citet{Duan2020} \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{adjustbox}
\caption[Detectors.]{Detectors used in this chapter. $\bar y_{\mathcal{N}}$ and $s_{\mathcal{N}}$ are the mean and standard deviation of the labels of molecule $i$'s $k$ nearest training neighbors, and $\sigma_y$ is the standard deviation of the training labels. ``New'' means no published source was found.}
\label{tab:noise:detectors}
\end{table}

% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
The gap over neighbor spread is the buddy check used on weather-station readings \citep{Baserud2020}. RegENN uses a similar threshold on regression labels \citep{Kordos2012}, but we found no earlier use of either on molecular labels. The cliff-aware gap divides by the spread of all labels plus that of the neighbors. A molecule whose neighbors disagree with each other, as near an activity cliff, therefore scores lower. SLOM lowers an outlier score in the same way where a neighborhood is unstable, but for spatial data, where neighbors are found by location \citep{Chawla2006}.

% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
The variance-head network predicts a mean $\mu(x)$ and a variance $\sigma^2(x)$, and is trained with
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{L} = \frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{(y - \mu)^2}{\sigma^2} + \log \sigma^2\right) \cdot \sigma^{2\beta},
\label{eq:beta_nll}
\end{equation}
where $\beta = 0.5$ keeps the variance from hiding the errors in the mean \citep{Seitzer2022}.

% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
A neighbor detector needs a space, a neighbor count $k$ and a neighbor weighting. The space is a single representation, a hybrid, or a similarity space of Chapter~\ref{chap:similarity}. We try $k$ of 5, 10 and 20, with neighbors weighted by $1/\text{rank}$ or $1/d^2$. To choose, we hold back 20\% of the training scaffold families and predict each held-back label from its neighbors among the other training molecules. The three settings that predict best are kept.

% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
Two experiments change the space itself. The first multiplies each z-scored feature by the square root of its importance on the damaged training labels, rescaled to average 1, as in feature-weighted nearest neighbors \citep{Wettschereck1997}. The second compares four families of distance. These are the plain features under Euclidean and Manhattan distance, the features weighted by importance to the power 0.5 or 1.0, the hybrid's features, and forest proximity. The forest proximity of two molecules is the share of trees in which they share a leaf \citep{Liaw2002}, and their distance is one minus this share. The forest is fitted to the damaged training labels.

% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
One experiment compares further detectors with the out-of-fold residual. These are each molecule's Shapley value to a nearest-neighbor predictor \citep{ghorbani2019data, jia2019efficient} and the cleanlab regression score \citep{zhou2024detecting}, which draws its internal folds at random rather than by scaffold family. The rest divide the residual by a spread for each molecule, from NGBoost, a quantile forest or a conformal interval.

% [TH lens edit 2026-10-10: names q where it is set]
% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
Two experiments test the out-of-fold LightGBM residual under the shift sized by model error, grading the ranking by average precision. The first ranks molecules by the residual. The second divides each residual by a second LightGBM model's estimate of that molecule's usual residual, and turns the result into conformal $p$-values \citep{bates2023testing}. The calibration set for these is a random 20\% or 50\% of the training molecules, or the molecules with three or more agreeing repeat measurements. Molecules are flagged by the Benjamini--Hochberg rule \citep{benjamini1995controlling}. It bounds the expected share of false flags, written $q$, at 5\%, 10\% or 20\% if the calibration labels are clean.

% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
We can grade a detector only where some labels were damaged and others not, namely under outlier noise, censoring, the two shifts in 10\% and label flips. Gaussian and grouped-shifted noise move every label. The grade is the average precision of the suspicion scores against the damaged molecules. A detector that ranks at random scores the damaged share of the training molecules. Some experiments report the area under the ROC curve instead, where chance is 0.5.

\subsection{Noise mitigation}\label{sec:noise:mitigation}

% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
Table~\ref{tab:noise:mitigation} describes each noise mitigation method by what selects the molecules and what is done to them, as defined in Table~\ref{tab:noise_terms}. A few ways of selecting are crossed with three actions.

% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
Most methods read the suspicion score rescaled to $[0, 1]$, $\tilde s_i = (s_i - \min s)/(\max s - \min s)$. Deleting drops the molecules above a quantile of $s$, the filtering of \citet{Brodley1999} that \citet{Zhao2017} tested on QSAR modeling sets. Down-weighting gives molecule $i$ the weight $1/(1 + \alpha\tilde s_i)$, divided by the mean weight. A proportional move takes the label part of the way to a target $t_i$,
\begin{equation}
y_i' = y_i + \alpha\,\tilde s_i\,(t_i - y_i),
\label{eq:noise:move}
\end{equation}
so the most suspect label moves a share $\alpha$ of the way and the least suspect stays. The target is the weighted mean of the neighbors' labels \citep{nadaraya1964estimating, watson1964smooth}, or an out-of-fold LightGBM prediction, close to the target blending of \citet{reed2014training}. The score-scaled shrink moves a share $0.5\tilde s_i/(1 + 0.5\tilde s_i)$ of the way to that prediction. It is modeled on James--Stein shrinkage \citep{james1961estimation}, which pulls an estimate toward a common value by an amount set by its variance. Here the variance cancels, so the share depends only on the score.

% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]

Method & Selected by & How many & Action & Target, how far \\
\midrule

Delete & Forest residual or neighbor gap & Top 5, 10, 20\% & Delete & -- \\

Down-weight & Forest residual or neighbor gap & All & Count less & $1/(1+\alpha\tilde s_i)$ \\
Label smoothing & As above & All & Move & Neighbors' mean; Equation~\ref{eq:noise:move}, $\alpha = 0.25$ \\
Move toward prediction & As above & All & Move & Out-of-fold LightGBM; Equation~\ref{eq:noise:move} \\
Score-scaled shrink & As above & All & Move & Out-of-fold LightGBM; $0.5\tilde s_i/(1+0.5\tilde s_i)$ \\
Neighbor weights & Neighbor gap, in a chosen distance & All & Count less & $1/(1+3\tilde s_i)$ \\

Close-pair weights & Near-identical pairs whose labels disagree & Flagged pairs & Count less & Weight 0.25 \\
