\subsubsection{Selected by a detector}

The main cleanup experiment pairs the out-of-fold forest residual with each action above, and grades the cliff-aware gap as a detector alongside it. A second experiment then asks what to do with that flagged label, do you delete it or keep it with a lower weight or a modified label? Its control deletes the same number of molecules at random, at five seeds. A third experiment swaps in five detectors, specifically the cliff-aware gap, the GP, the heteroscedastic GP, the variance-head network and NGBoost. Each then proceeds to delete its top 5\%, 10\% or 20\%. A detector that makes its own prediction also moves every label toward its associated predicted value. The share moved is given by Equation~\ref{eq:noise:move}, or by $c_i = \alpha s_i/(1 + \alpha s_i)$, which uses the score in its own units.

Instead of deleting samples, four experiments weight them. Three use the weight $1/(1 + 3\tilde s_i)$, under which the most suspect molecule counts a quarter as much as the least suspect. They differ in where they look for neighbors, whether it be features scaled by importance on clean labels, or each of the four families of distance. The fourth uses the weight $\exp(-0.1\,\tilde s_i)$, under which the most suspect molecule still counts about 0.9 times as much. Its detector is the gap over neighbor spread, the GP or NGBoost.

Close-pair weights only look at near-identical pairs, defined as those whose pairwise distance is in the lowest 1\% of training pairs. The squared label difference between two noisy copies of one value averages twice the noise variance. We therefore estimate the noise from the closest fifth of these pairs as $\hat\sigma^2 = \overline{\Delta y^2}/2$. Both molecules of a close pair whose labels differ by more than $3\sqrt{2}\,\hat\sigma$ get weight 0.25. The same experiment asks how precise a detector must be before acting on its flags helps. It acts on sets of molecules of equal size whose share of truly damaged molecules runs from none to all. The share at which the gain crosses zero is the break-even precision.

The Cliff put-back method runs after a cleanup of the top 1\%, 5\% or 20\% of flagged molecules, and undoes it for activity-cliff molecules. A training molecule is designated to be a cliff  if a partner with ECFP4 Tanimoto similarity of at least 0.55 \citep{Hu2015} has a label far from its own \citep{Maggiora2006, Stumpfe2012}. Far means more than twice the median absolute deviation of the labels. ECFP4 at this threshold is the standard definition of a similar pair, and we did not test other representations for this step, although the count of cliffs depends on the representation, as Chapter~\ref{chap:flaws} showed. We keep the name on labels that are not a biological activity, such as LogD. The cliff set is rebuilt from the damaged training labels each time. Its control puts back as many molecules, drawn at random from the non-cliff molecules. We found no earlier method that puts examples back after a cleanup. Some cleaning methods exempt a whole class decided in advance \citep{Kubat1997, Stefanowski2008}, and \citet{Brodley1999} name the risk of discarding correct exceptions without giving a rule.

The hybrid's features then are chosen against the labels, such that the labels can be cleaned before the features are chosen or after. One experiment compares the two orders, under injected label damage and on labels as delivered, with nine cleanups driven by the out-of-fold forest residual. The search and the fit each use the labels as given or cleaned, giving four combinations. Down-weighting has only the two with an uncleaned search, because no weight enters the feature ranking or the budget split. We found no source that crosses the two. The nearest design ranks features on one version of the data and fits on another, but its second step is class-imbalance sampling, not label cleaning \citep{Shanab2012}.

Nothing says which molecules definitively have noise on labels as delivered (except in the special case of QM9) so a gain can come from removing hard molecules rather than wrong ones. One experiment runs seven cleanups on delivered labels, with the neighbor gap and the forest residual as detectors. Its control shuffles each detector's scores across the training molecules, at three seeds, and reruns the cleanup. The shuffled cleanup touches as many molecules, but not the ones the detector picked.

\subsubsection{Selected by a known flag}\label{sec:noise:censoring}
When labels are clipped to an assay limit, the detector is left with two things to guess: which labels are wrong and in what direction? We take the limit $L$ as the largest clipped training label, if at least two molecules and 1\% of the training molecules sit on it. An out-of-fold LightGBM model fitted to the clipped labels gives each molecule a prediction $p_i$. Let $\sigma$ be the standard deviation of $y_i - p_i$ over the molecules below the limit, and $m_i$ the inverse Mills ratio,
\begin{equation}
m_i = \frac{\phi(a_i)}{1 - \Phi(a_i)}, \qquad a_i = \frac{L - p_i}{\sigma},
\end{equation}
where $\phi$ and $\Phi$ are the standard normal density and distribution function. The normal model for labels cut at a limit is that of \citet{Tobin1958}. The truncated-normal mean replaces each clipped label by the mean of a normal distribution centered on its prediction and cut at the limit,
\begin{equation}
y_i' = \max\!\left(L,\; p_i + \sigma m_i\right).
\label{eq:noise:truncated}
\end{equation}
Censored expectation maximization (EM) repeats this for three rounds, after the iterated estimator of \citet{buckley1979linear}. Each round refits the downstream model out of fold. The constant lift raises every clipped label to $L + \overline{\sigma m}$, where $\overline{\sigma m}$ is the mean of $\sigma m_i$ over the clipped molecules. On average this shared lift exceeds the truncated-normal mean's by $L - p_i$. Two further methods test what the flag is worth. One deletes the clipped molecules. The other raises each clipped label by its own $\sigma m_i$, and its control deals the same lifts to the wrong clipped molecules, at three seeds. The closest precedent is \citet{Svensson2025}, who fit censored regression to drug-discovery labels.

Another known flag is a scaffold family, as under grouped-shifted noise each family carries its own offset, and subtracting the family's mean out-of-fold residual undoes it. Three versions differ in how much they trust a small family. The first corrects every family and the second only families of three or more training molecules. The third is a random-intercept model, fitted by up to five rounds of expectation maximization as in mixed-effects random forests \citep{hajjem2014mixed, sigrist2022gaussian}. It shrinks a small family's offset further toward zero.

One sign that your data has such noise is when several sources measured each mocule, resulting in a measuring source. We simulate this by creating eight ``sources'', such that each molecule is measured by one to four of them, and a number is drawn from hERG IC$_{50}$'s real counts or uniformly. Measurement $s$ of molecule $i$ is
\begin{equation}
y_{is} = y_i + b_s + \sigma_e\left(\sqrt{\rho}\,u_i + \sqrt{1-\rho}\,v_{is}\right),
\end{equation}
where $b_s$ is the source's offset, $u_i$ an error shared by all of the molecule's measurements and $v_{is}$ an error of its own. The value of $\rho$, 0, 0.3 or 0.7, sets how much of the error the measurements share, and the offsets have standard deviation 0 or $0.5\,\sigma_e$. The training label is then the mean of the molecule's measurements. The methods remove each source's offset, estimated by a mixed model with the source as a fixed effect and the molecule as a random intercept. They also weight each molecule by the inverse of its estimated error variance \citep{hedges1985meta}. Two further versions use the true $\rho$ and $\sigma_e$, which a user never has, so we treat them as bounds.

hERG IC$_{50}$ carries its own flag, specifically how often each molecule was measured and how far apart the measurements are. A molecule is one canonical SMILES string exactly as ChEMBL gives it, with no further standardization. Stereoisomers or salt forms written as different strings therefore count as separate molecules. A molecule's count is its number of IC$_{50}$ records, and its spread is the variance of their pIC$_{50}$ values. One method weights each molecule by its count. A second weights it by $1/(\sigma_u^2 + \tilde s_i^2/n_i)$, the inverse of its estimated error variance \citep{hedges1985meta}. Here $n_i$ is the count and $\sigma_u^2$ is the error that remains however often a molecule is measured. Because a variance from two or three records is unreliable \citep{smyth2004limma}, $\tilde s_i^2$ is pulled toward the pooled spread, as if the pool were three more measurements. A third moves each label toward an out-of-fold LightGBM prediction by a share $v_i/(v_i + \tau^2)$, so a less precise label moves further. Here $v_i$ is the spread divided by the count and $\tau^2$ the variance of the out-of-fold residual. Only held-out molecules with three or more measurements are graded.

\subsubsection{Every molecule}

One method moves every label halfway toward the out-of-fold prediction of a LightGBM model fitted with a Huber loss \citep{Huber1964}. The Huber loss keeps outlying labels from pulling that prediction. Its threshold is 1.4826 times the median absolute out-of-fold residual.

\subsection{Noise-robust training}\label{sec:noise:robust}

Five experiments test noise-robust training, as defined in Table~\ref{tab:noise_terms}. They change the downstream model, rerun the twelve published methods, use a robust loss, exchange molecules between two learners, or use repeat measurements.

\subsubsection{Changing the model instead of the labels}
One important comparison point for every mitigation result is changing the model instead of the labels. One experiment does this in four ways. Retuning tries the default hyperparameters and 20 random settings on three scaffold-split inner folds of the damaged labels, and refits the best. Its control tunes on clean labels and fits the chosen setting to the damaged ones, which separates tuning against the noise from tuning at all. Seed averaging averages ten copies of LightGBM, XGBoost or the network with different seeds, each tree model fitted on 80\% of the molecules and features. Stacking combines the dataset's two models by non-negative least squares on their out-of-fold predictions \citep{Breiman1996stacked}. The damage is each regression kind of Table~\ref{tab:noise:kinds}, plus Gaussian noise with variance 0.1, 0.3 or 1.0 times a LightGBM model's out-of-fold mean squared error on clean labels. This experiment ran on Buchwald--Hartwig, Caco-2, hERG $K_i$, LogD and QM9.

\subsubsection{The expanded published sweep}

If we recall, the LNL investigation began in Chapter~\ref{chap:flaws} with 12 methods. All use the plain model's network, with hidden layers of 256, 128 and 64 units. Unlike the earlier sweep, this one covers many datasets instead of QM9 alone, every damage kind instead of Gaussian noise alone, and hybrids as well as single representations. It also scores each method against the plain network on the same damaged labels and features. Four of the twelve delete molecules a detector selects, so under Table~\ref{tab:noise_terms} they get classified as noise mitigation. 

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering
\small
\begin{adjustbox}{max width=\textwidth,max totalheight=0.85\textheight}
\begin{tabular}{@{}P{0.20\linewidth}P{0.12\linewidth}P{0.42\linewidth}P{0.18\linewidth}@{}}
\toprule
Method & Group & What it does here & Source, and how it differs \\
\midrule
Small-loss selection & Mitigation & After 10 epochs, keeps the 70\% of molecules with the smallest loss and retrains & \citet{Han2018}; one network, not two \\
Confident learning & Mitigation & Three networks; deletes molecules with error above the 85th percentile and ensemble spread below the 25th, then retrains & \citet{Northcutt2021}; in-sample, no per-class thresholds \\
Distance-based selection & Mitigation & Deletes the 15\% with the largest neighbor-label gap & After \citet{Wilson1972} \\
Multistage cleaning & Mitigation & Three rounds, each deleting the 10\% with the largest in-sample error & Adapted from \citet{zhao2025}, which fits a mixture to loss histories and deletes once \\
Early learning & Robust training & Each epoch, molecules with above-median loss lose 5\% of their weight & Adapted from \citet{zhang2025}, which updates weights from a clean subset \\
Uncertainty curriculum & Robust training & After 20 epochs, trains on the lowest-loss 25\%, then 50\%, 75\% and 100\% & Self-paced learning \citep{kumar2010selfpaced} \\
DivideMix & Robust training & Two networks; a two-component mixture on each network's loss picks the molecules the other trains on & \citet{li2020dividemix}, without its semi-supervised half \\
Meta-Weight-Net & Robust training & A small network learns each molecule's weight from its loss, tuned on a held-back set of noisy labels & \citet{shu2019metaweightnet}, which needs clean labels for that set \\
MentorNet & Robust training & A small network weights each molecule from its loss, loss history and distance to its neighbors & After \citet{jiang2018mentornet} \\
Contrast-to-divide & Robust training & Down-weights molecules with high loss whose predictions are stable when the features are perturbed & Named after \citet{zheltonozhskii2021contrast}, which does not use perturbation \\
Heteroscedastic GP & Robust training & A GP's residuals train a network that predicts each molecule's noise; the plain network is then trained with weights $1/(1 + \text{noise})$ & After \citet{kersting2007most} \\
Evidential network & Robust training & The network predicts the parameters of a normal-inverse-gamma distribution & \citet{amini2020deep} \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{adjustbox}
\caption[The expanded published sweep.]{The twelve published methods of the expanded sweep. All use the same network. ``Mitigation'' marks the four that select molecules with a detector and delete them. Multistage cleaning, early learning and contrast-to-divide are our adaptations of the named methods.}
\label{tab:noise:robust}
\end{table}

\subsubsection{Huber loss}

We explore alternative losses, specifically a Huber loss \citep{Huber1964}, with threshold $c$ times 1.4826 times the median absolute out-of-fold residual, for $c$ of 0.5, 1, 1.5 or 2. Under the shift sized by model error, each model gets its own robust loss at $c = 1.5$. We use this loss for LightGBM and the network, a pseudo-Huber for XGBoost, and absolute-error splits for the random forest.

\subsubsection{Co-teaching}
Every molecule gets scored by LightGBM and an SVM by its out-of-fold error. For two rounds, each is refitted on the molecules the other finds easiest. Each then keeps its easiest 80\%, 90\% or 95\%, and the downstream model is fitted on the molecules both keep. This is the exchange of co-teaching \citep{Han2018}, made between whole refits rather than inside one training loop. Its control deletes as many molecules at random, at three seeds.

\subsubsection{Repeat measurements}
On hERG IC$_{50}$, four methods change what the model receives. The first trains on one row per measurement instead of one per molecule. The second adds the log of the measurement count as a feature, and the third gives NGBoost that feature. The fourth fits an XGBoost accelerated-failure-time model \citep{Barnwal2022} to the interval between each molecule's lowest and highest measurement.

\subsection{Evaluation and controls}\label{sec:noise:tests}

Each group of methods has its own question to answer.
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Noise detection.} Is the suspicion score high on the damaged molecules? This can only be read where some labels were damaged and others were not, as explained in Section~\ref{sec:noise:detection}. On QM9 with no noise added, every flag is false.
    \item \textbf{Noise mitigation, selected by a detector or a flag.} We compare the held-out score with that of the same model and features with no action. Where a control ran, the action must also beat the same action on randomly chosen molecules.
    \item \textbf{Noise mitigation on every molecule.} We compare against no action. No random selection is possible, so QM9 with no noise added is the only check.
    \item \textbf{Noise-robust training.} We compare against the same model trained normally on the same labels. A gain on QM9 with no noise added comes from the training change alone, and we set it beside the gain under damage.
\end{itemize}

Each control is described alongside its experiment. QM9 acts as an entire control dataset due to its clean, computed labels \citep{Ramakrishnan2014}. A method that removes noise should therefore do nothing on QM9, or make it worse. A gain on QM9 comes from a change that would help on any labels, such as a better-trained model. The strongest evidence that a method found real noise is that it leaves QM9 unchanged and improves a measured dataset's labels as delivered. The shuffled-score control cannot make this distinction alone, because it asks whether the choice of molecules matters, not whether there is noise. Section~\ref{sec:noise:res:qm9test} applies this test.

% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
Where a clean score exists, we also report how much of the lost score a method wins back. Each model $m$ is fitted to the clean, damaged and treated training labels, giving held-out scores $s^{m}_{\text{clean}}$, $s^{m}_{\text{damaged}}$ and $s^{m}_{\text{treated}}$ in the dataset's own metric. The recovery is
\begin{equation}
\mathrm{Recovery}_m = 100 \times \frac{s^{m}_{\text{treated}} - s^{m}_{\text{damaged}}}{s^{m}_{\text{clean}} - s^{m}_{\text{damaged}}}\ \%,
\label{eq:recovery}
\end{equation}
which reads the same whether higher or lower values are better. It is undefined when the clean and damaged scores differ by 2\% of the clean score or less. We report it for single representations only. A hybrid's features are re-ranked on the damaged labels, so its clean score comes from different features.


% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
\section{Results}

% SOURCE [N7-01]: anchor for tests/analysis/ch7_numbers.py
% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
Mitigation helps most often under injected damage and least often on the labels as delivered, as counted for each group in Additional file~\ref{app:add:noise_bygroup}.

Three caveats hold for every result below. First, due to time and compute constraints, most results are on the first outer fold only, as explained in Section~\ref{sec:method:evaluation}, and we give the fold count wherever more exist. Second, where a method has several settings, we report its best on the held-out score. The setting is picked on the same molecules that score it, so every gain is optimistic, for the single representation and the hybrid alike. The same choice is made on QM9 with no noise added. A gain there cannot come from removing noise, so Section~\ref{sec:noise:res:qm9test} also shows how much the choice of setting and better training can add together. Third, the network's score changes between fits. On QM9, with the same fold and labels, its second hybrid scores between 0.348 and 0.364 RMSE depending on the experiment, listed in Table~\ref{tab:noise:res:qm9_sweep}. A network gain of up to about 0.016 RMSE is inside that spread. LightGBM gives the same score every time.

\subsection{The clean-label test: where noise is removed}\label{sec:noise:res:qm9test}

We first sort the methods by what they do to QM9, as set out in Section~\ref{sec:noise:tests}. A method that also helps QM9 is, at least in part, a better way to train.

% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
Deletion passes the test. On QM9 with no noise added, it made every model worse in every configuration of Table~\ref{tab:noise:res:qm9_deletion}, and in the deletion row of Table~\ref{tab:noise:res:shuffled}. In other QM9 runs it gained at most 0.010 RMSE, about the network's spread between fits. On SMAC colloidal stability and expression titer, and on the weaker configurations of nanobody thermostability and AC-SINS self-association, deletion improved the labels as delivered and beat every shuffled control. These are the clearest cases in the chapter of a method finding noise already in the data. On the small-molecule labels as delivered, deletion found almost nothing to remove, as Section~\ref{sec:noise:res:delivered} shows.

% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
Label smoothing and weighting pass the test only in part. With the network they also improve QM9, while LightGBM barely moves, as Tables~\ref{tab:noise:res:weighting} and~\ref{tab:noise:res:shuffled} and Figure~\ref{fig:noise:did_cleaning_work_detector} show. On Caco-2, LogD and MDR1--MDCK efflux as delivered, the network gains about as much as on QM9, so those gains are not evidence of noise. The support vector regressor gains almost nothing on these labels. The exception among small molecules is label smoothing on hERG $K_i$. There the network's first single representation gains several times more than on QM9, relative to its score.

% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
With the network, passing the shuffled-score control means little on its own. In Table~\ref{tab:noise:res:shuffled}, label smoothing on QM9's first single representation beats all three shuffles, although there is no noise to find. The shuffles move about as much as the network does between fits.

% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
Changing the model fails the test, so its gains come more from better training than from handling noise. On QM9 with no noise added, stacking improves every configuration and averaging network seeds improves most, as Table~\ref{tab:noise:res:change_model_delivered} shows. On hERG $K_i$ and LogD as delivered, they help by about as much. Part of seed averaging's gain does respond to noise, though. Under injected Gaussian noise it gains more than without noise in every configuration on hERG $K_i$, and with both models on QM9, as Table~\ref{tab:noise:res:change_model} shows.

% [TH lens edit 2026-10-10: 'in the other direction' replaced by what happened]
% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
The twelve published methods mostly pass the test, unlike changing the model. On QM9 with no noise added, each made the network worse in a first run, reported in Table~\ref{tab:noise:res:qm9_sweep}. In a second run about half beat it, but only by about the network's own spread between fits. A gain from one of them on a measured dataset is therefore more likely to be noise found. The clearest case is the heteroscedastic GP on $\Delta\Delta G$, which raises the best single representation's Spearman and makes QM9 worse, as shown in Table~\ref{tab:noise:res:sweep_examples}.

% TODO: check (TH lens): name the methods with no QM9 result here; the hERG IC50 repeat-measurement methods are one case. The paragraph this points to does not list them, and its label has no heading.
% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
Noise is removed most clearly where deletion or a published method helps an antibody dataset as delivered and does nothing for QM9. On the small-molecule and reaction labels as delivered, three results clear this bar, namely label smoothing on hERG $K_i$ and on hERG blocking, and deletion on Buchwald--Hartwig. Under injected damage, moving every label toward a Huber-fitted prediction also clears it. It helped hERG $K_i$ under three kinds of damage and made QM9 worse, as Section~\ref{sec:noise:res:other_kinds} reports. The test rests on one computed property of one dataset, and a few methods have no QM9 result, as discussed in Section~\ref{sec:noise:limitations}.

\subsection{Changing a label against deleting a molecule}\label{sec:noise:res:action}

% SOURCE [N7-03]: anchor for tests/analysis/ch7_numbers.py
% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
On hERG blocking with 10\% of labels flipped along a confusion matrix, every action helped and deletion helped most. Table~\ref{tab:noise:res:herg_flip_actions} compares the actions using gradient-boosted trees, on the hybrid and the best single representation. Deletion, the only action with a control, also beat deleting the same number of molecules at random, as Table~\ref{tab:noise:res:delete_vs_random} shows. With the neighbor-label gap as the detector, it took the hybrid to 0.838 AUROC, against 0.818 for the best of five random deletions. With the forest residual, it beat random deletion on each of the three folds tested.

Deletion kept its lead over random deletion as the share of molecules flagged changed, as Figure~\ref{fig:noise:score_vs_level} shows for hERG blocking with flipped labels. Under both kinds of flips, it beat the best random deletion at every share, on the hybrid and on the best single representation. This control is strict, since the best of the five random deletions is picked on the held-out score. Under the confusion-matrix flips, every action lifted the hybrid above the single representation, which had started ahead. The figure also varies the share of labels clipped on Caco-2. There censored EM lowered the network's RMSE at every clipped share, while deleting the clipped molecules raised it. At 40\% clipped, censored EM took the hybrid from 0.365 to 0.317 RMSE.

\centering
=\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figures_ch5_7/f11_score_vs_damage_level.png}
\caption[Held-out score against the share flagged and the share clipped.]{Left and middle: held-out AUROC (higher is better) of XGBoost on hERG blocking with 10\% of training labels flipped, against the share of training molecules flagged by the neighbor-label gap. The blue line is the score on undamaged labels. Right: held-out RMSE (lower is better) of the network on Caco-2 against the share of training labels clipped. Solid lines are the first hybrid and dashed lines the first single representation (``Top hybrid'' and ``Best single representation'' in the legend). Outer fold 0. Dataset short names as in Table~\ref{tab:dataset_short_names}.}
\label{fig:noise:score_vs_level}
\end{figure}

The actions differ less on a regression dataset, as Table~\ref{tab:noise:res:logd_outlier_actions} shows for LogD with 5\% outliers and the network. On the first single representation every action helped, most of all deletion and the score-scaled shrink toward an out-of-fold prediction. On the first hybrid, down-weighting helped most and the shrink made it slightly worse. Against random deletion the result is mixed. Table~\ref{tab:noise:res:delete_vs_random} shows deletion beating it on the second hybrid and losing to it on the first.

The detector matters more than the action that follows. Under the same hERG flips, we deleted the top 10\% by each of five detectors, as Table~\ref{tab:noise:res:herg_flip_actions} lists. The cliff-aware gap helped the hybrid most, followed by the GP. The other three helped the hybrid least and made the single representation worse. They predict a spread from the features alone and never read a label, so they cannot see a flipped one, as explained in Section~\ref{sec:noise:detection}. On a second hERG fold, averaged over three noise draws, the cliff-aware gap improved both configurations. The three detectors that never read a label made both worse. On LogD with outliers, the GP was best for the network's hybrid, as Table~\ref{tab:noise:res:logd_outlier_actions} shows.

The gain depends on which molecules the action reaches, which we test with flagged sets whose content we control. On hERG $K_i$ with wide errors in 5\% of molecules, down-weighting the 10\% the residual flags improved the network on both configurations in Table~\ref{tab:noise:res:known_flags}. The first single representation went from 0.689 to 0.642 RMSE. Down-weighting the same number of molecules, none of them damaged, made both worse. On QM9 with no noise added, the same down-weighting made both slightly worse.

Weighting keeps every molecule and every label, and it helped on hERG, LogD and Buchwald--Hartwig, as Table~\ref{tab:noise:res:weighting} shows. Under the hERG flips it helped the hybrid and barely moved the single representation. It also helped the network on QM9 with no noise added, by about 0.01 RMSE with neighbor or detector weights, while LightGBM barely moved. So a network gain of that size from weighting is not evidence that noise was removed. Down-weighting near-identical pairs that disagree, the weighting that helped Buchwald--Hartwig, also helped the network on QM9 but made LightGBM worse.

Additional file~\ref{app:add:noise_mitigation_broad} counts, for each broad noise mitigation method, the conditions where it improved the best single representation, the hybrid, both or neither.

Deleting almost never helps on clean labels, which is the intention. On QM9 with no noise added, deletion made every model and configuration in Table~\ref{tab:noise:res:qm9_deletion} worse. The network's hybrid, for example, went from 0.355 to 0.393 RMSE. In two other QM9 experiments, deletion also made both models worse on the best single representation and the hybrid. In a third, deleting the top 10\% by each of five detectors made the first hybrid worse with every model and detector. It improved single representations by at most 0.004 RMSE. It's clear that when deletion helps on a damaged dataset and beats random deletion, as under the hERG flips, the gain comes from removing noise.

\subsubsection{The labels as delivered}\label{sec:noise:res:delivered}

% SOURCE [N7-10]: anchor for tests/analysis/ch7_numbers.py
% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
On all five small-molecule datasets in Table~\ref{tab:noise:res:shuffled}, label smoothing or down-weighting beat shuffled detector scores on the labels as delivered, narrowly on Caco-2 and LogD. Here nothing marks which labels are wrong, so the control reruns each cleanup with the detector's scores shuffled across the training molecules. On hERG $K_i$, label smoothing passed with both detectors. With the neighbor-label gap, it took the network's first single representation from 0.638 to 0.578 RMSE, while every shuffle made it slightly worse. It also improved the network's hybrid on each of three folds, as Table~\ref{tab:noise:res:hergki_smoothing_folds} shows. On hERG blocking, label smoothing beat all its shuffles with gradient-boosted trees. On MDR1--MDCK efflux, label smoothing and down-weighting beat all shuffles with the network, but the support vector regressor gained nothing from any cleanup. On Caco-2 and LogD, one shuffle recovers more than half of the gain from down-weighting.

% SOURCE [N7-11]: anchor for tests/analysis/ch7_numbers.py
% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
On the small-molecule labels as delivered, deletion does not pass its control. On Caco-2, hERG $K_i$ and LogD, no deletion setting beat both no cleaning and every random deletion. On MDR1--MDCK efflux, deleting the 5\% with the largest neighbor-label gap did, but only by 0.004 RMSE over the best random deletion. That margin is inside the network's spread between fits. Buchwald--Hartwig is different. There, deleting the 20\% of reactions with the largest neighbor-label gap improved LightGBM and beat random deletion on the hybrid and the second single representation, as Table~\ref{tab:noise:res:delete_vs_random} shows. Random deletion alone also lowered the RMSE a little.

% SOURCE [N7-12]: anchor for tests/analysis/ch7_numbers.py
% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
Putting activity-cliff molecules back after a cleanup gave mixed results, as Table~\ref{tab:noise:res:cliff_putback} shows. The control puts back a random set of the same size. On Caco-2, hERG and LogD this control cannot be run, as more than half of the training molecules count as cliff molecules. On hERG $K_i$, putting the cliff molecules back gave a lower RMSE than putting random ones back on the first and third folds, but not on the second. On QM9 with no noise added, only 19 of 7,982 training molecules count as cliff molecules. Putting them back recovered part of the loss from deletion, while random molecules recovered none of it.

\subsection{Detection of the suspect labels}\label{sec:noise:res:detectors}

% SOURCE [N7-13]: anchor for tests/analysis/ch7_numbers.py
% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
The out-of-fold forest residual finds most kinds of damage well above chance, but not clipped labels. We grade the detectors on injected damage alone, since only there do we know which labels are wrong, as explained in Section~\ref{sec:noise:detection}. Table~\ref{tab:noise:res:detect_residual} shows the residual well above chance under outliers, shifted labels and flipped classes on every dataset. On Caco-2 under outliers, for example, its average precision is 0.338 against a chance level of 0.051. With 25\% of labels clipped, it is near or below chance on the small molecules and reactions, and above chance on the antibody datasets. A model fitted to clipped labels predicts values near the limit for the clipped molecules, so their residuals are small. Chapter~\ref{chap:robustness} found the same in the models' own uncertainty, which was lowest on the clipped labels.

% SOURCE [N7-14]: anchor for tests/analysis/ch7_numbers.py
% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
The cliff-aware gap beats the residual when only some labels are shifted, and no detector is best everywhere. Under the shift in 10\% on Caco-2, its median neighbor setting reaches an average precision of 0.269, against 0.202 for the residual. Under outliers its best setting is ahead of the residual and its median setting behind. Under censoring it trails the residual on Caco-2, hERG $K_i$ and LogD at every setting, as Table~\ref{tab:noise:res:detect_residual} shows. A neighbor detector is better at a shift that moves a few labels a long way, and a model residual is as good or better at outliers.

% [TH lens edit 2026-10-10: opens with the answer to the chapter's question; adds one example range from tab:noise:res:detect_families (Caco-2 outliers row)]
% SOURCE [N7-15]: anchor for tests/analysis/ch7_numbers.py
% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
Noisy labels were not easier to find with neighbors in the hybrid's features than in other spaces, at least with each detector at its best setting. We group the spaces into four families, namely plain features, importance-weighted features, forest proximity and the hybrid's own features. Table~\ref{tab:noise:res:detect_families} gives the best setting in each family, and the space changes detection little. On Caco-2 under outliers, for example, the four families reach 0.335 to 0.352 average precision. The importance-weighted features were best on most datasets. The hybrid's own features were best on no dataset overall, and won only single damage kinds, such as outliers on the change in binding free energy ($\Delta\Delta G$) and a few others shown in the table. Averaged over the detector settings instead, the hybrid's features come out ahead more often, so this ranking depends on how a detector's setting is chosen.

% SOURCE [N7-16]: anchor for tests/analysis/ch7_numbers.py
% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
Scaling each feature by its importance before finding the neighbors raised detection under the shift in 10\% on most datasets, and lowered it under censoring on every dataset. Table~\ref{tab:noise:res:importance_scaling} lists the scores. The largest gain was on QM9, from 0.538 to 0.634 average precision. On hERG $K_i$ and CHO polyreactivity it was unchanged or slightly lower, and under outliers scaling barely changed detection. Importance is computed against the damaged labels. Under censoring it likely rewards the features that predict the clipped values, so the clipped molecules look like their neighbors.

Only one model-uncertainty detector could be graded, namely a Gaussian process's own predicted spread plus its residual, since the other three predict a spread from the features alone. It was slightly ahead of the cliff-aware gap under outliers on Caco-2 and LogD, and behind it under every shift and hERG flip kind. Detectors from research fields outside cheminformatics did better where the residual is weakest, and Additional file~\ref{app:add:noise_detect_other} gives these results. Under censoring on Buchwald--Hartwig, the nearest-neighbor Shapley value reached an average precision of 0.391, against 0.195 for the residual, close to the 0.193 in Table~\ref{tab:noise:res:detect_residual}. The Shapley value was also ahead on SMAC colloidal stability, and the cleanlab score was well ahead on Buchwald--Hartwig under the shift in 10\%. These numbers are means over each detector's settings, and the Shapley value has far more settings than the residual.

The out-of-fold LightGBM residual can rank damaged molecules, but it cannot reliably say which ones to flag. It ranked the shifted molecules above chance on every dataset; on expression titer, for example, its average precision was 0.20 to 0.25, against 0.106 by chance. Turning that ranking into flags with conformal $p$-values, as described in Section~\ref{sec:noise:detection}, almost never flagged a molecule on the measured datasets. It flagged molecules regularly only on QM9 with outliers or a shift injected, and acting on those flags barely changed the held-out score.

% SOURCE [N7-19]: anchor for tests/analysis/ch7_numbers.py
% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
A detector can rank the damaged labels above chance and still make the model worse. The break-even precision is the share of truly damaged molecules a flagged set needs before acting on it helps, as explained in Section~\ref{sec:noise:mitigation}. Table~\ref{tab:noise:res:breakeven} compares it with the precision of the residual's flags. With wide errors in 5\% of molecules, the residual's precision is above the break-even on Caco-2 and hERG $K_i$. Under the shift sized by model error, it falls below the break-even for deletion. On Caco-2, for example, the top 5\% has a precision of 0.171, while deleting them pays off only above 0.616.

% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
Deleting the molecules that two learners flag made the model worse under the shift in 10\% on hERG $K_i$ and Caco-2. Figure~\ref{fig:noise:detection_vs_cleaning} compares the precision of the deleted molecules with the break-even precision for the two learners, LightGBM and an SVM, that exchange molecules during training. Each flags the training molecules with the largest loss. We delete every molecule that either one flags, so slightly more than the flagged share is deleted. On hERG $K_i$ and Caco-2, the precision stayed below the break-even at every share. LogD has no break-even precision. There, deleting 5\% under outliers, with a precision of 0.36, lowered the RMSE from 0.587 to 0.557.

\subsection{Effect by noise type}\label{sec:noise:res:kind}

\subsubsection{Labels clipped at an assay limit}\label{sec:noise:res:censoring}

% SOURCE [N7-20]: anchor for tests/analysis/ch7_numbers.py
% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
Clipping labels at an assay limit is the damage we could most often undo, and undoing it needs no detector. Figure~\ref{fig:noise:censoring_ladder} gives the held-out RMSE with a quarter of the training labels clipped. There, censored EM lowers it for every model and configuration, though not to the level before clipping. On LogD, the network on the best single representation wins back about three quarters of the lost RMSE, as defined in Equation~\ref{eq:recovery}. QM9 shows the same for the network and LightGBM, and Caco-2 a smaller gain.

% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
No one way of raising the clipped labels is best on every dataset. We compare three, namely the constant lift, the truncated-normal mean and censored EM. Table~\ref{tab:noise:res:censoring_moves_main} gives one row per dataset, and Additional file~\ref{app:add:noise_censoring_moves} gives every model and configuration. Censored EM is best in most configurations on LogD, QM9 and AC-SINS self-association. On Buchwald--Hartwig it brings the random forest back to about its score before clipping. The constant lift is best in most configurations on hERG $K_i$ and MDR1--MDCK efflux, and wins back most of the loss for the network's hybrid on nanobody thermostability. The truncated-normal mean trails both on LogD, hERG $K_i$ and Caco-2.

Deleting the clipped molecules instead is worse than keeping their clipped labels on Caco-2, hERG $K_i$ and QM9, also shown in Figure~\ref{fig:noise:censoring_ladder}. It is also worse on MDR1--MDCK efflux, with both models in every configuration. On LogD, it beats the clipped labels with the network's hybrid but stays far behind raising them. Without the clipped molecules, the top of the label range is missing from training.

% SOURCE [N7-24]: anchor for tests/analysis/ch7_numbers.py
% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
The size of each molecule's own lift does not matter. We raised each clipped label by its own estimated amount, and compared this with dealing the same lifts to the clipped molecules at random, three times over. In Table~\ref{tab:noise:res:censoring_lift}, every shuffle beats the molecule's own lift in every configuration on LogD and Caco-2. The same holds on hERG $K_i$ for the second single representation, and on MDR1--MDCK efflux for both hybrids. What matters is knowing which labels are clipped and raising them by about the right average amount, which is what the constant lift does.

Network, 25\% of training labels clipped. One row is one dataset and configuration. Held-out RMSE, lower is better. Outer fold 0. Bold: the values discussed in the text. On three antibody datasets the clipping did no harm, so there was nothing to fix. Clipping a quarter of the training labels raised the held-out Spearman in most configurations, as listed in Additional file~\ref{app:add:noise_censoring_moves}. With nothing lost, censored EM moved the score back down in most configurations on SMAC and $\Delta\Delta G$, and raised it a bit on expression titer.

We clipped a quarter of QM9's training labels again, this time on all five outer folds rather than the single fold of Table~\ref{tab:noise:res:censoring_moves_main}. As a control, we applied the same treatment to the same number of unclipped labels. Moving the clipped labels lowered the RMSE with both models in every feature space, while moving the unclipped labels raised it, which was the intended behavior. With the network on the first hybrid, the mean RMSE went from 0.5072 before treatment to 0.3980 after censored EM.

\subsubsection{A shared offset per scaffold group}\label{sec:noise:res:offsets}


Let's assume you know you have some form of systematic, grouped-shifted noise. Then, you can estimate a per-scaffold offset. Subtracting this offset helped on Caco-2, hERG $K_i$, LogD and QM9. We estimate the offset either as the family's mean label or with a random-intercept model. Table~\ref{tab:noise:res:family_offsets} shows that on Caco-2, LogD and QM9, both improved the network on the hybrid on every noise draw, without reaching the score on clean labels. The random-intercept model was usually the better of the two. On MDR1--MDCK efflux, the random-intercept model improved the hybrid by less, while subtracting family means made one of the two draws worse.

\midrule
Caco-2 & 0 & 0.321 & 0.297 & \textbf{0.280} & 0.239 \\
 & 1 & 0.344 & 0.298 & 0.294 & 0.242 \\
 & 2 & 0.361 & 0.277 & 0.267 & 0.231 \\
LogD & 0 & 0.684 & 0.562 & 0.546 & 0.398 \\
 & 1 & 0.690 & 0.606 & 0.573 & 0.402 \\
 & 2 & 0.672 & 0.570 & 0.589 & 0.395 \\
HK$_i$ & 0 & 0.804 & 0.691 & 0.693 & 0.610 \\
 & 1 & 0.834 & 0.708 & 0.723 & 0.611 \\
 & 2 & 0.743 & 0.719 & 0.666 & 0.591 \\
QM9 & 0 & 0.909 & 0.574 & 0.507 & 0.346 \\
 & 1 & 0.844 & 0.683 & 0.571 & 0.347 \\

MDR1 & 0 & 0.536 & 0.515 & 0.513 & 0.459 \\
 & 1 & 0.511 & 0.516 & 0.499 & 0.460 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption[Removing a shared offset per scaffold family.]{Removing a shared offset per scaffold family under grouped-shifted noise at level 1.0. Network on the first hybrid. One row is one dataset and noise draw. Held-out RMSE, lower is better. Outer fold 0. Dataset short names as in Table~\ref{tab:dataset_short_names}.}
\label{tab:noise:res:family_offsets}
\end{table}

Applied to the labels as delivered, neither method found a shared offset per scaffold family, as Table~\ref{tab:noise:res:offsets_delivered} shows. Both made every dataset worse, except that the random-intercept model lowered the RMSE on MDR1--MDCK efflux by less than the network's spread between fits. Caco-2, LogD and MDR1--MDCK efflux each come from one company's measurements, where such offsets are less likely. The test is also weak on these datasets, as their scaffold families hold about two molecules or fewer on average. A family's mean is then mostly its own molecules' labels. On QM9 with no noise added, subtracting family means made every configuration of both models worse, because it removed real differences between families.

\subsubsection{Several sources per molecule}\label{sec:noise:res:sources}

% SOURCE [N7-28]: anchor for tests/analysis/ch7_numbers.py
% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
When each molecule has several simulated sources, each with its own offset, removing the offsets made little difference on small molecules. On hERG $K_i$, the network on the hybrid scored 0.742 RMSE with the plain mean of the measurements and 0.748 after removing the offsets. Removing the offsets helped most often on Buchwald--Hartwig, where the offsets were large compared to the spread of the labels. Even there, the random forest on the hybrid stayed far from its score on clean labels. On the other datasets, QM9 among them, it helped in about half the settings or fewer.

Table~\ref{tab:noise:res:huber_blend} covers a method without any detector, which moves every label halfway toward a Huber-loss model's prediction. This helped the network on hERG $K_i$ under all three kinds of damage. Under Gaussian noise at level 1.0, it took the second hybrid from 0.718 to 0.681 RMSE, and the gains held on every noise draw. On QM9 with no noise added, the same blend made every configuration worse with both models, so its gains on hERG $K_i$ are noise removed.

\subsection{Effect by dataset}\label{sec:noise:res:dataset}

% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
We now turn to the labels as delivered, dataset by dataset. On five antibody datasets, cleaning these labels passes the shuffled-score control in some configurations, and where it passes it gives the largest gains in this chapter.

\subsubsection{The antibody datasets}\label{sec:noise:res:antibodies}

% SOURCE [N7-30]: anchor for tests/analysis/ch7_numbers.py
% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
SMAC colloidal stability gives the strongest controlled result on any labels as delivered. Table~\ref{tab:noise:res:shuffled} lists four configurations where deletion helps the support vector regressor and moving flagged labels toward an out-of-fold prediction helps the network. In all four, every shuffle of the detector scores does worse. Deleting the 20\% of antibodies flagged by the forest residual, for example, raises the support vector regressor's first single representation from 0.274 to 0.403 Spearman. The held-out fold has only 53 antibodies, but the shuffles were fitted on the same fold and stayed low.

% SOURCE [N7-31]: anchor for tests/analysis/ch7_numbers.py
% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
$\Delta\Delta G$ and expression titer also pass the shuffled control on their labels as delivered, in all four of their configurations in Table~\ref{tab:noise:res:shuffled}. On both datasets, the cleaned single representation still beats the cleaned hybrid. On nanobody thermostability and AC-SINS self-association, deletion passed the control mainly where the starting score was low. Where the starting score was highest, as for the GP on AC-SINS, the gain was small or a shuffle did as well.

% SOURCE [N7-32]: anchor for tests/analysis/ch7_numbers.py
% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
Table~\ref{tab:noise:res:smac_weights} shows that weighting each antibody by its agreement with its neighbors helped the network on SMAC by about as much as deleting the flagged antibodies. This method has no control, though.

% [TH lens edit 2026-10-10: caption says what bold marks; here bold is a pair or value from the text, not the best]

\caption[Neighbor-agreement weights on SMAC colloidal stability.]{Weighting by agreement with the neighbors on SMAC colloidal stability's labels as delivered. Network, outer fold 0. One row is one configuration. Held-out Spearman, higher is better. Bold: the values discussed in the text.}
\label{tab:noise:res:smac_weights}

The held-out folds of the antibody datasets contain only 44 to 135 antibodies, and several start off from weak scores, so large gains come easily and count for less. We still count SMAC, $\Delta\Delta G$ and expression titer as real results because they pass the shuffled controls, alongside nanobody thermostability and AC-SINS on their weaker configurations.

\subsubsection{Small molecules and the reaction set}\label{sec:noise:res:smallmol}

On the small-molecule labels as delivered, two label smoothing gains pass their control and are not explained by the network's spread between fits. One is for the network on hERG $K_i$ and the other for XGBoost on hERG blocking, as described in Section~\ref{sec:noise:res:delivered}. Buchwald--Hartwig is the one dataset where deleting flagged reactions from the labels as delivered beats random deletion.

\subsubsection{Repeat measurements on hERG IC\texorpdfstring{$_{50}$}{50}}\label{sec:noise:res:herg_ic50}

hERG IC$_{50}$ is the one dataset whose noise was measured rather than injected, as explained in Section~\ref{sec:noise:methods}. As such, we do not add any noise, and in each draw a held-out molecule's label is a fresh, random choice of its measurements. Which use of the repeat measurements helps depends on the model, as Table~\ref{tab:noise:res:herg_ic50} shows. Weighting each molecule by its number of measurements improves the SVR on the hybrid on all three draws, but makes the network worse. The network gained instead from the log of the measurement count as a feature, and from shrinking each label toward a prediction by its spread. Training on one row per measurement instead of the mean barely changed either model.

ChEMBL curators flagged 301 of the 12,008 records, most of them as outside the typical range. We graded the detectors against these flags on the first outer fold. The neighbor-label gap and the forest residual ranked the flagged records above chance in every configuration. Their average precisions were 0.14 to 0.23, against a chance level of 0.036.

\quad Weight by inverse error variance & 0.735 & 0.738 & 0.738 \\

\subsection{Cleaning inside the feature search}\label{sec:noise:res:search}

% SOURCE [N7-35]: anchor for tests/analysis/ch7_numbers.py
% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
We can clean the labels used by the hybrid search, the labels used to fit the final model, both or neither, as outlined in Section~\ref{sec:noise:mitigation}. Cleaning both does best in about half the rows of Table~\ref{tab:noise:res:clean_before_search}. On expression titer's labels as delivered, the forest residual flags 20\% of the antibodies. Deleting them from both the search and the fit takes the network on the hybrid from 0.155 to 0.562 Spearman, and cleaning only one of the two helps by less. On SMAC colloidal stability with a quarter of the labels clipped, deleting the flagged 5\% also does best in both. There, cleaning the search alone does worse than no cleaning.

% SOURCE [N7-36]: anchor for tests/analysis/ch7_numbers.py
% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
The largest gain in Table~\ref{tab:noise:res:clean_before_search} comes from cleaning the search alone, but cleaning before the search can also hurt. Shrinking SMAC colloidal stability's labels as delivered toward an out-of-fold prediction, for the search only, lifts the network's second hybrid from $-0.014$ to 0.560 Spearman. That gain starts near zero, on a held-out fold of 53 antibodies. Deleting flagged antibodies before the search made the network's first hybrid on SMAC worse. On hERG $K_i$, cleaning before the search helped on some noise draws and folds and hurt on others. This experiment has no random-flag control.

For most datasets, Gaussian noise does little damage to the search itself. We searched the hybrid on clean labels or on labels injected with Gaussian noise, fitted both, and scored them on held-out labels as delivered. Table~\ref{tab:noise:res:search_clean_vs_damaged} shows the two searches scoring almost the same on most datasets and folds. The largest loss was on hERG $K_i$ with the network, 0.669 against 0.779 RMSE on one fold. On SMAC colloidal stability and $\Delta\Delta G$, the two searches swap places from fold to fold.

\subsection{Changing the model instead of the labels}\label{sec:noise:res:model}

% SOURCE [N7-38]: anchor for tests/analysis/ch7_numbers.py
% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
On hERG $K_i$ under Gaussian noise, changing the model helped as much as the best cleaning of the labels, with no detector and no label changed. Averaging ten network seeds, for example, takes the network's second hybrid from 0.718 to 0.656 RMSE at level 1.0, as Table~\ref{tab:noise:res:change_model} shows. It wins on each of the three noise draws. Stacking and retuning help that configuration by less, and none of the three reaches the clean-label score. On QM9, Table~\ref{tab:noise:res:change_model_delivered} shows that seed averaging helps the network's second hybrid more under Gaussian noise than with none.

% SOURCE [N7-39]: anchor for tests/analysis/ch7_numbers.py
% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
Table~\ref{tab:noise:res:change_model_delivered} shows that on the labels as delivered, seed averaging, stacking and retuning also help, but not everywhere. They help QM9 with no noise added by about as much, so their gains on the labels as delivered are better training rather than noise found, as explained in Section~\ref{sec:noise:res:qm9test}. On hERG $K_i$, the network's best single representation does not improve with any of the three, and retuning makes both hybrids worse. Stacking combines both models, so its gains over the support vector regressor compare two models with the weaker one alone.

% SOURCE [N7-40]: anchor for tests/analysis/ch7_numbers.py
% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
Table~\ref{tab:noise:res:change_model} shows that under Gaussian noise on hERG $K_i$, retuning on the damaged labels mostly beats reusing settings tuned on clean labels. Only on the network's first hybrid are the clean-label settings slightly ahead. With a quarter of the labels clipped, the clean-label settings did better in every configuration, and retuning on the clipped labels rarely beat the plain model.

% SOURCE [N7-41]: anchor for tests/analysis/ch7_numbers.py
% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
The other noise-robust training methods are in Additional file~\ref{app:add:noise_robust_broad}, and each helped only in some settings. A Huber loss for the network helped under outliers on hERG $K_i$ and made Gaussian noise worse at every threshold. Each model's own robust loss helped in some configurations under the shift sized by model error, though by little. On LogD's first hybrid, for example, it improved the network by less than 0.01 RMSE, about half the gap to clean labels. The two-learner selection beat random deletion with tree models. On Buchwald--Hartwig under the shift in 10\%, it took the random forest's first single representation from 20.3 to 18.6 RMSE in yield points. It beat random deletion at all three seeds. The tree models and the network ran on different datasets, so we cannot say whether the model or the dataset is the reason.

The earlier Caco-2 example's d'=1 rows were set aside on 2026-10-10 (inputs changed) and are not in 10-10b [N7-41]

\subsubsection{The expanded published sweep}\label{sec:noise:res:sweep}

Methods who's primary mechanism was deleting molecules based on large training losses did not have success on small molecules. Both small-loss selection and multistage cleaning work this way. Table~\ref{tab:noise:res:sweep} shows them helping more often on Buchwald--Hartwig and the antibodies, though Buchwald--Hartwig is a single dataset.

Only two of the twelve methods helped in a specific case, and each only a little. The heteroscedastic GP was the only method to help on $\Delta\Delta G$'s labels as delivered, for some of the configurations listed in Table~\ref{tab:noise:res:sweep_examples}. It was also one of the methods that held up under Gaussian noise on QM9 in Chapter~\ref{chap:flaws}. Distance-based selection was the only method to help both the single representation and the hybrid on hERG blocking with 10\% of labels flipped one way, by less than 0.01 AUROC.

\end{table}

On QM9 with no noise added, every one of the twelve methods made the network worse, on both the best single representation and the hybrid, as Table~\ref{tab:noise:res:qm9_sweep} shows. In a second run of eleven of the methods on the same labels, about half of them beat the plain network on its second hybrid. The difference between the runs is the network's own spread from one fit to the next, which we come back to in Section~\ref{sec:noise:res:repr}.

\caption[The expanded sweep on QM9 with no noise added.]{The expanded sweep on QM9 with no noise added, in two runs of the same network. One row is one method. Held-out RMSE, lower is better; bold is the lowest in each column. Outer fold 0. Empty: not run.}

As is a trend in this thesis, everything is conditional. The method that works the best with hybrids is rarely the one that works best on single representations. On the first hybrid there are three exceptions, namely Caco-2 under the shift in 10\% (distance-based selection), hERG under class-dependent flips (confident learning) and hERG under instance-dependent flips (distance-based selection). A ranking of noise-handling methods made on one representation just does not carry over to another.

\subsection{Effect by model and representation}\label{sec:noise:res:repr}

Cleaning methods are definitely benefitted by hybrid representations on small molecules, the hybrid beats the best single representation in almost every small-molecule condition prior to noise injection, and still does even after. On LogD with outliers in Table~\ref{tab:noise:res:weighting}, for example, the network's cleaned hybrid scores 0.556 RMSE against 0.591 for the cleaned single representation. Cleaning rarely changes which of the two wins, and when it does, it more often than not moves the win towards the hybrid. On antibodies the hybrid is ahead in about half the conditions either way, and on the reaction set it's on par.

We now return to the question this thesis began with: does a better feature set make it easier to train under noise? The closest test down-weights each molecule by how far its label is from its neighbors' labels. The neighbors are found either in the model's own single representation or in the hybrid. Neither choice is reliably better. The hybrid's neighbors helped slightly more often, but the largest differences go both ways, as Table~\ref{tab:noise:res:neighbor_space} shows. On LogD under a scaffold-family shift, for example, the hybrid's neighbors helped the network while its own neighbors made it worse. This comparison also has no control.

\midrule
\multicolumn{5}{@{}l}{\textit{RMSE (lower is better)}} \\

\midrule
