% [TH lens edit 2026-10-10: gathers the model-dependent results of the chapter in one place (QM9 test and hERG IC50 sections)]
% [TH lens edit 2026-10-10: opens the paragraph with its point; splits a long sentence]
% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
% SOURCE [N7-48]: anchor for tests/analysis/ch7_numbers.py
The same cleaning helps some models and not others. The network gains from cleaning more often than the support vector regressor on Caco-2, hERG $K_i$ and LogD, though part of that may be its own spread between fits. A deterministic model whose score is unchanged never counts as helped. On $\Delta\Delta G$ the support vector regressor gains more often, and on expression titer the random forest gains more often than the network. The model also matters on clean labels and on repeat measurements. On QM9 with no noise added, label smoothing and weighting improve the network while LightGBM barely moves, as Section~\ref{sec:noise:res:qm9test} found. On hERG IC$_{50}$, Table~\ref{tab:noise:res:herg_ic50} shows that count weights helped the support vector regressor and made the network worse.

% [TH lens edit 2026-10-10: 'lose nothing' replaced by what was found; states the intermediate conclusion]
% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
\label{sec:noise:discussion}
% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
On small molecules, cleaning the labels did not cost the hybrid its lead over the best single representation. Cleaning can still make a model worse, as deletion did on QM9 with no noise added. Which method did best also changed between the hybrid and the single representations, as Section~\ref{sec:noise:res:sweep} describes. So whether a noise-handling method works depends on the feature space it runs in, as Chapter~\ref{chap:flaws} found for distance.

\section{Conclusions}\label{sec:noise:conclusion}

% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
% SOURCE: share of the lost RMSE won back, network on the first hybrid, 25% clipped, fold 0, Table tab:noise:res:censoring_moves_main (10-10b): best of constant lift and censored EM, (clipped - moved) / (clipped - before): QM9 0.47, Caco-2 0.52, hERG Ki 0.54, LogD 0.73, MDR1 0.91. Deletion on QM9: see the comment under sec:noise:res:qm9test (largest gain on a best single or hybrid 0.010, network). Huber blend: n33 QM9 level 0, 40 of 40 rows worse.
We found noisy labels in QSAR data and corrected them in several settings. QM9, the one dataset with computed labels, tests whether a method removes noise or only trains a better model, as described in Section~\ref{sec:noise:res:qm9test}. Deletion and the published methods helped QM9's clean labels by at most about the network's own spread. Where they helped an antibody dataset as delivered, they found noise that was already in the data. Label smoothing, weighting and changes to the model improved QM9 as well. Their gains on measured labels are therefore largely better training. The exceptions are label smoothing on hERG $K_i$ and on hERG blocking. Moving every label toward a Huber-fitted prediction helped hERG $K_i$ under damage and made QM9 worse, so its gains are noise removed.

Corrections worked best when the damaged labels were flagged in the data, as with labels clipped at an assay limit. Raising these labels won back about half to over four fifths of the lost RMSE on the small-molecule datasets, with no detector. With a detector, deleting or relabeling flagged molecules helped under label flips and outliers. Cleanups driven by a detector passed random or shuffled controls under hERG's label flips. They also passed on the labels as delivered of hERG $K_i$, Buchwald--Hartwig and five antibody datasets. On two of these they passed only in their weaker configurations. QM9 shows, though, that the shuffled control alone is weak evidence with the network.

% TODO: check (TH lens): the introduction asks whether a method improves the hybrid more than the best single representation; answer it here, e.g. 'Cleaning rarely changed which of the two won, and more often moved the win to the hybrid.'
The hybrid stayed ahead of the single representation on small molecules in almost every condition, before and after treatment. Finding a detector's neighbors in the hybrid, rather than in the model's own single representation, helped slightly more often, with large differences in both directions. Which noise-handling method works best thus depends on the feature space it runs in.

% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
\label{sec:noise:limitations}
% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
% SOURCE [N7-49]: anchor for tests/analysis/ch7_numbers.py
Most results rest on the first outer fold, and the further folds that exist mostly agree with it. Cleaning before the search gave large gains. However, on QM9 with no noise added only the fit was cleaned, never the search, so those gains cannot be split into noise removed and a better model. Chapter~\ref{chap:discussion} uses these results to answer the research questions of Chapter~\ref{chap:intro}, and to set out how to choose a noise-handling method for a new dataset.

\chapter{Conclusions and Further Thoughts}\label{chap:discussion}

% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
This chapter returns to the research questions of Chapter~\ref{chap:intro} on label noise in molecular data. It then discusses how to choose a noise-handling method and lists the contributions of this thesis.

\section{Summary of findings}\label{sec:discussion:summary}\label{sec:discussion:antibodies}

% [TH lens edit 2026-10-10: the number from Chapter robustness]
% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
We answered the four questions of the first half in Chapter~\ref{chap:robustness}. First, the model family decided noise robustness more than the representation did. On QM9 under Gaussian noise, model family explained 47.2\% of the variance in \aucnorm{}, and the representation 8.1\%. The representation mattered more for predictive performance, as expected, since label noise leaves the features untouched. Second, random forests were the most consistent model under noise. LightGBM and XGBoost predicted well on clean labels but lost the most as noise rose, so a clean score says little about robustness. Third, the type of noise mattered more than the shape of its distribution. Systematic noise did the most damage, namely noise shared within a scaffold family or labels clipped at an assay limit. Fourth, mean predicted uncertainty rose with noise, but no model's uncertainty identified the corrupted labels, except under censoring. There, dividing a molecule's error by its uncertainty picked out the clipped labels better than the error alone.

% [TH lens edit 2026-10-10: his wording 'negatively impacted'; the added-distance clause rewritten; 20 configurations from the Chapter flaws text]
% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
In Chapter~\ref{chap:flaws}, we asked why published methods for learning with noisy labels did poorly in an earlier exploratory study. In its QM9 sweep, all 20 configurations with the lowest retention of $R^2$ under noise used a pairwise distance between molecules. Some methods were not designed with a distance, and the sweep added one. We then looked at the features the distance is computed over. Within one dataset, most features of a standard representation barely vary. Every feature that varies adds a term to the distance. So when only a few features matter, the others can dominate it. Any method that relies on such a distance can be negatively impacted by this. Several representations also give different molecules, mostly stereoisomers, the same feature vector. The adaptive hybrid representation was built to address both problems.

% [alignment 2026-10-10: antibody ensemble result added, as in Ch5 conclusions]
% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
% SOURCE: /Volumes/seagate/kirby_results_2026-10-09/complexity/c02_complexity_matching_grid/<ds>.json (RERUN-SWAP-DNN)
The second half asked whether the representation can be adapted to the task. First, Chapter~\ref{chap:method} showed that the best set of features depends on the dataset and the downstream model, and that a search can find it. The adaptive hybrid representation beat the best single representation on every small-molecule dataset with both downstream models. It carried over to antibodies, although there the gain depended on the dataset. On most antibody datasets, an ensemble of one model per representation did better. On two antibody datasets, the best single representation was one built for this thesis, which reads only the regions or mutations that vary. On the Buchwald--Hartwig reactions, the hybrid gained nothing beyond the spread across folds. Second, Chapter~\ref{chap:similarity} showed that distance in a hybrid tracked the label better than distance in a single representation, on all six small-molecule datasets and on antibodies and reactions. Predicting each label from its five nearest neighbors was also more accurate on small molecules, but worse on four antibody datasets and on Buchwald--Hartwig.

% [alignment 2026-10-10: split as in Ch7 conclusions: flips on hERG blocking, labels as delivered on the others]
% [TH lens edit 2026-10-10: adds the clean-label caveat of the noise chapter's conclusions]
% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
Third, Chapter~\ref{chap:noise} showed that noisy labels can be found and corrected. Corrections worked best when the damaged labels were flagged in the data. Correcting labels clipped at an assay limit won back about half to over four fifths of the lost RMSE on the small-molecule datasets, with no detector. Cleaning guided by a detector beat random deletion and shuffled detector scores under label flips on hERG blocking. It also beat them on the labels as delivered of hERG $K_i$, Buchwald--Hartwig and five antibody datasets. Not every gain was noise removed, though. Label smoothing, weighting and changes to the model also improved QM9, whose labels are computed and carry no assay error. Their gains on measured labels are therefore largely better training. The exceptions were label smoothing on hERG $K_i$ and on hERG blocking. On small molecules, the hybrid stayed ahead of the single representation in almost every noise condition, though not always with the network on hERG $K_i$.

% [TH lens edit 2026-10-10: names where the training result is shown (Chapter method, using importance during training)]
% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
Fourth, feature importance helped in building the hybrid, but reusing the importance scores during training added little on small molecules, according to Table~\ref{tab:method:importance_training}. The hybrid ranks features by importance within each representation. At the same number of features, it beat recursive feature elimination and random features on most datasets. Chapter~\ref{chap:similarity} showed that weighting a single representation's features by importance also brought its distance part of the way toward the hybrid's. In Chapter~\ref{chap:noise}, for detectors that compare each label to its neighbors, the best feature space depended on the dataset.

\section{Choosing a noise-handling method}\label{sec:discussion:noise}\label{sec:discussion:conditional}\label{sec:discussion:simple}

% [AI text, revised 2026-10-10]
No single method handled label noise on every dataset. One detector flags the labels that a random forest predicts worst when they are held out. Under clipping, it fell below chance on Buchwald--Hartwig, LogD and QM9, where methods that used the known clipping flag did best. Subtracting an offset per scaffold family made the model worse on Caco-2's labels as delivered. Deleting the molecules the network fits worst helped on the reactions, but almost never on small molecules. Even the ranking of methods changed between the hybrid and the single representations, so a method chosen on one may not be best on another.

We recommend modeling the noise in a dataset before choosing a method. Data pooled from several laboratories can carry an offset per source, and an assay with a reporting limit gives clipped labels. In both cases the data carry a flag that a correction can use. A method built for a kind of noise that the data do not carry can do harm, as subtracting family offsets did on Caco-2's labels as delivered. Simple methods were often as good as complex ones. On hERG $K_i$, raising every clipped label by one shared amount beat censored EM in most configurations.

\section{Limitations}\label{sec:discussion:limitations}

We were limited first by the data available. QM9 is the only dataset whose labels carry no measurement error, and as in Chapter~\ref{chap:robustness} we used only one of its properties, the HOMO--LUMO gap. The assay datasets are smaller, and their labels already carry an unknown amount of noise, so injected noise sits on top of it. Most antibody datasets hold only a few hundred antibodies, so their results say as much about size as about antibodies, as discussed in Section~\ref{sec:method:limitations}. Only one dataset holds reactions. The separate sites, described in Section~\ref{sec:method:sites}, were simulated from two public datasets, and in the main experiment every site held the same molecules. As Section~\ref{sec:method:datasets} describes, the LogD and Caco-2 labels are clipped at their 1st and 99th percentiles, so on Caco-2, 22 molecules share the highest label.

Our evaluation was limited by time and compute. Not every experiment ran on every fold or every dataset, and most noise experiments ran on the first outer fold only, as Section~\ref{sec:method:evaluation} explains. Where a method has several settings, Chapter~\ref{chap:noise} reports its best on the held-out score, so every gain is optimistic. Each dataset's grid came from an earlier search of the whole dataset, which included the samples that the outer folds later hold out. Section~\ref{sec:method:results_why} showed that on a few hundred samples, the search's own scores overstate the hybrid's results, and its conformal intervals cover too few held-out antibodies. The network's score also changes between fits. On QM9, one hybrid scored between 0.348 and 0.364 RMSE on the same fold and labels. Several design choices in Section~\ref{sec:method:searchspace} rest on preliminary runs, such as scoring features within each representation rather than in one pool.

Some comparisons are weaker than others. When the nearest-neighbor setting is picked again for each fold, the hybrid has five or six times as many combinations to choose from. Only the hybrid can also use importance weighting, described in Section~\ref{sec:similarity:methods}. The test of a new embedding in Section~\ref{sec:method:results_why} swapped random features into the same places of one hybrid, with no new search.

As Section~\ref{sec:method:limitations} notes, the search is costly, and must be rerun whenever the data, representations or model change. To save compute, each dataset's search space was hand-selected from the full search, as Section~\ref{sec:method:decided_grid} describes, so it may find a less informative hybrid. QM9 and hERG blocking were searched over a shorter fixed list of settings. Adaptive pruning, described in Section~\ref{sec:method:pruning}, exists but was not used. The distance search of Section~\ref{sec:similarity:spaces} capped its budgets at 500 features. As noted in Section~\ref{sec:method:results_single}, we have not tested whether the best settings follow measurable properties of a dataset.

Our noise experiments cover only some kinds of noise. Most are on regression data. A noise level is a fraction of the spread of the clean training labels, so it cannot be mapped onto a measurement error. The robustness score \aucnorm{} of Chapter~\ref{chap:robustness} is a ratio to clean $R^2$, so a model with a low clean score has less to lose. There, the heavy-tailed, outlier and censored conditions ran on only some pairings of model and representation, set out in Section~\ref{sec:robustness:methods}. As Section~\ref{sec:noise:detection} explains, a detector can be graded only where some labels were damaged and others not, which excludes Gaussian and grouped-shifted noise. The exploratory sweep of Chapter~\ref{chap:flaws} added a distance to some methods designed without one, so poor adaptation may explain part of its result.

The controls for noise handling are also limited. QM9 with no noise added is the only clean control, and it rests on one computed property. A few methods have no QM9 result, and some have no control at all, as noted in Section~\ref{sec:noise:tests}. With no ground truth for the labels as delivered, a control can show that a gain beats chance, but not that a label was corrected. Section~\ref{sec:noise:limitations} notes that on QM9, cleaning before the search cleaned only the fit, so those gains cannot be split into noise removed and a better model. We did not study in depth why the methods that worked do so, or how each interacts with each kind of noise.

Finally, the representations and models were fixed in advance. As Section~\ref{sec:flaws:ties} showed, ECFP4 was built with stereochemistry off, and PDV and Mordred ignore it, so these merge stereoisomers. As Section~\ref{sec:method:searchspace} describes, the single representations of each dataset were chosen before the search, by their scores and the kind of information they carry. A sparse feature, such as one position of a one-hot sequence, scores low importance on its own. Model hyperparameters were kept the same across datasets. Tuning was tested only on one outer fold of five datasets, as Section~\ref{sec:method:models} describes.

\section{Future work}\label{sec:discussion:future}

The most immediate and obvious next step is to finish what time did not allow. Because of time and compute constraints, not every experiment ran on every fold or every dataset, and the majority of the results in Chapter~\ref{chap:noise} come from the first outer fold only. While we do observe consistent gains in the same directions and can draw preliminary conclusions from that, running the remaining folds would show which of these results carry signficance. The searches themselves could also be more expansive. Each dataset searched a grid cut down from an earlier search, and QM9 and hERG blocking were limited to the fast tier. A full search on every dataset would show whether those cuts cost any accuracy. The upside to this setup is that the results in this thesis if anything found worse-performing hybrids than what we could have found had a full search been run. 

The noise-handling methods that worked deserve a closer look. Correcting labels clipped at an assay limit, label smoothing on the two hERG datasets, and deletion on SMAC colloidal stability and expression titer all passed their controls and showed promise on the tested datasets. We did not have the time at the end of this work to study these methods in as much depth as we would have liked, in particular why they work and how each one interacts with the different types of noise. A systematic comparison of each method under each noise type, with clean controls throughout, is the next step. QM9 offers twelve computed properties, and we used only the HOMO--LUMO gap, so it can supply more of those controls. Cliff put-back should also be tested with cliffs defined in other representations, including the hybrid itself, since the cliffs found depend on the representation.

Classification also needs more work. Most of our noise experiments cover regression tasks. In QSAR, a class label usually comes from cutting a continuous measurement at a threshold, so measurement error mostly flips molecules near that threshold. The detectors of noisy labels should next be tested on flips of that kind, on more classification datasets. We have the code in place, however no in-depth analysis was conducted. 

The search could also be sped up. Summed over five folds, it took up to 28.2 hours on a small-molecule dataset, and on the largest datasets the full grid was out of reach. This is counterintuitive, considering the hybrids perforomed well on larger datasetes. Adaptive pruning already exists in the framework but was not used for the results here due to time constraints. The most logical next step would be to further calibrating adaptive pruning on more datasets. Another option is reusing the importance scores and fitted models shared between configurations.

Finally, the hybrid search needs only a supervised task and several feature matrices for the same samples. It can be applied to any domain, from gene-expression profiles to financial time series, since each has many competing representations. A hybrid could also be the input to the model that scores molecules for a generative model, and a deployment across real sites would test the results of Chapter~\ref{chap:method} on data held at separate sites.

\section{Concluding remarks}\label{sec:discussion:conclusions}\label{sec:discussion:claim}

Label noise in molecular data poses some complex problems. But when it comes to selecting methods of handling that, whether it be choosing a model that is inherently robust to noise, conducting specific noise-robust training methods, or attempting to mitigate the label noise itself, it is important to remember that there is no one-size-fits-all solution. The ``right'' method depends on the task, the data, the model, and the type of noise.

Knowing what type of noise is in your data ahead of time, through statistical tests or through knowledge of the source of the data, can help guide you towards a solution. And you can successfully train with noise. Most studies just assume label noise is Gaussian, but that's not a sure bet given how experimental assay data is collected. And non-Gaussian noise, specifically systematic noise has the potential to do far more damage. It is also the easiest to correct. 

One way around the conditional nature of the methods in this thesis is adaptivity, specifically within the feature set. Most features within a standard molecular representation barely vary on a given dataset and have little relation to the actual prediction task. Yet, each one still contributes to the pairwise distance measure between molecules. And any method that uses such distance metrics, such as neighbor-based comparison methods, inherits this flaw. The adaptive hybrid representation selects features that are relevant to the dataset and model given. On small molecules, it predicted better than every single representation tested, its distances tracked label differences more closely, and it outeperformed standard representations across almost every noise condition.

When working with noisy molecular data, we recommend that the first step, which is often overlooked, should be to consider what kind of noise the labels carry. Then, be prepared to adapt to the problem, considering the differences brought on by the data and the models chosen. Label noise, which is pervasive in cheminformatics data, is not something to be ignored, but rather something to be understood. And hopefully, with the right features, something we can learn from.
